## Question 25 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q25-civilsdaily**

**To what extent, in your view, the Parliament is able to ensure accountability of the executive in India? [Year: 2021] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Parliament of India** is the **supreme instrument of democratic accountability.** Under **Article 75(3)** of the Constitution, the **Council of**

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p25_img61.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Ministers is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha**

**Parliament ensuring accountability of the executive**

**1. Question Hour** - Enables MPs to **question ministers** and expose administrative lapses

**2. Zero Hour** - Allows MPs to raise **urgent matters** of public importance without prior notice.

**3. Adjournment and Censure Motions** - Hold the executive accountable on specific issues of **public or national concern.**

**4. No-Confidence Motion** - Tests the **majority support** of the government and can lead to its fall if lost.

**5. Budgetary Control (Articles 112-114)** - **power of the purse** by approving or rejecting demands for grants.

**6. Cut Motions** to **reduce or disapprove government expenditure,** signalling disapproval of policy.

**7. Parliamentary Committees**

- **Public Accounts Committee (PAC)** - Examines CAG reports and ensures post-audit accountability (e.g., 2G and coal scam scrutiny).

- **Estimates Committee** - Evaluates government expenditure efficiency.

- **DRSCs) in-depth scrutiny of ministries.**

- **Joint Parliamentary Committees (JPCs)** - Eg- Waqf Amendment Bill

**8. Parliamentary Privileges** - Allow MPs to **speak freely and fearlessly,** enhancing deliberative accountability.

**Issues with parliamentary form of government**

1. **Reduced Scrutiny by Parliamentary Committees-** While 60% of the Bills in 14th LS and 71% in 15th LS were referred to DRSCs concerned, this proportion came down to 25% in 16th LS and 17% in 17th LS.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p25_img62.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

2. **Decline in the number of sittings-** The 17th Lok Sabha sat for only 55 days

3. **Frequent Disruptions and Adjournments** due to protests and unruly behavior, leading to wasted time and resources.

4. **Low Productivity-** Eg- the 2023 winter session saw significant disruptions, resulting in the suspension of over 140 MPs and hindering legislative business.

5. **Resort to Money Bill route-** Eg- Aadhar Bill

6. **Inadequate Discussion-** 58% of the Bills were passed within two weeks of their introduction in 17th LS. Eg- J&K Reorganisation Bill, 2019, and the Women’s Reservation Bill, 2023 were passed within two days

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p25_img63.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

7. About 31% of the total functioning time in Lok Sabha, and 32% in Rajya Sabha was spent on **discussions other than legislation and budgets.** Eg- President’s Address to Parliament, matters of public importance, and trust votes.

8. Between **2019 and 2023,** about **80% of the Budget was passed without discussion,** and in **2023,** the **entire Budget was approved without debate.**

9. **Rise Legislation through Ordinances** bypassing the usual legislative process.

Along with **‘maximum governance,** there needs to be **‘maximum accountability’,** which must start with **an empowered and effective Parliament.**

[Question ID: 2021-gs2-q25-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 26 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q26-civilsdaily**

**Why do you think the committees are considered to be useful for parliamentary work?**

**Discuss, in this context, the role of the Estimates Committee. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**According to LS speaker Om Birla, Parliamentary Committees are the "backbone of parliamentary**

**democracy.**

**Parliamentary committees**

1. **Standing Committees** - PAC, Estimate committee, Committee on public undertaking

2. **Ad-Hoc Committees** - Eg- JPC **Importance of Parliamentary Committees**

1. **Checks and Balances** - Eg- **PAC** examined **CAG’s 2G spectrum report** for irregularities.

2. **In-Depth Policy Analysis** - Enable **clause-by-clause scrutiny** of Bills, which is not possible in open House debates. Eg- **JPC** on **Data Protection Bill (2019)** proposed over **90 amendments**

3. **Reducing Legislative Workload** - Committees share Parliament’s burden, ensuring **thorough scrutiny** without delaying legislation.

4. **Expert Consultation** - Committees invite **experts, industry, and civil society** to promote **evidence-based policymaking.**

5. **Budgetary Oversight** - **Departmentally Related Standing Committees (DRSCs)** analyse **ministry budgets** to ensure fiscal prudence.

6. **Consensus Building** - Committees promote **bipartisan dialogue,** e.g., **IT Committee** built consensus on the **Personal Data Protection Bill (2019).**

7. **Public Engagement** - Committees seek **public and expert inputs,** e.g., **Standing Committee on Environment** reviewed the **EIA Draft Notification (2020).**

8. **Continuity in Oversight** - Committees function **year-round,** ensuring continuous policy review beyond parliamentary sessions.

9. **Bridging Knowledge Gaps** - Committees **enhance MPs’ expertise** through research support and technical briefings.

**Role of the Estimates Committee**

1. Established under **Rule 310 of the Lok Sabha,** it has **30 Lok Sabha members** and is chaired by a **ruling party MP.**

2. Examines **budget estimates** to ensure **efficiency, economy, and effectiveness.**

3. Suggests **alternative policies** for better fiscal management and administrative reform.

4. Monitors **implementation of government schemes** and performance of ministries.

5. Eg- Under **Murli Manohar Joshi (2018),** it reviewed **PSU disinvestment and defence procurement,** highlighting fiscal prudence.

| Issues | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| Decline in bills referred. 15th<br>LS(71%), 16th (28%), 17th (16%). | Mandatory referral of Bills (UK system) |
| Lack of research support | Create professional research & data wing |
| Partisan approach, low attendance | Enforce code of conduct and attendance norms |

In the words of **Woodrow Wilson,** “Congress in session is Congress on public exhibition, while Congress in its committee rooms is Congress at work.”

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q26-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 27 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q27-civilsdaily**

**Do Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committees keep the administration on its toes and inspire reverence for parliamentary control? Evaluate the working of such**

**committees with suitable examples. [Year: 2021] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**According to LS speaker Om Birla, Parliamentary Committees are the "backbone of parliamentary**

**democracy. The DRSCs, introduced in 1993, are vital to ensure continuous and informed Parliamentary**

**control over executive.**

**Composition**

1. **Total 24 DRSCs- 16** under **LS** and **8** under **RS.**

2. **31 Members** - **21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha**

3. **Chairperson-** Appointed by the **Speaker of the Lok Sabha**

4. **Tenure- nominated annually Role and Contribution of DRSCs**

1. **Detailed Legislative Scrutiny** - examine **Bills clause by clause,** preventing hasty or poorly drafted laws. Eg The **IT Committee (2019)** proposed key revisions to the **Personal Data Protection Bill.**

2. **Financial Oversight** - scrutinise **Demands for Grants** and expenditure trends of ministries, ensuring **fiscal prudence.**

3. **Policy Review and Evaluation** - Assess the **performance of ministries and flagship schemes,** suggesting reforms for better outcomes.

4. **Expert Consultation** - Invite **domain experts, civil society, and industry** to facilitate **evidence-based policymaking.**

5. **Non-Partisan Deliberation** - Function as **bipartisan forums** that foster cooperation beyond party lines.

6. **Continuous Oversight** - Operate **throughout the year,** ensuring sustained monitoring even when Parliament is not in session.

7. **Transparency and Accountability** - Their reports promote **executive accountability** and strengthen **parliamentary control over administration.**

8. **Knowledge Enhancement** - Equip MPs with **technical and subject expertise,** improving **legislative quality** and policy insight.

**Issues-**

1. **Transparency Concerns-** Meetings held behind closed doors, with no published minutes.

2. **Non-binding Recommendations** allow bypassing of detailed bill scrutiny.

3. **Limited Research Support** limits committee's ability to conduct thorough analysis and research.

4. **Selective Referral of Bills-** During the term of **17th Lok Sabha, 19% of reports by DRSCs** were on subjects other than Bills and budgets.

5. **One-year tenure** provides **limited time for members to specialize** in specific areas, impacting scrutiny depth.

6. **Weak attendance** of MPs and handling multiple ministries burdens the committee, hindering effective scrutiny and functioning.

**Way Forward-**

1. **The National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution (2002)** recommended providing **dedicated research support to committees.**

2. Establish a **dedicated research cell** for each DRSC, similar to the **Congressional Research Service (CRS) in the US.**

3. **Mandatory Response from Government** within a fixed timeline (e.g., 3 months) to DRSC recommendations.

4. **Ensure Transparency-** A **“Committee Action Report”** should be tabled in Parliament detailing the status of recommendations.

The DRSCs remain **crucial instruments of accountability,** ensuring **executive discipline and informed lawmaking.**

[Question ID: 2021-gs2-q27-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 28 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q28-civilsdaily**

**Explain the structure of the Parliamentary Committee system. How far have the financial committees helped in the institutionalization of Indian Parliament [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

? 15** According to LS speaker Om Birla, **Parliamentary Committees** are the **"backbone of parliamentary democracy.** It is an essential mechanism of **legislative oversight,** promoting **informed, bipartisan, and continuous accountability.**

**Structure of the Parliamentary Committee System**

**(a) Standing Committees (Permanent in Nature)-**

1. **Department-related Standing Committees (DRSCs)** - 24 in number; examine Bills, budgets, and policies of ministries.

2. **Financial Committees** - **Public Accounts Committee (PAC), Estimates Committee,** and **Committee on Public Undertakings (COPU).**

3. **Housekeeping Committees** - e.g., **Business Advisory Committee, Committee on Subordinate Legislation,** etc.

**(b) Ad hoc Committees (Temporary in Nature)-** Constituted for specific purposes - e.g., **Joint Parliamentary Committee (JPC)** on the **2G Spectrum Scam** (2011) and **Waqf Amendment Bill.**

**Importance of Parliamentary Committees**

1. **Ensuring Fiscal Accountability** - Committees like **PAC** scrutinize CAG reports, ensuring that **public funds are used efficiently and lawfully.**

2. **Continuous Executive Oversight** - They enable **ongoing parliamentary supervision** over expenditure, even when Parliament is not in session.

3. **Strengthening Audit Mechanism** - The **PAC’s linkage with CAG** institutionalizes the audit process, reinforcing transparency in governance.

4. **Promoting Rational Budgeting** - The **Estimates Committee** evaluates expenditure patterns and recommends **economy and efficiency** in public spending.

5. **Enhancing PSU Accountability** - The **Committee on Public Undertakings (COPU)** monitors performance and financial health of PSUs, promoting **corporate discipline.**

6. **Institutional Continuity** - These committees function annually, ensuring **regular scrutiny beyond political cycles.**

7. **Evidence-Based Decision Making** - Reports are based on expert testimony and data, improving **quality of legislative oversight.**

8. **Non-Partisan Deliberation** - Work in committees is largely **bipartisan,** reducing political confrontation seen in the House.

9. **Public Transparency** - Publication of reports increases **citizen awareness** and reinforces Parliament’s role as a **watchdog of the executive.**

10. **Institutional Memory Building** - The accumulated reports and recommendations create a **repository of fiscal best practices** for future governance.

**Challenges-**

1. **Non-binding recommendations** and **lack of discussion on parliamentary reports**

2. **Low attendance of members-** In 17th, average attendance for subject committee meetings was only 47%.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p28_img64.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

3. **Fewer bills referred-** In the 15th Lok Sabha -71%, 16th Lok Sabha -27% and in 17th Lok Sabha -16% of the bills introduced were referred to the committees.

4. **Lack of specialised knowledge** among members in financial and economic matters leads to **superficial scrutiny.**

5. **Bulky CAG and budgetary reports** result in **inadequate time** for detailed examination.

6. **Post mortem analysis-** This lowers the quality of oversight and recommendations.

7. **Political influence** often causes **biased decision-making** and undermines objectivity.

8. **Lack of Public Awareness and Transparency-** Committee proceedings are held behind closed doors.

**Way Forward**

1. Implement **ARC & NCRWC** suggestions for **expert support units** and **public disclosure** of reports.

2. Enhance **capacity-building of MPs** on financial management.

3. Make **committee recommendations time-bound and mandatory** for government response.

In the words of **Woodrow Wilson,** “Congress in session is Congress on public exhibition, while Congress in its committee rooms is Congress at work.”

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q28-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 29 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q29-civilsdaily**

**“Parliament’s power to amend the Constitution is a limited power and it cannot be enlarged into absolute power.” In the light of this statement explain whether Parliament**

**under Article 368 of the Constitution can destroy the Basic Structure of the Constitution by**

**expanding its amending power? [Year: 2019] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Article 368** empowers Parliament to **amend the Constitution,** but this power is **not absolute.** The SC, through the

**Basic Structure Doctrine,** has clarified that while Parliament can amend any part of the Constitution, it **cannot alter**

**or destroy its essential features.**

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p29_img65.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Limitations on the Amending Power of Parliament**

1. **Procedure under Article 368** - Constitutional amendments must strictly follow **special majority** and, where applicable, **state ratification** (for federal provisions).

2. **Basic Structure Doctrine (Kesavananda Bharati, 1973)** - Parliament cannot alter, abrogate, or destroy the **Basic Structure** of the Constitution.

3. **Minerva Mills, 1980** - The **limited amending power itself** is part of the Basic Structure.

4. **Balance Between Fundamental Rights and DPSPs** - Parliament cannot give **unlimited precedence** to one over the other, preserving constitutional harmony.

5. **Separation of Powers** - Parliament cannot encroach upon the **independence of the judiciary** or the **executive-legislative balance.** Eg- NJAC

6. **Federal Structure** - Amendment cannot centralize power to the extent of **destroying federalism** or **state autonomy.** (S.R. Bommai Judgment)

7. **I.R. Coelho v. State of Tamil Nadu (2007)** - Even laws under the **Ninth Schedule** are open to judicial review if they **violate Basic Structure.**

Thus, parliament **cannot destroy or expand** its amending power beyond constitutional limits. It is subject to Basic Structure

1. **Supremacy of the Constitution**

2. **Republican and democratic form of government**

3. **Secularism**

4. **Federalism**

5. **Judicial Review and Rule of Law**

6. **Separation of Powers**

7. **Limited amending power (Minerva Mills) Analysis**

| Justification | Criticism |
| --- | --- |
| Basic Structure protection | Judicial overreach |
| Prevents constitutional destruction | Undermines parliamentary sovereignty |
| Maintains constitutional balance | Vague and subjective doctrine |
| Ensures rule of law & judicial review | Creates judicial supremacy |
| Safeguards democracy & federalism | Limits democratic will of Parliament |
| Preserves Fundamental Rights | Restricts socio-economic reforms |

The power of amendment under **Article 368** is an instrument to ensure the **Constitution’s continuity, and dynamic adaptation,** not **institutional annihilation.** In a democracy governed by the **Rule of Law,** no organ can arrogate to itself **absolute power,** for that would negate the **very idea of constitutionalism.**

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q29-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 30 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q30-civilsdaily**

**Indian Constitution has conferred the amending power on the ordinary legislative institutions with a few procedural hurdles. In view of this statement, examine the procedural**

**and substantive limitations on the amending power of the Parliament to change the**

**Constitution. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **Indian Constitution,** under **Article 368,** confers the power of amendment on **Parliament.** However, this power is **neither absolute nor unlimited,** being subject to **procedural** and **substantive limitations.**

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p30_img66.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Procedural Limitations**

These are **explicit constitutional conditions** laid down in **Article 368.** Violation of any renders the amendment **invalid.**

**1. Special Majority Requirement - Majority of total membership** of each House, and **Two-thirds of members present and voting.**

**2. Separate Approval by Each House - No joint sitting** is allowed under Article 108.

**3. Ratification by Half States for Federal Provisions -** Eg- **101st Amendment (GST)** required ratification.

**4. Presidential Assent** under Article 368(2).

**5.** Bill must follow all **legislative procedures** - notice, debate, quorum, and voting. Procedural irregularities can invite **judicial scrutiny** (as per **Raj Narain v. Indira Gandhi, 1975).**

**Substantive Limitations on the Amending Power**

These are **judicially evolved constraints** that restrict Parliament from altering the **core identity** of the Constitution.

**1. Doctrine of Basic Structure (Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala (1973))**

**2. Limited Amending Power Itself is Basic Structure (Minerva Mills, 1980)** - Clauses (4) and (5) of Article 368 (inserted by the 42nd Amendment) were struck down.

**3. I.R. Coelho v. State of Tamil Nadu (2007)** held that even laws placed in the **Ninth Schedule** after 1973 are open to **Basic Structure review.**

**4.** Parliament cannot amend to **abrogate or emasculate** Fundamental Rights forming part of the Basic Structure (e.g., equality, liberty).

**5.** Parliament cannot alter the **essential federal balance** between Centre and States. **(Kuldip Nayar v.**

**Union of India (2006))**

**6. Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain (1975)-** Free and fair elections, rule of law, and judicial review are part of Basic Structure.

**7. Harmony between Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles -** Both must coexist; destroying one in favor of the other violates Basic Structure.

**Examination of limitations**

| Justification | Criticism |
| --- | --- |
| Prevents hasty amendments | Slows institutional reform. Eg- NJAC<br>Judgment |
| Upholds federal balance | Creates procedural bottlenecks, obstructs<br>timely reforms |
| Prevents Lok Sabha dominance | Possibility of deadlock between Houses |
| Strengthens constitutional supremacy over<br>parliamentary supremacy | Undermines democratic will of elected<br>representatives |
| Preserves basic structure and rule of law | Judicial Overreach and Violates Separation of<br>Power |
| Protects fundamental rights and core democratic<br>values | Limits legislative flexibility to pursue social<br>justice |
| Promotes constitutional morality and<br>accountability | Reduces parliamentary autonomy and<br>political responsiveness |

Thus, the **limited amending power** under **Article 368** makes the Indian Constitution a **living document,** capable of growth yet immune from destruction.

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q30-civilsdaily]

## Question 31 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q31-civilsdaily**

**Individual Parliamentarian’s role as the national [Year: 2019]**

### Answer

lawmaker is on a decline, which in turn, has adversely impacted the quality of debates and their outcome. Discuss.(15)**

The Parliament of India is the **temple of democracy** and an MP would act as a **national trustee.** However, as per

**Former VP, Hamid Ansari,** Parliament has turned into **‘chamber of anarchy’** due to declining **role and productivity**

of members.

**Functions of MPs**

**1. Legislative Function** - Making, amending, and repealing laws.

**2. Deliberative Function** - Debating national policies and public issues.

**3. Executive Oversight Function** - Question Hour, motions, committees

**4. Financial Function** - Approving the Budget, Demands for Grants, and financial bills.

**5. Representative Function** - Voicing citizens’ concerns and constituency interests.

**Indicators of Decline in Individual Parliamentarian’s Role**

A. Legislative Function

  - **Marginalization of Private Members’ Bills** - Only **14 passed since 1952.**

  - **Decreasing referral of bills to parliamentary committees-** Only 27% in the 16th Lok Sabha and 16% in the 17th Lok Sabha.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p32_img67.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

  - **Lack of thorough scrutiny of bills-** During the term of the 17th LS, 58% of the Bills introduced were passed within two weeks of their introduction. Eg- the J&K Reorganisation Bill, 2019

  - **Weak research and knowledge support** - Inadequate access to legislative data and expert analysis undermines quality of debates.

B. Deliberative Function

  - **Decline in sitting days** - From **120+ days (1950s)** to around **55 days** in 17th LS, curtailing deliberation

  - **Declining attendance-** In 2021, average attendance dropped to 71% in the Lok Sabha and 74% in the Rajya Sabha.

  - **Frequent disruptions and adjournments** - During the 17th Lok Sabha, MPs were suspended on 206 instances, across both Houses of Parliament. In Winter Session 2023, 146 MPs were suspended for serious misconduct in the House.

  - **Party whip system** - Strict control discourages independent viewpoints and policy reasoning.

C. Financial Function

  - Between 2019 and 2023, on average, about 80% of the budget has been voted on without discussion. In 2023, the entire budget was passed without discussion.

  - **Decline in budget scrutiny** - Detailed examination of Demands for Grants often guillotined without debate.

  - **Weak engagement with financial committees** - Falling participation in PAC, Estimates Committee, etc.

D. Executive Oversight Function

**1. Question Hour functioned for 60% of scheduled time in LS and 52% in Rajya Sabha.**

  - **Underutilized parliamentary committees** - Low attendance and poor follow-up dilute scrutiny of executive performance.

  - **Resort to Money Bill route** undermines RS. Eg- Aadhar Act **Consequences of the Decline in Individual Parliamentarian’s Role**

  - **Reduced Accountability** - Rushing bills like the **Women’s Reservation Bill, 2023** limits scrutiny and weakens executive oversight.

  - **Criminalization of politics** - as per ADR data, **46% of MPs elected in 2024** have criminal cases pending.

  - **Poor Legislative Quality** - **Farm Laws (2020)** passed with minimal debate led to backlash and repeal.

  - **Legislative Stagnation** - Delay in passage of important bills due to disruption

  - **Judicial Intervention** - Weak legislative performance prompts courts to step in (e.g., **NGT,** social justice rulings).

  - **Ordinance Culture** - Fewer sittings have led to rising ordinances **(11 in 2020),** bypassing Parliament.

  - **Public Disillusionment** - Citizens lose faith in Parliament’s deliberative and representative role.

  - **Democratic Imbalance** - Executive consolidation weakens **checks and balances,** harming deliberative democracy.

  - **Diminishing role of opposition MPs** - Reduced speaking time and political polarization undermine effective oversight.

**Way Forward-**

1. **Increase Working Days-** Eg- **NCRWC (2002)** recommended Parliament meet for at least 110 days a year; currently, it averages 60-70 days.

2. **Enhancing Parliamentary Decorum-** Encourage a culture of respect, professionalism, and active participation in debates.

3. **Enhance Research Support for MPs-** Eg- **UK Parliament’s Research Service** aids MPs in informed decision-making

4. **Engaging Responsible Opposition-** allowing **opposition to set agenda** on lines of **UK House of Commons**

**5. Mandatory referral of Bills to Committee** As former Vice President **M. Venkaiah Naidu** aptly stated, “Disorder, disruption, and delay should not replace debate, discussion, and decision, which form the edifice of parliamentary democracy.”

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q31-civilsdaily]

## Question 32 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q32-civilsdaily**

**“Once a speaker, Always a speaker’! Do you think the practice should be adopted to impart objectivity to the office of the Speaker of Lok Sabha? What could be its implications**

**for the robust functioning of parliamentary business in India. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**According to** Nehru,'Speaker represents dignity & freedom of house & as House represents nation, Speaker becomes a symbol of nation’s freedom & liberty.’ **Once a speaker, Always a speaker** is a British system aimed to ensure **neutrality** of office of speaker.

**Issues in office of Speaker**

1. Combining **presiding** and **quasi-judicial** roles, causes conflict of interest.

2. **Partisan Bias** - Delays in **Manipur & Maharashtra defection cases (2020-23)** benefited ruling coalitions.

3. **Unilateral Suspension Powers** - **Rule 374A** allows mass suspensions (e.g., **146 MPs in 2023)** without adequate checks.

4. **Money Bill** - **Aadhaar Act (2016)** passed as **Money Bill,** bypassing Rajya Sabha scrutiny.

5. Speaker remains **party-affiliated,** unlike the UK’s non-partisan model.

6. **Judicial Non-Interference (Art. 122)** - Speaker’s discretion largely **beyond judicial review.**

7. **Weak Parliamentary Conventions** - No codified norms; decisions often **politically motivated.**

8. **Decline in Deliberation** - **“Railroading”** of bills (e.g., **Farm Bills, 2020)** reduces discussion quality.

9. **Executarian Speakership** - Rising **executive influence** eroding neutrality and institutional integrity.

**Positive Implications of Adopting “Once a Speaker, Always a Speaker”**

1. **Restored Neutrality** - Permanent detachment from party politics enhances **objectivity and moral authority.**

2. **Improved Parliamentary Discipline** - Equal enforcement of rules encourages **constructive debate over disruption.**

3. **Fair and Equal Treatment** - Ensures equal opportunities for participation, giving every member’s voice due respect, irrespective of party affiliation.

4. **Better Executive Oversight** - Impartial Speaker can ensure **accountable functioning** of government in Parliament.

5. **Building Consensus** - A neutral Speaker fosters an environment conducive to **consensus-building** and cooperative decision-making.

6. **Impartial Decision-Making** - Promotes fairness in-

- **Rulings on points of order,**

- **Interpretation of parliamentary rules,**

- **Decisions on admissibility of bills and motions,** and

- **Protection of members’ rights and privileges.**

7. **Upholding the Dignity of Office** - Acts as a symbol of **neutrality, integrity, and impartiality,** thereby strengthening the moral authority of Parliament.

8. **Global Best Practice Alignment** - Similar to **UK model,** where Speaker contests as “Speaker seeking re-election,” maintaining impartiality.

**Way Forward-**

1. **Codify Speaker’s neutrality** - mandatory resignation from political party upon election.

2. **Amend Rule 374A** - require **House concurrence** for suspensions beyond one day.

3. **Limit Speaker’s Role in Anti-Defection Law-** In the **Keisham Meghachandra Singh case,** the Supreme Court suggested that the current system could be replaced by a **permanent tribunal.**

4. **Introduce legislative due process** - guarantee minimum debate standards. (Inspired by **Rosalind Dixon’s theory of legislative dysfunction** and **Israel’s Quantinsky v. Knesset (2021),** which annulled a rushed tax law for violating **principle of participation.)**

5. Create a **Standing Committee on Parliamentary Procedures** to oversee the Speaker’s administrative decisions, particularly related to the agenda-setting and motion approvals.

As **M. Venkaiah Naidu** observed, debate, discussion, and decision, which form the edifice of parliamentary democracy.” A **truly impartial Speaker** is the cornerstone of that edifice.

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q32-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 33 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q33-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the role of Presiding Officers of state legislatures in maintaining order and impartiality in conducting legislative work and in facilitating best democratic practices [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

. 10 Article 178** provides for Presiding Officers of state legislatures. **According to** Nehru,'Speaker represents dignity & freedom of house & as House represents nation, Speaker becomes a symbol of nation’s freedom & liberty.’

**Role in Maintaining Order**

1. **Presiding Over Sessions** - Conducts proceedings, enforces rules of procedure, and ensures **smooth functioning** of the House.

2. **Maintaining Order and Decorum** - Eg- suspension under Rule 374A equivalent.

3. **Regulating Debates** - Allots time to members, ensures **balanced participation** from treasury and opposition benches.

4. **Decision on Points of Order** - Interprets procedural rules and resolves disputes.

5. **Certification Authority** - Certifies **Money Bills** and authenticates proceedings.

6. Safeguards the **sanctity of legislative decision-making** through accurate voting processes.

**Role in Upholding Impartiality**

1. **Neutral Arbiter** - Acts above party lines, ensuring **equal treatment** of all members.

2. **Adjudicator under Anti-Defection Law (Tenth Schedule)** - Decides on **disqualification of members.**

3. **Protection of Members’ Privileges** - Defends **legislative freedom of speech and expression** within the House.

4. **Guardian of Legislative Autonomy** - Ensures the **executive’s accountability** to the legislature, maintaining separation of powers.

5. **Promoting Transparency** - Ensures **publication of debates, committee reports, and live telecast** of proceedings to strengthen public trust.

**Facilitating Best Democratic Practices**

1. **Encouraging Committee System** - Strengthens **Departmental Standing Committees** for detailed policy review.

2. Enhancing **transparency and accountability - Eg- live telecast of sessions**

3. **Training & Capacity Building of MLAs** - Enhances understanding of legislative procedures and constitutional duties.

4. **Upholding Minority Rights** - Protects the **voice of opposition and smaller parties** within the assembly.

5. **Adherence to Constitutional Morality** - Ensures decisions reflect **constitutional values** rather than political expediency.

6. **Coordination with Lok Sabha & Other States** - Fosters **uniform parliamentary standards** and best practices nationwide.

**Concerns Affecting Impartiality**

1. **Partisan Bias** in disqualification cases (e.g., **Maharashtra, Manipur Assemblies, 2020-23).**

2. **Executive Pressure** in agenda-setting and session duration.

3. **Lack of Codified Conventions** for neutrality and ethical norms.

4. **Absence of Independent Oversight** of the Speaker’s procedural decisions.

A **truly impartial Speaker** is the cornerstone of the edifice of parliamentary democracy.

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q33-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 34 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q34-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the role of the Vice -Presidents of India as the chairman of the Rajyasabha** [Year: 2022] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Vice-President of India,** under **Article 64 of the Constitution,** is the **ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha**

**(Council of States).**

**Role of the Vice-President**

1. **Presiding Authority** - The Vice-President, as **ex-officio Chairman,** conducts proceedings, maintains **order and decorum,** and ensures smooth legislative functioning.

2. **Regulator of Debates** - Allocates time, permits discussions, and ensures **equal opportunity** to both treasury and opposition benches.

3. **Interpreter of Rules** - Decides **points of order** and interprets parliamentary rules to maintain procedural consistency.

4. **Casting Vote** - Does not ordinarily vote but exercises a **casting vote in case of a tie,** ensuring impartial decision-making.

5. **Promotes Deliberation** - Facilitates **meaningful debates and consensus-building,** enabling Rajya Sabha to act as a **House of Review.**

6. **Upholds Federal Balance** - Protects **state interests** and ensures fair representation of diverse regional perspectives.

7. **Encourages Transparency** - Ensures proceedings are **open and accessible** (e.g., **live telecast of sessions)** to enhance accountability.

8. **Maintains Impartiality** - Expected to act **above party lines,** setting high ethical standards in conduct.

**Limitations of the Vice-President Compared to the Speaker of Lok Sabha**

1. **Does not preside over joint sittings** of Parliament (Article 118).

2. The Vice-President **cannot suspend or expel MPs** for disorderly conduct, unlike the Speaker who can act under **Rules 373 and 374A.**

3. **No Role in Money Bills** under **Article 110.**

4. **No Power Over Confidence Motions** The **Vice-President as Chairman** ensures that the Rajya Sabha functions as the **House of sober second thought,** balancing **federal interests and national priorities.**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q34-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 35 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q35-civilsdaily**

**Rajya Sabha has been transformed from a ‘useless stepney tyre’ to the most useful supporting organ in past few decades. Highlight the factors as well as the areas in which this**

**transformation could be visible. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **Rajya Sabha,** conceptualised under the **Montague-Chelmsford Reforms (1918)** and institutionalised by the **Government of India Act, 1919,** was envisioned as the **Council of States-a** forum of mature reflection.

**Rajya Sabha as a “Useless Stepney Tyre”**

1. **Limited Legislative Powers** - cannot initiate or amend **Money Bills (Article 110)**

2. **No Role in Government Formation** - Council of Ministers is **collectively responsible only to the Lok Sabha**

3. **Joint sittings (Article 118)** are dominated by Lok Sabha’s numerical majority.

4. Viewed as a **delaying or obstructive chamber** rather than a constructive revising body.

5. Cannot influence **budgetary priorities or grants,** diminishing its fiscal relevance.

**Factors Behind Transformation into a Useful Supporting Organ**

1. **Rise of Coalition Politics** - Need for consensus enhanced Rajya Sabha’s negotiating role.

2. **Federal Assertion** - The **rise of regional parties** since the 1980s has **strengthened federal politics**

3. **Diversified Composition** - Eminent professionals, academicians, and experts improved deliberation quality.

4. **Revitalized Committee System** - Department-related committees (many chaired by RS members) improved policy scrutiny.

5. **Continuity and Stability** - Not subject to dissolution, ensuring **legislative stability** and institutional memory.

6. **Judicial and Media Attention** - **Live telecast of sessions** and judicial oversight improved transparency and accountability.

7. **Active Opposition Role** - RS became a **check on executive dominance** during coalition and one-party phases alike.

**Areas Where Transformation is Visible**

1. **Legislative Review and Refinement** - Rajya Sabha acts as a **revising chamber. Eg-** Referred the **GST Constitutional Amendment Bill (2014)** to a Select Committee

2. **Federal Balance** - Protects **state interests** against central overreach. **Eg-** discussed **federal fiscal implications** of the **National Education Policy, 2020.**

3. **Special Constitutional Powers** - Can authorise **creation of All-India Services (Article 312),** make laws on **State List (Article 249)**

4. **Deliberative Forum** - Provides **in-depth, issue-based policy debates** on national concerns. **Eg-** debates on **COVID-19 management (2021)**

5. **Accountability and Oversight** - Through **Question Hour, Zero Hour, and Calling Attention Motions. Eg-** Rajya Sabha discussions led to clarifications on **Pegasus spyware issue (2021)** and **farmers’ protests (2020-21).**

6. **Voice to smaller and regional parties** to represent **state and sectional interests. Eg- state-specific issues** such as river water disputes and fiscal devolution.

7. Being a **permanent chamber,** it ensures **institutional memory** and continuity during Lok Sabha dissolutions.

**Way Forward-**

1. **Equal representation** for each state, so that large states do not dominate the proceedings. **(US system)**

2. **More debates, less disruptions-** Eg - Introduce **‘whip-free debates’** for non-financial and non-confidence issues.

3. Amend procedures so that laws disguised as **Money Bills** can be **challenged and reviewed.**

4. **NCRWC, 2002** - Called for a **clear procedure for nominating eminent members** under Article 80.

5. **Punchhi Commission (2010)- expanding the electoral college** for Rajya Sabha to include **panchayats and municipalities.**

As envisaged by **Dr. B.R. Ambedkar,** RS must act as a **“safety valve of federalism”,** ensuring **reasoned, representative, and regionally inclusive governance** in India’s democracy.

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q35-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 36 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q36-civilsdaily**

**Explain the constitutional provisions under which Legislative Councils are established.**

**Review the working and current status of Legislative Councils with suitable illustrations. [Year: 2021] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Legislative Councils (Vidhan Parishads)** are the **upper chambers** in certain states, designed to act as a **revisory**

**and deliberative body.**

**Constitutional Provisions**

1. **Article 168** - Provides for a **bicameral legislature** (Governor, Assembly, and Council) in few states.

2. **Article 169** - **Parliament** may create or abolish a Council by law if the **State Assembly passes a resolution** by **special majority.**

3. **Article 171** - Defines **composition-**

  - 1/3 elected by MLAs,

  - 1/3 by local bodies,

  - 1/12 by teachers,

  - 1/12 by graduates,

  - 1/6 nominated by Governor (eminent persons).

4. **Article 172(2)** - Council is a **permanent body,** one-third retiring every two years.

5. **Article 182-184** - Deal with **Chairman, Deputy Chairman, and conduct of business.**

**Current Status**

- **Six states** currently have Legislative Councils- **UP, Bihar, Maharashtra, Karnataka, Telangana, Andhra Pradesh.**

- Previously existed in **Tamil Nadu, West Bengal, Punjab, J&K** but later abolished.

**Working and Role**

1. **Deliberative Function-** Reviews legislation. Eg- **Andhra Pradesh Council** played a pivotal role in the Land Titling Bill (2023).

2. **Representative Function-** Includes teachers, graduates, and professionals - ensuring **diversity in law-making.**

3. **Check on Hasty Legislation-** Provides a **“cooling chamber”** function to prevent rushed or populist law-making by the Assembly.

4. **Continuity-** Being a **permanent house,** ensures governance continuity even when the Assembly dissolves.

5. Serves as a forum for **experienced legislators and experts** to contribute to policymaking, even if not elected directly.

**Challenges**

1. **Limited Powers-** Can **delay** ordinary bills for 4 months and **money bills** for 14 days, but cannot veto.

2. **Political Patronage-** Used to **accommodate defeated leaders.**

3. **Low Productivity-** Weak participation and debate quality in several states.

4. **Financial Burden-** Maintenance cost often exceeds functional utility.

5. **Unequal Representation-** Teacher and graduate constituencies have **low turnout** and poor inclusivity.

**Way Forward**

1. **Reform Composition-** Include **women, professionals, civil society** members.

2. **Define Role Clearly-** Give Councils specific **review and policy oversight functions.**

3. **Regular Evaluation-** Periodic **performance audits** to justify continuance.

4. **Curb Political Misuse-** Transparent nomination and election process.

Legislative Councils reflect India’s **deliberative democratic ethos.** With **reforms,** they can evolve from **political parking spaces** into **effective revisory institutions** upholding **constitutionalism.**

**Judiciary**

## Question 37 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q37-civilsdaily**

**“Constitutionally guaranteed judicial independence is a prerequisite of democracy”.**

**Comment [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

. 10 Judicial independence** refers to the freedom of judges from external pressures - political, or popular - enabling them to uphold the **Constitution and rule of law** without fear or favour.

**Constitutional Guarantee of Judicial Independence**

1. **Separation of Powers (Article 50)** - Directive to separate judiciary from the executive.

2. **Security of Tenure (Articles 124(4), 217)** - Judges can be removed only through **impeachment.**

3. **Financial Autonomy (Article 112, 125)** - Judicial salaries and pensions are **charged on the Consolidated Fund.**

4. **Appointment Process (Articles 124-147)** - The **Collegium system,** evolved through Second Judges Case (1993), protects against executive dominance.

5. **Power of Judicial Review (Articles 13, 32, 226)** - Enables courts to strike down unconstitutional laws or actions.

6. **Contempt of Court Powers (Article 129, 215)** - Protects the dignity and authority of courts.

**Judicial independence as a prerequisite of democracy**

1. An independent judiciary safeguards **rule of law** over **rule by power,** ensuring that all state actions remain within constitutional limits.

2. Courts act as **guardians of the Constitution** by protecting citizens’ **fundamental rights** from legislative or executive encroachment. Eg- Shreya Singhal case invalidating Section 66A of IT Act

3. **Maintains Separation of Powers** and prevents concentration of power.

4. In a democracy based on **constitutional supremacy,** courts act as a bulwark against **populist or authoritarian tendencies.** Eg- **Basic Structure Doctrine.**

5. **Ensures Accountability of the Government** through **judicial review (Articles 13, 32, 226).** Eg- NJAC Judgment

6. **Upholds Constitutional Morality** by progressive interpretation of laws. Eg- Sabrimala Verdict

7. **Prevents Executive Overreach** - Independent courts can **strike down arbitrary laws or actions** (Indira Gandhi v. Raj Narain, 1975).

8. **Ensures Peaceful Resolution of Disputes** - A credible judiciary provides **non-violent conflict resolution,** strengthening democratic stability.

| Challenges | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| Lack of transparency in Collegium | Early finalization of MoP |
| Post-retirement appointments | Cooling-off period; bar on political postings |
| Allegations of corruption | Judicial accountability law |
| Judicial overreach criticism | Adherence to judicial restraint & separation of powers |

“There is no better test of the excellence of government than the working of its judicial system.” - **Lord Bryce**

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q37-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 38 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q38-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the desirability of greater representation to women in the higher judiciary to ensure diversity, equity and inclusiveness. [Year: 2021] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

“Indian Judiciary is not only guardian of the Constitution but also Active Participants in the Social Revolution.” - **J.L.**

**Nehru.** Greater women representation in the judiciary is essential for this vision.

**Fact File**

**1. Only 4% of all the Supreme Court judges have been women**

**2. No women CJI**

  - As of 2025, only **1 Supreme Court judge** and about **13% of High Court judges** are women **Desirability of Greater Representation**

  - **Constitutional Imperative** - Fulfils **Articles 14, 15(3), and 39A,** ensuring equal opportunity and access to justice for all.

  - **Corrects Historical Imbalance-** Addresses structural and institutional barriers in legal and judicial careers.

  - **Diversity of Perspective** - Women judges bring unique **social insights, empathy, and lived experiences,** enriching judicial reasoning and deliberation.

  - **Gender-Sensitive Adjudication** - Enhances sensitivity in matters of **sexual violence, family law, and gender rights.** Eg- Justice Indu Malhotra’s dissent in **Sabarimala (2018).**

  - **Enhances Legitimacy and Public Trust** - A judiciary reflective of society’s diversity strengthens **citizen confidence** in judicial impartiality.

  - **Democratization of Judiciary** - Promotes **inclusive representation** across gender, class, and region, aligning with democratic ideals.

  - **Global Democratic Benchmark** - Countries like **Canada and the UK** have achieved near gender parity, improving judicial balance and inclusivity.

  - **Role-Model Effect** - Inspires **women in the legal profession,** helping address underrepresentation in senior judicial positions.

| Challenges | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| Opaque Collegium System lacking<br>gender-sensitive selection criteria | Introduce diversity criteria in judicial<br>appointments |
| Structural Barriers in the legal profession<br>(limited networking, mentorship) | Encourage women’s participation in bar<br>councils and tribunals |
| Socio-cultural Biases discouraging women<br>from litigation careers | Establish institutional mentorship and<br>leadership programs |

“There is no better test of the excellence of government than the working of its judicial system.” - **Lord Bryce.** The true measure of that excellence lies in **Gender Equality** in Judiciary.

[Question ID: 2021-gs2-q38-civilsdaily]

## Question 39 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q39-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the evolution of the collegium system in India. Critically examine the advantages and disadvantages of the system of appointment of the Judges of the Supreme**

**Court of India and that of the USA. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **appointment of judges** is central to **judicial independence** and part of the **Basic Structure (Kesavananda**

**Bharati, 1973).**

In India, this evolved through **judicial interpretation,** whereas in the **USA,** the process is constitutionally and politically structured.

**Evolution of the Collegium System in India**

1. **Articles 124(2)** and **217(1)-** Provide for appointment of **judges** by the **President** after **consultation with the Chief Justice of India (CJI).**

2. **First Judges Case (S.P. Gupta v. Union of India, 1981)**

  - Held that **“consultation” does not mean “concurrence.” CJI’s opinion was not binding**

  - Gave **primacy to the executive** in judicial appointments and transfers.

3. **Second Judges Case (Supreme Court Advocates-on-Record Association v. Union of India, 1993) -** Overruled the 1981 judgment. Established the **Collegium System- CJI + 2 senior-most judges** of the Supreme Court to recommend appointments.

4. **Third Judges Case (Re Presidential Reference, 1998) -** Expanded the collegium to **CJI + 4 senior-most judges.** Made consultation with the collegium **binding on the executive.**

5. Parliament passed the **99th Constitutional Amendment** to replace the collegium with NJAC. CompositionCJI, 2 senior judges, Law Minister, and 2 eminent persons.

6. **Supreme Court Judgment (2015),** NJAC was **struck down** as **unconstitutional,** violating the **Basic Structure** (judicial independence).

7. **Present Status**

- **Collegium Composition-**

  - **Supreme Court-** CJI + 4 senior-most judges.

  - **High Court-** Chief Justice + 2 senior-most judges.

- **Executive Role-** Can **seek reconsideration once,** but must **accept the collegium’s recommendation** thereafter.

In the USA, Judges of the Supreme Court are **nominated by the President** and **confirmed by the Senate** after public hearings.

**Analysis of system of appointment**

1. India

| Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- |
| Ensures judicial independence from executive<br>interference. | Opaque and unaccountable - no transparency in<br>selection criteria. |
| Merit-based selection among senior judges<br>familiar with the judicial process | Lack of diversity - poor gender, regional, and<br>social representation. |
| Prevents politicisation of appointment process | Alleged nepotism (“judges appointing judges”). |
| Upholds separation of powers | No formal mechanism for public or institutional<br>oversight. |

2. USA

| Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- |
| Transparent process - public hearings and<br>debates ensure accountability. | Highly politicised - influenced by the President's<br>ideology and Senate majority. |
| Checks and balances through legislative<br>scrutiny (Senate). | Lifetime tenure can lead to prolonged ideological<br>bias. |
| Allows public assessment of nominee’s<br>philosophy and ethics. | May compromise judicial neutrality due to<br>partisan loyalties. |

India’s collegium ensures independence but **lacks transparency,** whereas the U.S. model ensures openness but **risks politicisation.** Thus, a mechanism balancing **independence and accountability** is essential to preserve **judicial integrity and democratic legitimacy.**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q39-civilsdaily]

## Question 40 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q40-civilsdaily**

**Explain the reasons for the growth of public interest litigation in India. As a result of it, has the Indian Supreme Court emerged as the world’s most powerful judiciary? [Year: 2024] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Public Interest Litigation (PIL)** is a judicial mechanism that allows **any public-spirited individual or organization**

to approach the court **on behalf of others** for the enforcement of **fundamental or legal rights.** It evolved in India as a tool of **“social action litigation”,** expanding the reach of **justice under Article 32 and 226.**

**Objectives PIL**

1. Accessibility of Justice

2. Government Accountability

3. Judicial Oversight

4. Rule of Law and Balance **Reasons for the Growth of PIL in India**

1. **Historical Reasons-** Postcolonial India witnessed **deep social inequality and poverty.** PIL emerged as a tool for **social transformation and distributive justice.**

2. **Liberalization of Locus Standi-** Shift from **“personally aggrieved”** to **“any public-spirited citizen”** can file a case.

3. **Judicial Activism-** Post-Emergency judiciary reasserted itself as the **guardian of rights and liberties.** EgJustice Krishna Iyer first introduced the idea of public interest litigation in India in Mumbai Kamagar Sabha v.

Abdul Thai in 1976.

4. **Decline of Parliament-** Legislative inefficiency and lack of responsiveness to social issues. Eg- Visaka Guidelines

5. **Rise of Civil Society Activism-** Growth of **NGOs, media, and human rights groups.** Began using PILs to advance **environmental, social, and human rights causes.** Eg- Right to livelihood under Article 32 in Bandhua Mukti Morcha judgment

6. **Executive Overreach and Inaction-** Bureaucratic corruption, maladministration, and policy failures prompted the judiciary to step in as a **corrective mechanism.** Eg- Vineet Narain v. Union of India (1997) CBI accountability case.

7. **Global Factors-** Comparative judicial trends (e.g., U.S. public interest litigation) inspired Indian legal activism.

8. **Constitutional Powers and Design- Directive Principles of State Policy (Part IV)** provided moral backing for socio-economic justice through judicial intervention.

9. **Expansion of Fundamental Rights-** Courts interpreted **Article 21** broadly - including **right to livelihood, education, health, and clean environment (Olga Tellis, 1985; M.C. Mehta, 1986).**

**Indian Supreme Court as the world’s most powerful judiciary**

**Arguments in Favour**

1. **Widest Judicial Review Powers-** Can strike down laws, executive actions, and even constitutional amendments under **Articles 13, 32, and 226.** Eg- NJAC verdict

2. **Suo Motu Jurisdiction-** Eg- SC took suo motu cognizance of **COVID-19 Migrant Crisis (2020).**

3. **Epistolary Jurisdiction-** Allows letters/postcards as petitions, widening access to justice. Eg- **Sunil Batra v.**

**Delhi Administration (1978)** - Letter treated as habeas corpus.

4. **Expansion of Fundamental Rights-** Eg- **Puttaswamy (2017)** - Right to Privacy recognized as a Fundamental Right.

5. **Judicial Oversight on Policy-** Eg- **Vishaka Guidelines (1997)** - Workplace sexual harassment norms framed by the SC.

6. **Legislative powers** under **Article 141** and **Executive powers under Article 142. Eg-** SC judgment on government’s veto power

7. **Moral and Institutional Legitimacy-** Eg- **Navtej Johar (2018)** - Decriminalized homosexuality on grounds of dignity and liberty.

**Arguments Against**

1. **Judicial Overreach-** Encroachment into executive and legislative domains. Eg- **SC ban on firecrackers** was criticized for overreach.

2. **Weak Enforcement Capacity-** Eg- **Prakash Singh Police Reforms (2006)** - Non-compliance by most states.

3. **Frivolous or Politically Motivated PILs** increase **pendency (more than 5Cr cases pending)** Eg- Misuse in **Election-related PILs** delaying legitimate processes.

4. **Lack of Judicial Accountability-** Eg- **In-House Inquiry (Justice Ramaswamy case)** exposed inadequacies in judicial discipline.

5. **Opaque Collegium System-** criticised for lack of transparency and nepotism. Eg- **uncle judge syndrome (law commission)**

6. **Inconsistency and Selective Intervention-** Uneven judicial response to similar issues affects credibility. EgContrasting stances in **Bail cases.**

7. Frequent judicial intrusion weakens **separation of powers.**

**Way Forward**

1. **Institutional Restraint-** Judiciary must uphold **separation of powers**

2. Enforce **strict scrutiny** of PILs to prevent misuse for personal, political, or publicity motives.

3. Develop a **judgment-monitoring mechanism** to ensure effective execution of court orders

4. Promote **constitutional literacy** and encourage responsible use of judicial remedies by citizens.

5. Dedicated **PIL Cells** in High Courts As **Justice J.S. Verma** cautioned, “Judicial activism must not become judicial adventurism.” Thus, judicial activism must be balanced with Judicial Restraint.

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q40-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 41 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q41-civilsdaily**

**Judicial Legislation is antithetical to the doctrine of separation of powers as envisaged in the Indian Constitution. In this context justify the filing of a large number of public interest**

**petitions praying for issuing guidelines to executive authorities. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

Doctrine of separation of powers is derived from **Montesquieu’s Spirit of Laws (1748)** and divides state power into **Legislature, Executive, Judiciary** to prevent concentration and arbitrariness.

**Judicial legislation,** i.e., when courts make laws or frame policies, is generally **inconsistent** with this doctrine.

**Separation of Powers in India**

**1. Art. 245-246 & 368 -** Only legislature can enact/amend laws under

**2.** Article 105, 194 - Executive cannot breach **parliamentary privileges**

**3. Art. 122, 212 -** Courts cannot inquire into parliamentary/state legislature proceedings ().

**4. Art. 124(4) Judicial Conduct Immunity**

**5. Judicial Independence** - Security of tenure, salaries, and contempt powers.

**Judicial Legislation Antithetical to Separation of Powers**

**1. Encroachment on Legislative Domain** undermines Separation of Powers.

**2. Undermines Democratic Accountability -** judges are **unelected** and not responsible for policy outcomes. **Eg-** Criticism of **Ban on firecrackers (2017)**

**3. Creates judicial supremacy and Judicial governance (Bhikhu Parekh) - Eg- Prakash Singh guidelines** on police reforms

**4. Weakens Public trust in Legislature and Executive - Eg- Coal Allocation Case (2014)** - Court cancelled 214 coal blocks, creating **policy paralysis** in the energy sector.

**5.** Courts **lack technical and administrative expertise** for framing complex policy guidelines. **Eg- Ban on diesel vehicles in NCR (2016)**

**6. Judicial itself has warned against overreach in**

- **Aravali Golf Club v. Chander Hass (2008)-** Court warned against **“judicial enthusiasm turning into judicial adventurism.”**

- **State of Tamil Nadu v. State of Kerala (2014)-** Reiterated that **courts should not enter the policy-making domain.**

**Justification for Filing Large Number of PILs**

1. **Executive Inaction or Policy Vacuum Eg- Vishaka** guidelines on workplace sexual harassment.

2. **Protection of Fundamental Rights** - **Eg- Hussainara Khatoon (1979) case** Recognized right to **speedy trial** as FR under Article 21.

3. **Accountability and Transparency** - **Eg- In Vineet Narain** case, SC issued directions to make **CBI independent** from political interference.

4. **Access to Justice** - **Liberal locus standi** allows citizens to represent the poor and marginalized.

5. **Legislative or Administrative Delay** - **Eg- Navtej Johar (2018)-** Decriminalized homosexuality after years of legislative inaction.

6. **Constitutional Mandate** under **Article 141 and 142** to uphold **complete justice.**

7. **Rise of Civil Society Activism** - NGOs and activists use PILs to **expose violations and empower citizens.Eg- M.C. Mehta Cases on environmental protection.**

8. **Judicial Review** powers under Article 13, 32, 226 to uphold **Constitutional Supremacy**

9. **Speedy Justice - timely intervention** in cases affecting public interest. **Eg- Olga Tellis (1985)-** Protected the **right to livelihood.**

10. **Public Trust in Judiciary** - Rise in PILs reflects **citizens’ confidence** in the judiciary as the **last resort for justice.**

**Issues**

1. **Judicial Overreach-** Encroachment into executive and legislative domains. Eg- **SC ban on firecrackers** was criticized for overreach.

2. **Weak Enforcement Capacity-** Eg- **Prakash Singh Police Reforms (2006)** - Non-compliance by most states.

3. **Frivolous or Politically Motivated PILs** increase **pendency (more than 5Cr cases pending)** Eg- Misuse in **Election-related PILs** delaying legitimate processes.

4. **Lack of Judicial Accountability-** Eg- **In-House Inquiry (Justice Ramaswamy case)** exposed inadequacies in judicial discipline.

5. **Opaque Collegium System-** criticised for lack of transparency and nepotism. Eg- **uncle judge syndrome (law commission)**

6. **Inconsistency and Selective Intervention-** Uneven judicial response to similar issues affects credibility. EgContrasting stances in **Bail cases.**

7. Frequent judicial intrusion weakens **separation of powers.**

**Way Forward**

1. **Institutional Restraint-** Judiciary must uphold **separation of powers**

2. Enforce **strict scrutiny** of PILs to prevent misuse for personal, political, or publicity motives.

3. Develop a **judgment-monitoring mechanism** to ensure effective execution of court orders

4. Promote **constitutional literacy** and encourage responsible use of judicial remedies by citizens.

5. Dedicated **PIL Cells** in High Courts PILs represent **participatory constitutionalism,** ensuring that justice remains **accessible, accountable, and rights-oriented.**

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q41-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 42 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q42-civilsdaily**

**Who are entitled to receive free legal aid? Assess the role of the National Legal Services Authority(NALSA) in rendering free legal aid in India [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

. 10 Free legal aid** is a fundamental component of **access to justice.** It is provided under **Article 39A** of the Constitution and operationalized through the **Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987.**

**Persons Entitled to Free Legal Aid (Section 12, Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987)**

1. **Persons with Annual Income below prescribed limit** - ₹3 lakh (SCs), ₹1.5 lakh (District courts) (subject to State variation)

2. **Women and Children**

3. **Members of SC/ST Communities**

4. **Victims of Human Trafficking or Begar**

5. **Persons with Disabilities**

6. **Industrial Workmen**

7. **Persons in Custody** - including undertrials, detainees, and prisoners

8. **Victims of Mass Disaster, Ethnic Violence, Flood, Drought, or Industrial Accidents Role of NALSA in Rendering Free Legal Aid**

1. **Institutional Framework-**

  - NALSA is headed by the **Chief Justice of India** as Patron-in-Chief.

  - Supervises **State Legal Services Authorities (SLSAs), District Legal Services Authorities (DLSAs),** and **Taluk Legal Services Committees (TLSCs).**

2. **Legal Awareness-Conducts Lok Adalats, Legal Literacy Camps,** and **Legal Aid Clinics** in rural and marginalized areas.

3. **Alternative Dispute Resolution (ADR)-** Organizes **Lok Adalats** for **speedy, low-cost dispute resolution.**

Eg- National Lok Adalat (2023) settled over **1.3 crore cases** across India.

4. **Specialized Schemes for Vulnerable Groups- Eg- NALSA (Protection and Enforcement of Tribal Rights) Scheme, 2015.**

5. **Free Legal Representation and Counsel** to represent eligible persons in courts and tribunals.

6. **Legal Aid in Prisons for undertrials** through regular **jail legal aid clinics.**

7. **Use of Technology-** Launched **E-Lok Adalat** for digital access to justice in remote areas.

**Challenges**

1. **Low Awareness -** As per **India Justice Report,** despite over **80% of citizens eligible,** only **15 million** have availed legal aid since 1995.

2. **Shortage of Trained Lawyers -** avoid **pro bono work** due to **low honorarium (₹1,500-₹7,500)**

3. **Resource Constraints - India spends only ₹0.75 per capita annually (2017-18) on legal aid**

4. **Implementation Gaps-** Uneven performance across states and districts.

**Way Forward**

1. **Increase Grant-in-Aid** and resource allocation to NALSA.

2. **Launch awareness campaigns** via radio, TV, and local media.

3. **Fair compensation and incentives** for panel lawyers and PLVs.

4. **Mandatory annual pro bono work** for advocates with merit-based recognition.

**Free legal aid** is essential to realize the **constitutional promise of “justice-social, economic, and political.”**

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q42-civilsdaily]

## Question 43 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q43-civilsdaily**

**How far do you agree with the view that tribunals curtail the jurisdiction of ordinary courts? In view of the above, discuss the constitutional validity and competency of the**

**tribunals in India. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Tribunals** are **quasi-judicial bodies** established to deliver **specialized, speedy, and cost-effective justice** in specific matters like taxation, service disputes, environment etc.

**Objectives of Tribunals**

1. **Specialized Adjudication**

2. **Speedy Justice Delivery**

3. **Reduce Burden on Courts**

4. **Cost-Effective Dispute Resolution**

5. **Simplified Procedures - Principles of Natural Justice**

6. **Decentralized Justice Access**

7. **Efficient Enforcement of Rights - Eg- NGT Tribunals Curtailing the Jurisdiction of Ordinary Courts**

1. **Exclusion of High Court Jurisdiction** - Articles **323A** & **323B** permit exclusion of **High Courts’ writ powers** in tribunal matters.

2. **Executive Control over Appointments** of tribunal members

3. Creates **Parallel Judicial System** However, in **L. Chandra Kumar (1997)** Case, SC struck down exclusion of High Court jurisdiction as **unconstitutional,** reaffirming **judicial review** as part of **basic structure.**

**Constitutional Validity of Tribunals**

1. **Constitutional Basis** - Established under **Articles 323A & 323B** (42nd Amendment, 1976) for **specialized adjudication.**

2. **Legislative Competence** - Parliament empowered to create tribunals for **administrative and quasi-judicial functions** under **Entry 11A, Concurrent List.**

3. **Judicial Endorsement** - **L. Chandra Kumar v. Union of India (1997)-** Upheld **constitutional validity** of tribunals.

4. **Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021** - Reorganized and rationalized tribunals.

5. **Functional Validity** - Tribunals are **supplementary judicial mechanisms,** constitutionally valid as long as they **do not replace or restrict** High Court or Supreme Court powers.

**Competency of Tribunals in India**

1. **Limited Jurisdiction** - Competency confined to **specific subject matters.** Eg- NGT on environment.

2. **Quasi-Judicial Powers** - Empowered to **summon witnesses, take evidence, and deliver binding decisions** similar to civil courts.

3. **No Inherent Jurisdiction** - Unlike High Courts, tribunals **cannot exercise writ or contempt powers** unless conferred by statute.

4. **Subject to Judicial Review** - Decisions are **reviewable by High Courts.** (L. Chandra Kumar, 1997).

5. **Appellate and Supervisory Role** - Certain tribunals (e.g., **NCLAT, DRAT)** exercise **appellate functions** over subordinate tribunals or authorities.

6. **Administrative Control** - Function under **ministries** (NCLT under Corporate Affairs).

7. **Guided by Principles of Natural Justice** - Must ensure **fair hearing, reasoned orders, and impartial adjudication** in all proceedings.

Tribunals, in line with **Article 39A,** must enhance **access to justice** while upholding **judicial independence** and **judicial review** as constitutional safeguards.

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q43-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 44 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q44-civilsdaily**

**“The Central Administration Tribunal which was [Year: 2019]**

### Answer

established for redressal of grievances and complaints by or against central government employees, nowadays is**

**exercising its powers as an independent judicial authority.” Explain. (10)**

The **CAT** was established under **Article 323A** and **Administrative Tribunals Act, 1985** to provide **speedy and specialized justice** in **service matters** of Central Government employees.

**Objectives of the Central Administrative Tribunal (CAT)**

1. **Speedy Justice**

2. **Reduce Burden on Courts**

3. **Specialized Adjudication**

4. **Ensure Administrative Fairness** - Protect employees from **arbitrary, unjust, or discriminatory administrative actions.**

5. **Accessible and Affordable Justice**

6. Maintain **consistency and coherence** in decisions across departments.

7. **Promote Administrative Accountability**

8. **Realize Article 39A** by making justice **accessible to all employees.**

**CAT as Independent Judicial Authority**

**1. Exclusive Jurisdiction-**

- CAT adjudicates disputes on **recruitment, promotion, transfer, service conditions, and disciplinary actions** of Central employees.

- Its jurisdiction **excludes ordinary civil courts** in service matters.

**2. Powers of a Civil Court,** including summoning witnesses, receiving evidence, and delivering binding orders.

**3.** Though under the **DoPT** for administrative purposes, CAT functions **judicially independent** in decision-making.

**4. Judicial Review Authority-** CAT can **strike down administrative actions** violating **Articles 14, 16, or 21.**

**Eg-** It has quashed arbitrary transfer orders and disciplinary actions in several cases.

**5.** Following **L. Chandra Kumar v. Union of India (1997),** CAT decisions are subject to **judicial review** by High Courts under **Articles 226/227,** ensuring accountability.

| Issues | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Executive Control (DoPT<br>Dependence) | Ensure functional autonomy and financial independence. |
| 2. Vacancies & Staffing<br>Shortages (>50%) | time-bound recruitment, and balanced mix of judicial and<br>administrative members. |
| 3. Case Backlog | Introduce e-filing, virtual hearings, and case management<br>systems. |
| 6. Accessibility Issues (Few<br>Regional Benches) | Establish circuit benches in remote regions |
| 7. Inadequate Infrastructure<br>& Digitization | Enhance budgetary allocation, improve digital infrastructure, and<br>integrate with e-Courts network. |

CAT, in line with **Article 39A,** must ensure **administrative justice** while upholding **judicial independence** and **judicial review** as constitutional safeguards.

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q44-civilsdaily]

## Question 45 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q45-civilsdaily**

**Explain and distinguish between Lok Adalats And Arbitration Tribunals. Whether they entertain civil as well as criminal cases? [Year: 2024] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

Both **Lok Adalats** and **Arbitration Tribunals** are alternative dispute resolution (ADR) mechanisms aimed at ensuring **speedy, cost-effective, and accessible justice,** reducing the burden on regular courts.

**Objectives of Lok Adalats and Arbitration Tribunals**

1. **Speedy Justice Delivery**

2. **Reduction in Judicial Burden**

3. **Cost-Effective Dispute Resolution**

4. Encourage **conciliation, compromise, and mutual agreement** between disputing parties.

5. **Access to Justice (Article 39A)**

6. **Procedural Flexibility Difference between Lok Adalats And Arbitration Tribunals**

| Basis | Lok Adalat | Arbitration Tribunal |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Legal Basis | Legal Services Authorities Act,<br>1987 | Arbitration & Conciliation Act, 1996 |
| Objective | Access to justice for all (Article 39A) | Commercial efficiency and party<br>autonomy |
| Nature | Public, welfare-oriented forum | Private, contractual forum |
| Mode of<br>Settlement | Conciliation and compromise | Adjudication based on contract |
| Composition | Judges, lawyers, social workers | Arbitrators chosen by parties |
| Cost | No court fee is charged | Parties bear the cost of arbitration |
| Appeal mechanism | No appeal is allowed | Appeal allowed under Arbitration and<br>Conciliation Act, 1996 |

**Jurisdiction Over Civil and Criminal Cases**

**Lok Adalats**

1. Can hear **civil cases** such as family disputes, land or property matters.

2. **Non-compoundable offences** (e.g., murder, rape, theft) are **outside its jurisdiction.**

**Arbitration Tribunals**

1. **Civil Jurisdiction -** Deal with **contractual, commercial, and property disputes** arising from an agreement between parties.

2. Have **no jurisdiction** over criminal cases since criminal liability is **non-contractual** and involves offences against the State.

Both Lok Adalats and Administrative Tribunals ensure **access to speedy justice** under **Article 39A.** Their effective functioning is essential to ensure **justice is neither delayed nor denied.**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q45-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 46 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q46-civilsdaily**

**Comment on the need of administrative tribunals as compared to the court system.**

**Assess the impact of the recent tribunal reforms through rationalization of tribunals made in**

**2021. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **42nd Constitutional Amendment (1976)** inserted **Article 323A & 323B,** enabling the establishment of **administrative tribunals.**

**Need of Administrative Tribunals**

1. **Specialized Expertise** - Handle technical issues in areas like **taxation, environment, telecom, competition, service matters** requiring domain knowledge. **Eg- National Green Tribunal (NGT)** combines legal and scientific expertise.

2. **Speedy Justice** - Simplified procedures ensure faster disposal compared to lengthy **court litigation. Eg- Central Administrative Tribunal (CAT)** resolves service disputes faster than High Courts.

3. **Reducing Burden on Courts** - By transferring specialized disputes, tribunals ease the case load of **High Courts and Supreme Court.**

4. **Cost-effective and Accessible** - Flexible procedures and lower costs improve access to justice, especially for **citizens and government employees.**

5. **Quasi-Judicial Role** - Act as a bridge between **executive actions** and **judicial review,** combining policy interpretation with dispute resolution. **Eg- Telecom Disputes Settlement and Appellate Tribunal (TDSAT).**

6. **Consistency and Uniformity** - Ensure predictable and uniform rulings in specialized sectors. **Eg- Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT)** promotes consistency in investor protection cases.

7. **Citizen-Centric Justice** - Designed to make justice less formal, quicker, and more stakeholder-friendly in sensitive domains.

**Tribunal Reforms Act, 2021**

**Provisions**

- **Abolition/Merger of Tribunals-** Transferred functions of several appellate tribunals (e.g., Film Certification Appellate Tribunal, Airport Appellate Tribunal) to High Courts.

- **Appointment and Tenure-** Members appointed by the government from panels prepared by a selection committee; tenure fixed at **4 years** with maximum age of **50 years (Chairperson)** and **67 years (others).**

- **Rationalization Objective-** Reduce multiplicity of tribunals, improve efficiency, and strengthen judicial oversight.

**Positive Impacts**

1. **Rationalization of Tribunals** - Abolition/merger of smaller tribunals avoided **fragmentation and duplication. Eg- Film Certification Appellate Tribunal’s functions shifted to High Courts.**

2. **Judicial Oversight Strengthened** - Transfer of jurisdiction back to **High Courts and Supreme Court** enhances **consistency of judgments** and **constitutional accountability.**

3. **Resource Optimization** - Reduced expenditure on maintaining multiple tribunals with low caseloads.

4. **Efficiency in Case Management** - Consolidation brought clarity in **jurisdiction** and **procedures,** reducing confusion among litigants.

**Concerns and Challenges**

1. **Increased Burden on High Courts** - High Courts already face **60+ lakh pending cases (2023);** transfer of tribunal matters may worsen delays.

2. **Loss of Specialized Expertise** - High Courts may lack **technical knowledge** in fields like environment, telecom, or competition law, undermining quality of justice.

3. **Access to Justice Issues** - Litigants in remote areas lose proximity of tribunals; approaching High Courts can be costly and time-consuming.

4. **Independence Questioned** - Provisions on **short tenure (4 years)** and **government dominance in appointments** raised concerns, criticized in **Madras Bar Association v. Union of India (2021).**

5. **Dilution of Objective** - The original vision of tribunals under **42nd Constitutional Amendment** (Articles 323A & 323B) was to provide **specialized, speedy justice-this** stands weakened.

**Way Forward**

1. **Retain Domain Expertise** - Where technical issues dominate (like **NGT, TDSAT, SAT),** maintain **specialized tribunals** instead of shifting cases to High Courts.

2. **Capacity Building of High Courts** - Provide **technical benches, expert advisors, digital case management** to handle transferred tribunal functions effectively.

3. **Accessibility for Citizens** - Establish **regional benches** and adopt **e-filing, virtual hearings** to reduce distance and costs for litigants.

4. **Periodic Review of Tribunal Performance** - Independent audits to assess **caseload, efficiency, pendency reduction,** and decide which tribunals to retain/merge.

5. **Harmonization of Laws** - Avoid overlapping jurisdiction by clear statutory allocation of cases between tribunals and courts.

Tribunal reforms must balance **judicial efficiency** with **specialized expertise and independence,** ensuring tribunals remain **accessible, credible, and effective instruments of justice.**

**Local Government**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q46-civilsdaily]
