## Question 47 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q47-civilsdaily**

**To what extent, in your opinion, as the decentralisation of power in India changed the governance landscape at the grassroots? [Year: 2022] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

73rd and 74th CAA are the embodiment of **grass-root democracy** and **democratic decentralization** in India.

They are inspired by **Gandhiji’s concept** of **“Oceanic Circles of Power”** and **“Swaraj”.**

**Positive Transformations in Governance Landscape**

1. **Rajni Kothari** described local bodies as “schools of democracy” where political awareness and participation are cultivated at the grassroots level.

2. **Democratic Deepening** with over **32 lakh elected representatives** across **2.6 lakh Panchayats** and **4,000+ urban bodies.**

3. **Social Inclusion and Equity** - Reservation for **SCs, STs, and women** enhanced representation of marginalised groups in decision-making. Eg- Women’s Representation at **46.44%**

4. Panchayats manage programmes like **MGNREGA, PMAY-G, Jal Jeevan Mission,** bringing last-mile efficiency.

5. **Participatory Planning** - Gram Sabhas and Ward Committees have improved **local accountability** and **need-based development.** Eg- Social Audits of MGNREGA

6. **Strengthened Disaster Response** - Eg- during **COVID-19** and **Kerala floods.**

7. **Efficient Resource Management- Eg-** Hiware Bazar in Maharashtra effectively managed water resources through watershed development.

8. **Conflict Resolution-** Reports indicate a 30% reduction in petty disputes reaching district courts due to effective Panchayat mediation.

**Challenges**

1. **“PRIs exist as over-structured but underpowered organisations.” (2nd ARC)**

2. No decentralisation of power, rather **decentralisation of corruption - Mani Shankar Iyer Committee**

3. **Dependence on higher tiers-** Around **95% of Panchayat funds** come from **Central/State transfers,** limiting fiscal autonomy.

4. **Limited own-source revenue-** Poor **tax collection efficiency** (>1% own tax).

5. **Barriers to Local Taxation- Freebie culture** and **fear of losing popularity** discourage local taxation.

6. **Incomplete devolution-** less than **20% of States** have transferred all **29 subjects under 11th Schedule** (MoPR, 2022). (overall Panchayat Devolution Index is only 43.89% (2021-22))

7. **Centralised Welfare via Cash Transfers -** The welfare state now relies on **DBT** through **JAM,** bypassing panchayats and reducing **local accountability and participatory governance**

8. **Shortage of staff-** Average **0.67 Panchayat Secretaries per Gram Panchayat,** as low as **0.33 in Uttar Pradesh.**

9. **Weak Gram Sabhas-** Low participation, elite domination, and token meetings.

10. **Gender and social barriers** leading to **proxy control. (Sarpanch Pati) Manishankar Aiyar Committee recommendations.**

1. Establish **National Commission for Panchayati Raj.**

2. Create **Panchayat Ombudsman** for grievance redressal.

3. Set up a **separate cadre of Panchayat bureaucracy** with a **code of conduct.**

4. Adopt **activity mapping** for clear delineation of **3Fs - Funds, Functions, Functionaries.**

To realise the vision of **“Gram Uday se Bharat Uday”,** India needs **second-generation Panchayati Raj reforms**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q47-civilsdaily]

## Question 48 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q48-civilsdaily**

**Analyze the role of local bodies in providing good governance at local level and bring out the pros and cons of merging the rural local bodies with the urban local bodies. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

’** 73rd and 74th CAA are the embodiment of **grass-root democracy** and **democratic decentralization** in India.

They are inspired by **Gandhiji’s concept** of **“Oceanic Circles of Power”** and **“Swaraj”.**

**Role of Local Bodies in Ensuring Good Governance**

8. **Citizen Participation** in planning and decision-making through **Gram Sabhas** and **Ward Committees.** EgKerala’s people plan campaign

9. **Transparency- Eg-** Social audits under **MGNREGA** enhance transparency in fund utilization.

10. **Accountability-** Elected representatives are **directly answerable** to citizens.

11. **Effectiveness and Efficiency-** Proximity to citizens allows **timely service delivery** in areas like sanitation, water supply, and waste management.

12. **Equity and Inclusion** through **reservation for SCs, STs, OBCs, and women.** Eg- Women’s Representation at **46.44%**

13. **Responsiveness** during Disasters. Eg- **COVID-19 management** and disaster relief in **Kerala.**

14. **Rule of Law-** Local bodies function under **constitutional and legal mandates** of the **73rd and 74th Amendments,** ensuring lawful governance.

15. **Consensus-Oriented Governance-** Gram Sabhas and **District Planning Committees** promote **coordination and collective decision-making.**

16. **Sustainability-** service delivery in **waste recycling** and **water conservation.** Eg- **Hiware Bazar Model** of watershed development.

**Challenges**

6. **“PRIs exist as over-structured but underpowered organisations.” (2nd ARC)**

7. No decentralisation of power, rather **decentralisation of corruption - Mani Shankar Iyer Committee**

8. **Limited own-source revenue-** Poor **tax collection efficiency** (>1% own tax).

9. **Incomplete devolution-** less than **20% of States** have transferred all **29 subjects under 11th Schedule** (MoPR, 2022). (overall Panchayat Devolution Index is only 43.89% (2021-22))

10. **Weak Gram Sabhas-** Low participation, elite domination, and token meetings.

11. **Gender and social barriers** leading to **proxy control. (Sarpanch Pati) Pros of Merging Rural and Urban Local Bodies**

1. **Integrated Regional Planning** - Addresses **rurban** challenges and ensures seamless infrastructure and service delivery.

2. **Uniform Standards of Governance** - Streamlines policies, taxation, and land-use planning.

3. **Efficient Resource Utilization** - Shared funding and manpower reduce duplication.

4. **Better Management of Peri-Urban Areas** - Urban expansion can be better planned and regulated.

**Cons of Merging Rural and Urban Local Bodies**

3. **Dilution of Constitutional Intent** under **73rd and 74th Amendments.**

4. **Political Resistance** - Power redistribution may face **bureaucratic and political opposition.**

5. **Administrative Complexity** due to different laws, tax systems, and governance mechanisms.

6. Urban areas may dominate **resource allocation** and decision-making.

Local bodies are the **cornerstones of participatory democracy** and good governance. A **functional convergence,** not structural merger, aligns better with India’s **constitutional vision of decentralization.**

## Question 49 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q49-civilsdaily**

**Assess the importance of the Panchayat system in India as a part of local government.**

**Apart from government grants, what sources can the Panchayats look out for financing**

**developmental projects? [Year: 2018] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

73rd and 74th CAA are the embodiment of **grass-root democracy** and **democratic decentralization** in India. They are inspired by **Gandhiji’s concept** of **“Oceanic Circles of Power”** and **“Swaraj”.**

**Importance of PRIs**

1. **Rajni Kothari** - Described local bodies as “schools of democracy” where political awareness and participation are cultivated at the grassroots level.

2. **Democratic Decentralization** - Eg- Kerala’s People’s Plan Campaign grants local bodies control over **40% of the state’s plan budget.**

3. **Capacity Building-** Training programs for PRI members. Eg- e-Panchayat initiative

4. **Efficient Local Service Delivery-** Addresses local needs in **sanitation, drinking water, roads, housing, and education. Eg-** Hiware Bazar Model of watershed development.

5. **Transparency-** Direct accountability to local constituents. Eg- Rajasthan’s Social Audit Mechanism

6. **Implementation of Schemes,** ensuring that benefits reach the grassroots. Eg- MGNREGA.

7. **Financial Autonomy-** PRIs have the power to levy taxes and mobilize resources, which helps them fund and manage local development projects.

8. **Conflict Resolution-** Reports indicate a 30% reduction in petty disputes reaching district courts due to effective Panchayat mediation.

9. **Inclusivity-** Reserving seats for women, Scheduled Castes, and Scheduled Tribes. Eg- Women’s Representation at **46.44% Case Study**

**1. Bina Devi, Bihar - Known as “Mushroom Mahila”, train 1,500 women, and foster rural entrepreneurship.**

**Challenges**

1. **“PRIs exist as over-structured but underpowered organisations.” (2nd ARC)**

2. No decentralisation of power, rather **decentralisation of corruption - Mani Shankar Iyer Committee**

3. **Dependence on higher tiers-** Around **95% of Panchayat funds** come from **Central/State transfers,** limiting fiscal autonomy.

4. **Limited own-source revenue-** Poor **tax collection efficiency** (>1% own tax).

5. **Barriers to Local Taxation- Freebie culture** and **fear of losing popularity** discourage local taxation.

6. **Incomplete devolution-** less than **20% of States** have transferred all **29 subjects under 11th Schedule** (MoPR, 2022). (overall Panchayat Devolution Index is only 43.89% (2021-22))

7. **Centralised Welfare via Cash Transfers -** The welfare state now relies on **DBT** through **JAM,** bypassing panchayats and reducing **local accountability and participatory governance**

8. **Shortage of staff-** Average **0.67 Panchayat Secretaries per Gram Panchayat,** as low as **0.33 in Uttar Pradesh.**

9. **Weak Gram Sabhas-** Low participation, elite domination, and token meetings.

10. **Gender and social barriers** leading to **proxy control. (Sarpanch Pati) Alternate Sources of Panchayat Financing (Beyond Government Grants)**

1. **Own Tax Revenues- Property/House tax, Profession tax, Market fees,** and **Entertainment tax.**

2. **Non-Tax Revenues- Rent from panchayat buildings, water usage fees, license fees,** and **user charges** for services.

3. **Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs)** for **solid waste management, renewable energy,** or **tourism projects.** Eg- **waste-to-energy projects.**

4. **Community Contributions- Voluntary labor (Shramdaan), local donations,** and **community funds** for infrastructure.

5. **Borrowings and Bonds- Soft loans from NABARD** and **Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF)** for rural projects.

6. **Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR)- Eg- Tata Steel CSR projects** in Jharkhand villages for drinking water and sanitation.

7. Using funds from **MGNREGA, 15th FC grants** for rural infrastructure development.

**Way Forward**

1. Strengthen State Finance Commissions (SFCs) with mandatory action on their recommendations.

2. **Manishankar Aiyar Committee recommendations -** Adopt **activity mapping** for clear delineation of **3Fs - Funds, Functions, Functionaries.**

3. **Flexible Funding Norms** under CSS To realise the vision of **“Gram Uday se Bharat Uday”,** India needs **second-generation Panchayati Raj reforms**

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q49-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 50 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q50-civilsdaily**

**“The states in India seem reluctant to empower urban local bodies both functionally as well as financially.” Comment [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

. 10** 73rd and 74th CAA are the embodiment of **grass-root democracy** and **democratic decentralization** in India.

However, they have remained **Half Baked Cake (Mani Shankar Iyer Committee)** due to reluctance of states.

**Reluctance to empower Urban Local Bodies**

1. Funding Challenges

- **High fiscal dependence-** ULBs’ own revenue was only 47% of their total revenue in 2022, with property tax accounting for 29%.

- **Post-GST revenue loss-** Subsumption of **Octroi, sales tax, and entertainment tax** reduced ULB income. Eg- **BMC lost ₹7,000 crore (~35%)** of revenue post-GST.

- **Weak property tax collection-** Only **10-11% of revenue** from property tax vs **20-22% in China** (Peterson Institute).

- **Limited taxation powers-** ULBs lack fiscal autonomy unlike China (land rights), Denmark (local income tax), or USA (broad local taxes).

- **Weak SFC implementation- 15% shortfall** in **15th FC grants** and delayed **State Finance Commissions** reduce fiscal predictability.

2. Functional Challenges

- **Parastatal dominance-** Urban authorities and public corporations control key functions like **water, and transport,** limiting ULB autonomy. Eg- Delhi Jal Board

- **Rise of Special Purpose Vehicles (SPVs)-** Schemes like **JNNURM (2005)** and **Smart Cities Mission (2015)** implemented via SPVs, **bypassing elected bodies.** SPVs, led by bureaucrats, indicate **“procedural distrust”** of local democracy.

- **Incomplete devolution-** Most States haven’t transferred all **18 functions under the 12th Schedule.**

- **Inactive District Planning Committees (DPCs)-** Non-functional in **9 States** and ineffective in **15 others,** hindering integrated planning.

- **Greater Bengaluru Governance Bill (2024)-** Empowers **Greater Bengaluru Authority (GBA)** to **alter ULB boundaries** and override elected councils.

**Reasons for States’ Reluctance to Empower ULBs**

1. **Political Control** - Fear of losing urban dominance.

2. **Discretionary Devolution** - 74th Amendment left powers optional.

3. **Low Political Incentive** - No short-term electoral gain.

4. **Weak Administrative Capacity** - Perceived inefficiency of local bodies.

5. **Limited Citizen Pressure** - Urban voter apathy enables state control.

**Way Forward**

- **Empower ULBs legally** through activity mapping and fiscal autonomy.

- **Constitute SFCs timely,** link grants to performance.

- **Integrate local bodies into planning** via empowered DPCs and MPCs.

Urban Local bodies are the **cornerstones of participatory democracy** and good governance as 50% of India’s population is expected to reside in urban areas by 2030.

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q50-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 51 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q51-civilsdaily**

**The strength of local institutions in India has shifted from their formative phase of**

**‘Functions, Functionaries and Funds’ to the contemporary stage of ‘Functionality’. Highlight**

**the critical challenges faced by local institutions in terms of their functionality in recent**

**times. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

73rd and 74th CAA are the embodiment of **grass-root democracy** and **democratic decentralization** in India.

They are inspired by **Gandhiji’s concept** of **“Oceanic Circles of Power”** and **“Swaraj”.** However, they have remained **Half Baked Cake (Mani Shankar Iyer Committee).**

**Issues in the Formative Phase of Local Governance (3Fs)**

1. Functions-

- Incomplete devolution of 11th & 12th Schedule subjects

- Limited administrative authority

2. Functionaries-

- Lack of training & capacity

- Bureaucratic dominance over elected reps

3. Funds-

- High fiscal dependence on states/centre

- Conditional and tied grants **Critical Challenges to Functionality of Local Institutions**

A. Political Challenges

  - **Delayed Elections-** Eg- **Maharashtra’s 27 Municipal Corporations** functioning under administrators since 2022.

  - **Weak Gram Sabhas-** Low participation, elite domination, and token meetings.

  - With rural population falling from **75% (1990)** to **about 60%,** national development priorities have moved toward **urban governance and municipal reforms.**

B. Functional and Planning Challenges

  - **District and Metropolitan Planning Committees are largely** non-functional in most states.

  - **Poor digital capacity-** Limited use of **e-Gram Swaraj and GIS-based planning.**

C. Administrative Challenges

  - **Shortage of Functionaries-** Severe **staff vacancies-only 0.67 secretaries per Gram Panchayat** (falling to 0.33 in Uttar Pradesh).

  - **Parastatal Dominance** reduces the role of elected bodies. Eg- Delhi Jal Board

  - **Accountability issues-** Weak **social audit mechanisms** and poor grievance redressal.

D. Financial Challenges

  - **Declining Fiscal Autonomy-** Local bodies generate **<1% of GDP** as own-source revenue (RBI, 2022).

  - **Post-GST Revenue Loss-** Subsumption of **octroi, market, and entertainment taxes.**

  - Irregular constitution and poor implementation of **SFC recommendations.**

E. Social and Governance Challenges

  - **Elite Capture-** Local elites dominate decision-making, sidelining marginalized voices.

  - Despite reservation, **proxy representation (Sarpanch Pati)** reduces women’s leadership effectiveness.

**Way Forward**

- **Empower through Performance Grants** linked to service outcomes (as per 15th Finance Commission).

- **Regular Capacity Building** via State Institutes of Rural/Urban Development.

- **Digitization of Panchayats (e-GramSwaraj)** for transparency and efficiency.

- **Operationalize DPCs/MPCs** for integrated local planning.

- Adopting Best Practices - Eg- Kerala’s People’s Plan Campaign grants local bodies control over **40% of the state’s plan budget.**

Local bodies are the **cornerstones of participatory democracy** and good governance. Their **functional capacity and autonomy** are essential for the constitutional **vision of decentralization.**

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q51-civilsdaily]

## Question 52 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q52-civilsdaily**

**“The reservation of seats for women in the institutions of local self- government has had a limited impact on the patriarchal character of the Indian Political Process.” Comment.** [Year: 2019] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

73rd and 74th CAA are the embodiment of **grass-root democracy** and **democratic decentralization** in India.

**Women Reservation**

1. Article 243D - 33% Reservation for women

2. 21 States and 2 UTs have 50% reservation for women in Panchyats.

3. Overall Women’s Representation at **46.44% Positive Impact on Women’s Political Participation**

1. **Increased Representation-** Over **14 lakh women representatives** currently serve in local bodies (MoPR, 2024).

2. **Leadership Development-** Eg- **Sulata Deo** started her career as Sarpanch and became MP from Odisha

3. **Enhanced Service Delivery-** Women-led Panchayats prioritize **water, sanitation, education, and health** issues. Eg- **Chhavi Rajawat (Rajasthan)**

4. **Social Change Catalysts** by **challenging gender stereotypes** in public decision-making.

5. Women leaders have strengthened **Self-Help Groups (SHGs)** and **livelihood initiatives,** promoting inclusive development. Eg- **Bina Devi (“Mushroom Mahila”) Persistent Patriarchal Barriers in Women’s Political Participation at the Local Level Political Barriers**

1. **Proxy Representation-** The **“Sarpanch Pati” phenomenon** - husbands or male relatives exercise real power.

2. **Political parties rarely nominate women** beyond the mandated quota, restricting vertical mobility in politics.

3. **Tokenism-** Representation often **confined to fulfilling quotas** rather than genuine empowerment or participation in governance.

**Institutional Barriers**

4. **Institutional and Bureaucratic Dominance undermine elected women’s authority,** especially in resource allocation and project execution.

5. **Limited Decision-Making Power- exclusion from key committees** or budgetary discussions.

**Economic Barriers**

6. **Economic Dependence on male family members** limits independence in political and developmental decisions.

**Social barriers**

7. Women representatives face **verbal abuse, threats, and social ostracism,** especially when asserting authority.

8. **Cultural and Social Norms-** restrict women’s mobility and participation in public life. Eg- purdah system.

9. Lack of **education and political training** weakens women’s administrative confidence and negotiation skills.

10. **Intersectional Barriers-** Women from **SC/ST and minority communities** face **additional layer of discrimination.**

**Way Forward**

1. Mandate **political parties** to allot a fixed **percentage of tickets to women** beyond local bodies to ensure vertical mobility in politics.

2. **Mentorship and role model initiatives** led by successful women sarpanchs to inspire grassroots participation.

3. **Capacity Building-** Establish State Institutes of Rural Development (SIRDs) as nodal agencies for PRI training.

4. **Manishankar Aiyar Committee recommendations.**

- Create **Panchayat Ombudsman** for grievance redressal.

- Adopt **activity mapping** for clear delineation of **3Fs - Funds, Functions, Functionaries.**

“I measure the progress of a community by the degree of progress which women have achieved.” - **Ambedkar** True empowerment lies in **transforming participation into power,** ensuring women are **policy-shapers, not placeholders.**

**Elections**

## Question 53 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q53-civilsdaily**

**On what grounds a people’s representative can be disqualified under the Representation of People Act, 1951? Also mention the remedies available to such person against his disqualification. [Year: 2019] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **RPA, 1951** provides the **statutory framework** for conducting elections and maintaining the integrity of India’s democratic process.

**Grounds for Disqualification under RPA, 1951**

**(a) Disqualification on Conviction for Certain Offences - Section 8**

- **bribery, undue influence, promoting enmity (IPC 153A, 295A),** or **corruption.**

- If the sentence is **two years or more,** the disqualification is **immediate and lasts six years after release.**

- **Lily Thomas v. Union of India (2013)** - Section 8(4) was **struck down,** making disqualification **automatic on conviction.**

**(b) Disqualification for Dismissal from Government Service - Section 9 -** Disqualification for **five years** if dismissed for **corruption or disloyalty to the State.**

**(c) Disqualification for Office of Profit - Section 10 -** Holding an **office of profit under the government** (not exempted by law)

**(d) Disqualification for Government Contracts - Section 9A**

**(e) Disqualification for Failure to Lodge Election Expenses - Section 10A Remedies Available Against Disqualification**

**(a) Judicial Appeal - If conviction is stayed,** disqualification **ceases to operate** (Lok Prahari v. Union of India, 2018).

**(b)** Under **Articles 103 & 192, President or Governor’s Decision** acts **on the advice of the Election Commission.**

**(c) Election Petition (Section 80, RPA, 1951) in the High Court** within **45 days** of result declaration.

**Analysis**

| Issues | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| Delay in conviction and appeals (6%<br>Conviction) | Fast-track courts (SC directive, 2014). |
| Low Deterrence (46% Criminals) | Bar candidates at charge-sheet stage for serious<br>offences (Law Commission). |
| Office of profit ambiguity | Frame comprehensive definition and clear exemptions<br>by Parliament. |

Thus, the disqualification provisions ensure the **trinity of Ethics, Accountability and Fairness** of electoral process.

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q53-civilsdaily]

## Question 54 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q54-civilsdaily**

**“There is a need for simplification of procedure for disqualification of persons found guilty of corrupt practices under the Representation of peoples Act” Comment [Year: 2020] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **RPA, 1951** provides the **statutory framework** for conducting elections and maintaining the integrity of India’s democratic process.

**Procedure of Disqualification**

**1.** Under **Articles 103 & 192, President or Governor’s Decision** acts **on the advice of the Election Commission.**

**2. Election Petition (Section 80, RPA, 1951) in the High Court** within **45 days** of result declaration.

Appeal to SC within **30 Days**

| Need | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Lengthy and multi-tier process (HC<br>→ EC → President) | Empower Election Commission to decide<br>disqualification directly, with limited judicial review. |
| 2. Delay in disposal of election petitions | Set a statutory time limit (6 months) for disposal of<br>cases (Law Commission 244th Report). |
| 3. Risk of political influence in<br>President/Governor’s decision | Make ECI’s opinion binding to ensure neutrality. |
| 4. Weak deterrence (46%<br>Criminalisation) | Allow immediate suspension upon conviction or prima<br>facie finding. |
| 5. Ambiguity in law on corrupt practices | Clarify definitions and strengthen Section 123 through<br>legislative amendment. |
| 6. Low conviction rate (6%) | Establish Special Election Tribunals for speedy trial of<br>election offences. (SC) |
| 7. Judicial backlog in High Courts<br>(more than 5Cr cases pending) | Transfer cases to designated fast-track benches or<br>election tribunals. |

**Positive Impacts of Simplification**

1. **Enhances Public Trust**

2. **Improves Integrity of the Electoral Process**

3. **Strengthens Rule of Law**

4. **Promotes Ethical Politics**

5. **Upholds Constitutional Morality** Thus, simplification of disqualification provisions are needed to ensure the **trinity of Ethics, Accountability and Fairness** of electoral process.

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q54-civilsdaily]

## Question 55 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q55-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the ‘corrupt practices’ for the purpose of the Representation of the People**

**Act, 1951. Analyze whether the increase in the assets of the legislators and/or their**

**associates, disproportionate to their known sources of income, would constitute ‘undue**

**influence’ and consequently a corrupt practice. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Representation of the People Act, 1951** defines a set of **“corrupt practices”** (Section 123), violation of which can lead to **disqualification of a candidate (Section 8).**

**Corrupt Practices under RPA, 1951**

1. **Bribery** - Offering or accepting gratification (money, jobs, gifts, entertainment) to induce candidature, withdrawal, or voting/refraining from voting. Eg- Distribution of cash or liquor during elections.

2. **Undue Influence** - Interference (direct or indirect) with the free exercise of electoral rights through threats, inducements, or pressure. Eg- Threats of social boycott or misuse of official authority.

3. **Appeal on Identity Grounds** - Seeking votes based on **religion, caste, community, race, or language,** or using religious/national symbols.

4. **Promotion of Enmity** - Spreading hatred between groups for electoral gains.

5. **False Statements** - Publishing knowingly false information about a candidate’s **personal character or conduct** to prejudice election prospects.

6. SC in 2023 held that providing false information about electoral Candidates Qualification is not a **Corrupt Practice** under **RPA, 1951.**

7. **Free Conveyance of Voters** - Hiring vehicles/vessels for voters’ free transport to polling stations.

8. **Excessive Expenditure** - Spending beyond limits prescribed under **Section 77.**

9. **Misuse of Official Machinery** - Seeking help from government servants such as **gazetted officers, judges, police, armed forces.**

10. **Booth Capturing** - Seizing polling stations or ballot boxes by force.

11. **Glorification of Sati or Regressive Practices** - Using such appeals for electoral advantage.

**Assets of Legislators and the Question of Undue Influence**

1. Since 2004, under the **Representation of the People Act, 1951,** MPs must declare their **assets and liabilities** to the **Speaker (Lok Sabha)** or **Chairman (Rajya Sabha)** within **90 days of taking their seat.**

2. The **Supreme Court,** in **Lok Prahari case,** mandated that candidates disclose not only their **assets** but also the **sources of income** of themselves, their spouses, and dependents.

3. It directed the **Centre to amend election rules and nomination forms,** and sought a **permanent mechanism** to probe disproportionate asset growth of legislators.

4. The Court held that **non-disclosure of assets or income sources amounts to a corrupt practice** under **Section 123, RPA 1951.**

5. A legislator’s or associate’s **increase in assets disproportionate to known income** is also a matter of **corruption under Prevention of Corruption Act (1988)** or **criminal misconduct under IPC/Prevention of Money Laundering Act. Section 8 (3)** of the Act states that if an MP or MLA is convicted for any other crime and is sent to **jail** for **2 years or more,** he/ she will be disqualified for **6 years** from the time of release.

Thus, the disqualification provisions ensure the **trinity of Ethics, Accountability and Fairness** of electoral process.

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q55-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 56 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q56-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the procedures to decide the disputes arising out of the election of a Member of the Parliament or State Legislature under The Representation of the People Act, 1951.**

**What are the grounds on which the election of any returned candidate may be declared void?**

**What remedy is available to the aggrieved party against the decision ? Refer to the case**

**laws. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Article 329(b)** bars judicial intervention in elections except through an **election petition** under the **Representation**

**of People Act, 1951.** Part VI (Sections 80-122) governs adjudication of election disputes

**Procedure to decide election disputes**

**1. Election Petition (Section 80) -** No civil suit; only election petition can challenge an election.

**2. Who Can File (Section 81) -** Any **candidate** or **elector** of the constituency may file petition.

**3. Time Limit (Section 81) -** Filed within **45 days** from date of declaration of result.

**4. Jurisdiction (Section 80A) -** Heard by **single judge of the High Court** designated by Chief Justice.

**5. Time-Bound Disposal (Section 86(7)) -** Petition to be disposed of within **6 months** as far as practicable.

**Grounds for declaring election void (section 100)**

**1. Disqualification of Returned Candidate (Section 100(1)(a)) -** Candidate not qualified or disqualified under Constitution/RPA on election date.

**2. Corrupt Practices (Section 100(1)(b)) -** Bribery, undue influence, religious appeals, false statements, booth capturing, expenditure violations (Section 123).

**3. Improper Rejection of Nomination:** If a nomination was wrongfully rejected.

## **4. The result was "materially affected" by**

- Improper acceptance of any nomination.

- Improper reception, refusal, or rejection of any vote.

- Non-compliance with the provisions of the Constitution or the RPA, 1951.

**Remedy against decision**

**1. Direct Appeal to Supreme Court (Section 116A)** within **30 days.**

**2.** Supreme Court may **stay** High Court's order under stringent conditions.

**Landmark case laws**

**1. Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain (1975) -** Allahabad HC set aside PM's election for corrupt practices; led to Emergency.

**2. N.P. Ponnuswami v. Returning Officer (1952) -** Established **Article 329(b) as absolute bar;** election petition is sole remedy.

**3. Mohinder Singh Gill v. CEC (1978) - Justice Krishna Iyer:** Free and fair elections part of basic structure.

**4. Lily Thomas v. UoI (2013) -** Struck down Section 8(4) RPA; **automatic disqualification on conviction** with 2+ years.

**5. Abhiram Singh v. C.D. Commachen (2017) -** Constitution Bench broadened Section 123(3) - appeal to religion, caste, race by any party is corrupt practice.

Thus, the disqualification provisions ensure the **trinity of Ethics, Accountability and Fairness** of electoral process.

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q56-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 57 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q57-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the role of the Election Commission of India in the light of the evolution of the Model Code of Conduct [Year: 2022]**

### Answer

. 15 Model Code of Conduct** is the guidelines set by Election Commission of India for political entities during campaigns to ensure decorum. Though **not legally enforceable,** it has become an **indispensable moral instrument** for ensuring **free, fair, and level-playing elections** in India.

**Evolution of the Model Code of Conduct (MCC)**

1. **1960 -** First **voluntary code** introduced during the **Kerala Assembly elections.**

2. **1962 - First Nationwide Use** in the **Lok Sabha elections**

3. **1991 -** ECI under **T.N. Seshan formalised and reissued MCC** with **7 comprehensive parts**

4. **2013 - Supreme Court Recognition** in **S. Subramaniam Balaji v. Tamil Nadu**

5. **2018-Present -** MCC expanded to cover **social media, fake news, and online campaigning.**

**Role of the Election Commission of India in MCC Implementation**

1. **Acts under Constitutional Authority (Art. 324)-** Exercises **residual powers** to enforce MCC even in the absence of explicit statutory provisions.

2. **Ensures Free and Fair Elections-** ECI enforces MCC to maintain a **level playing field** among all political parties.

3. Restricts the **ruling party** from announcing new schemes, transferring officials, or using public resources for campaigns.

4. **Monitors Campaign Conduct-** Prohibits **hate speech, communal appeals, caste-based propaganda, and personal attacks** in campaigns.

5. **Acts Against MCC Violations-** Issues **warnings, censures, derecognition, or suspension** of political leaders and parties for violations.

6. Uses **Media Certification and Monitoring Committees (MCMC)** to monitor **paid news and political advertisements.**

7. **Controls Government Communication-** Prohibits use of **official media or public funds** for election-related publicity.

8. Deploys **observers and flying squads** to detect and address MCC violations promptly.

9. **Engages Citizens in Monitoring-** Introduced the **cVIGIL mobile app** allowing citizens to report MCC violations in real time.

10. **Coordinates with Law Enforcement-** Works with **district magistrates, police, and election observers** to ensure compliance on the ground.

**Challenges before the Election Commission in Enforcing MCC**

**A. Legal and Institutional Challenges**

  - **Lack of Statutory Backing-** enforcement depends on **moral authority,** not legal sanction.

  - **Delay in Judicial Remedies** on MCC violations, reduce **deterrent effect.**

**B. Administrative Challenges**

  - Monitoring compliance across **10+ lakh polling stations** and multiple states is logistically difficult.

  - **Limited Human Resources-** reliance on **district officials** who are part of state bureaucracy reduces independence.

**C. Political Challenges**

  - **Allegations of Bias,** especially against opposition leaders.

  - **Non-cooperation by Political Parties- ignore advisories** or **delay compliance** with MCC orders.

  - **Freebies and Populism-** Lack of clear guidelines to differentiate **welfare schemes** from **electoral freebies** (as seen in Tamil Nadu & Telangana cases).

**D. Technological and Media Challenges -** Difficulty tracking **fake news, hate campaigns, and paid promotions** on digital platforms.

**Way Forward**

1. **Give Statutory Backing to MCC** under **Representation of the People Act, 1951**

2. **Regulate Freebies and Populism -** Implement Supreme Court’s advice in **S. Subramaniam Balaji (2013)**

3. Establish a **digital MCC framework** in collaboration with **social media platforms** to tackle misinformation, hate speech, and paid content.

4. **Enhance Transparency and Accountability -** Publish all **MCC violations, ECI actions, and outcomes** on a public dashboard.

5. Set **time limits for inquiry and punishment** in MCC violation cases.

Electoral reforms is considered as **‘Mother of all Reforms’.** Giving statutory backing to MCC can ensure the **trinity of Ethics, Accountability and Fairness** of electoral process.

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q57-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 58 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q58-civilsdaily**

**Examine the need for electoral reforms as suggested by various committees with particular reference to “one nation - one election” principle. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**Free and fair elections** are the **foundation of Indian democracy** (Article 324). Thus, Electoral reforms are considered as **‘Mother of all Reforms’.**

| Issues in Elections | Committee Recommendations |
| --- | --- |
| Criminalization of Politics - In 2024, 46% of<br>Lok Sabha MPs have criminal cases | Law Commission (244th, 2014)- Disqualify<br>candidates once charges are framed for serious<br>offences.<br>ECI- fast-track courts. |
| Black Money - Between 2016-21, 71% of<br>income of national parties came from<br>unknown sources (ADR). | Indrajit Gupta Committee (1998)- partial state<br>funding |
| High Election Expenditure - 2024 Lok Sabha<br>elections cost ₹1.2 Lakh crore+ | 2nd ARC (2008)- Enforce strict expenditure<br>audits. |
| EVM - Allegations of tampering and bias<br>persist despite VVPAT | SC- Expand VVPAT coverage |

**Need for one nation-one election**

**1.** According to **ECI, ONOE reduces frequency of MCC & ensures policy continuity**

**2. NITI Aayog (2017):** simultaneous polls can cut election-related costs by 4500 Cr due to lower administrative and security deployment, single logistics cycle, and reduced campaign spending.

**3.** Combined elections **reduce voter fatigue** and improve **participation,** especially in urban areas.

**4. Stability -** Aligning Centre-State electoral cycles, making governments **more accountable and stable**

**5.** ONOE discourages reckless dissolution of assemblies due to high political cost.

6. Check on **political populism** due to reduced frequency of elections. Eg- farm loan waivers

7. Reduction in **vote bank politics and communalism.** Promote **social cohesion Challenges**

**1. Constitutional Amendments Needed -** Articles **83, 85, 172, 174, 356**

2. **Weak Political Consensus** as regional parties fear loss of visibility.

**3. Synchronisation Issues -** Assemblies dissolved mid-term would require **caretaker governments,** raising constitutional questions.

**4. Logistical Requirements -** Twice the number of **EVMs & VVPATs,** enhanced storage facilities, and large-scale training.

As suggested by the Parliamentary **Standing Committee (2015),** there is a need for wide political consultations.

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q58-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 59 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q59-civilsdaily**

**In the light of recent controversy regarding the use of Electronic Voting Machines**

**(EVM), what are the challenges before the Election Commission of India to ensure the**

**trustworthiness of elections in India? [Year: 2018] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

In **Abhiram Singh vs UoI,** SC has held that elections are the biggest secular and democratic event. **EVMs** were introduced to improve transparency and efficiency of the electoral process.

**Fact File**

1. **1982** - **First experimental use** in **Parur constituency, Kerala**

**2. 2004 -** First nationwide use of EVMs **Challenges before the Election Commission of India (ECI)**

A. Technical and Operational Challenges

  - **Allegations of EVM tampering** and demand for Postal Ballot system.

  - EVMs are produced by PSU’s **BEL and ECIL-** concerns about **source code security** and potential government misuse.

  - Large-scale deployment increases risk of **technical failure,** power supply issues, and **storage vulnerability.**

B. Institutional and Procedural Challenges

  - Political polarisation has led to **accusations of bias** against ECI decisions (timing of elections, model code enforcement).

  - **Lack of transparency-** Limited access for political parties and civil society to **audit or inspect EVM functioning.**

  - Opposition parties demand **100% VVPAT verification or return to ballot papers,** which the ECI has resisted citing **impracticality.**

C. Legal and Regulatory Challenges

  - Use of EVMs is governed by **Conduct of Elections Rules, 1961,** not by a **specific EVM legislation.**

  - **Judicial scrutiny and legal disputes-** Repeated petitions challenge **credibility of EVMs and VVPAT counting procedures.**

  - **Despite SC directives** (2024) to improve **VVPAT counting transparency** and **preserve paper trail records** - compliance remains partial.

D. Public Perception and Political Trust Deficit

  - **Decline in citizen confidence-** Lack of understanding of EVM functioning leads to **misinformation and conspiracy theories.**

  - **Political blame culture-** Losing parties often **question EVM integrity,** politicising the institution’s credibility.

  - **Social media misinformation-** Viral false claims about **EVM manipulation** erode voter trust. Eg“Black Box” Allegations (2024) **ECI’s Stand**

- **EVMs are standalone, non-networked machines;** cannot be hacked remotely.

- Rigorous **mock polling, sealing, randomisation, and observer monitoring** prevent manipulation.

- **Technical Expert Committee (TEC)** from IITs and DRDO regularly reviews EVM integrity.

**Way Forward**

**1.** Conduct **independent third-party audits** (IITs, NIC) of EVM software and random samples.

**2. VVPAT Verification -** Increase **sample size** or audit **entire constituency** in disputed cases.

**3.** Launch **voter education campaigns** explaining EVM and VVPAT mechanisms

**4.** Enact a **dedicated “Electronic Voting Regulation Act”** ensuring independent oversight **Free and fair elections** are the cornerstone of democracy (Art. 324). Enhancing the **transparency** in use of EVM’s is thus essential.

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q59-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 60 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q60-civilsdaily**

**While the national political parties in India favour centralisation, the regional parties are in favour of State autonomy.” Comment. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

According to Edmund Burke, “Political parties are a group of people sharing common ideology and coming together for the pursuit of common interest.” In India’s quasi-federal system, political orientation of parties influences the federal balance.

**National Political Parties - Tilt Towards Centralisation**

1. Political Reasons

1. **Ideological focus on unity and integrity** - Eg- Emphasis on **“One Nation”** narrative

2. **National security concerns** - Strong Centre seen as vital for sovereignty. Eg- AFSPA

3. **High-command party culture** - Central leaders dominate over state units.

4. **Uniform electoral strategy** - Centralised messaging ensures consistency.

2. Institutional Reasons

1. **Constitutional centralism** - Support for Centre’s powers under **Articles 249, 356, and 352.**

2. **Governor’s role** - Used as a **central instrument** in state politics.

3. Economic Reasons

1. **Fiscal centralisation** - Centre controls major taxes and revenue flows.

2. **Centralised planning legacy** - Planning Commission model favoured top-down control.

3. **Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS)** - Used for visibility and control (e.g., PM-KISAN).

4. **Uniform economic policy** - Eg- GST.

4. Social & Cultural Reasons

1. **Nation-building agenda** - Eg- Ek Bharat, Sreshtha Bharat

2. **Integration of diversity** - Central schemes for social cohesion and inclusion. Eg- One Nation, One Ration Card

3. **Promotion of national language/symbols** - Eg- Three Language Formula **Regional Political Parties - Favour State Autonomy**

1. Political Reasons

1. **Rooted in regional identity movements-** Eg - DMK (Tamil identity), TMC (Bengal pride).

2. **Opposition to central interference-** Resist misuse of **Article 356** and Governor’s powers.

2. Institutional Reasons

1. **Representation in central bodies-** Seek **stronger role for states** in institutions like **Finance Commission and NITI Aayog.**

2. **Push for cooperative federalism-** Eg- Anandpur Sahib Resolution on centre-state relations

3. Economic Reasons

1. **Fiscal autonomy-** Demand greater **tax devolution** and freedom in fund usage.

2. **Resource control-** Want more authority over **natural resources and industries.**

3. **Discontent over CSS-** Oppose **Centrally Sponsored Schemes** that restrict flexibility.

4. Social & Cultural Reasons

1. **Language and cultural protection-** Eg - DMK’s anti-Hindi protests.

2. **Address local inequalities-** Focus on **state-specific social justice** and caste realities.

“Federalism is not a monolith; it is a dialogue between self-rule and shared rule.” It must rest on the principles of **autonomy, adequacy,** and **elasticity.**

**Constitutional and Statutory Bodies**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q60-civilsdaily]

## Question 61 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q61-civilsdaily**

**Multiplicity of various commissions for the vulnerable sections of the society leads to problems of overlapping jurisdiction and duplication of functions. Is it better to merge all**

**commissions into an umbrella Human Rights Commission? Argue your case. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

“The true measure of a democracy is how it treats its weakest members.” - **Mahatma Gandhi** The Indian state has established a network of **statutory and constitutional commissions** to **uphold equality, dignity, and justice** as envisioned in **Articles 14-17, 21, and 46** of the Constitution.

**Problems of Overlapping Jurisdiction**

1. **Ambiguity in Mandate - Example-** Hathras case - NCSC & NCW both intervened.

2. **Parallel Investigations - Example-** Bilkis Bano case - NHRC, NCW, and NCM all investigated.

3. **Lack of Coordination** leads to contradictory recommendations.

4. **Confusion in Accountability -** Citizens unclear which body to approach for redressal.

**Problems of Duplication of Functions**

**1. Repetitive Reporting**

**2. Fragmented Data Systems -** Separate data collection on similar issues creates inconsistency.

**3. Resource Wastage -** Each commission maintains its own secretariat, inquiry cells, and legal divisions, leading to inefficiency.

**4. Overlapping Policy Recommendations,** causing delay and duplication.

**Merging of all commissions into an umbrella HR Commission**

**Arguments in Favour**

1. **Uniformity-** Brings consistency in standards, inquiry, and reporting across all vulnerable groups.

2. **Resource Efficiency-** Prevents duplication of staff, budgets, and administrative infrastructure.

3. **Accountability-** Establishes a single point of responsibility for human rights protection.

4. Removes confusion and brings **Clarity of Jurisdiction**

5. **Intersectional Approach-** Enables holistic handling of overlapping vulnerabilities like caste, gender, and religion.

6. **Global Best Practice-** Mirrors models in Canada, Australia, and New Zealand with unified human rights bodies.

7. **Data Integration-** Allows centralized data collection and analysis for evidence-based policymaking.

8. **Policy Coherence-** Facilitates unified engagement with ministries and state bodies for coordinated action.

**Policy Making Stage**

- Ensures **uniform policies** for all vulnerable groups.

- Promotes **integrated approach** to overlapping issues like caste and gender.

- Brings **policy coherence** by coordinating with all ministries and states.

- Builds **centralized research and data** for evidence-based decisions.

- Aligns with **global human rights models** (Canada, Australia, New Zealand).

**Implementation Stage**

- Provides **clarity of jurisdiction** and avoids overlaps in cases.

- Improves **use of resources** through shared staff and infrastructure.

- Enables **faster grievance redressal** through a single window system.

- Promotes **better coordination** among departments and agencies.

- Strengthens **capacity building** and awareness under one framework.

**Monitoring and Evaluation Stage**

- Creates **single accountability channel** for Parliament and public.

- Ensures **uniform data and indicators** for human rights tracking.

- Makes **evaluation simpler** with one national reporting mechanism.

- Builds **greater public trust** through one credible institution.

**Arguments Against**

5. **Specialization Loss-** Dilutes expertise of domain-specific bodies like NCW, NCSC, and NCPCR.

6. **Constitutional Violation-** Conflicts with Articles 338, 338A, and 338B granting separate constitutional status.

7. **Administrative Overload-** A single mega body may become slow, inefficient, and unresponsive.

8. **Representation Loss-** Marginalized groups lose dedicated institutional voices for advocacy.

9. **Risk of Bureaucratization** of commission

10. **Symbolic Dilution-** Weakens the government’s visible commitment to vulnerable sections.

11. **Better Coordination Alternative-** Inter-commission coordination can address overlaps without structural merger.

**Way Forward**

1. Create an **Inter-Commission Coordination Council** chaired by NHRC to avoid overlaps.

2. Establish a **common digital platform** for complaints, investigations, and monitoring.

3. **Joint Investigations and Reports** in intersectional cases (e.g., caste + gender).

4. Amend relevant Acts to **clearly demarcate jurisdiction.**

5. Simplify public interface through a **single online grievance portal** linked to all commissions.

The **3R approach of Reform, Reorientation and Restructuring** can enhance functioning of commissions as an effective **Bulwark Of Democracy** in India.

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q61-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 62 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q62-civilsdaily**

**Which steps are required for constitutionalization of a commission? Do you think imparting constitutionality to the National Commission for Women would ensure greater**

**gender justice and empowerment in India? Give reasons. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

“The true measure of a democracy is how it treats its weakest members.” - **Mahatma Gandhi** The Indian state has established a network of **statutory and constitutional commissions** to **uphold equality, dignity, and justice** as envisioned in **Articles 14-17, 21, and 46** of the Constitution.

**Steps Required for Constitutionalization of a Commission**

1. **Constitutional Amendment Bill** under **Article 368** to insert a new article providing for the commission’s structure, powers, and functions.

2. **Parliamentary Approval-** The bill must be passed by a **special majority** - i.e., a two-thirds majority of members present and voting, and a majority of the total membership of each House.

3. After parliamentary passage, the bill must receive **assent of the President of India.**

4. The amendment must be **notified in the Gazette** and necessary **rules and procedures** must be framed for operationalization.

**Would Constitutional Status to NCW Ensure Greater Gender Justice and Empowerment?**

**Arguments in Favour**

1. **Enhanced Autonomy and Authority- greater independence** from executive control and political interference.

2. Its reports and directives would carry **greater legal and moral weight,** improving government accountability.

3. Funding and staffing could be constitutionally guaranteed, reducing dependence on yearly budgetary allocations.

4. **Symbolic Empowerment-** signify **strong political commitment** to women’s rights and gender equality.

5. Similar bodies like **NCSC (Art. 338)** and **NCST (Art. 338A)** already enjoy constitutional status; extending the same to women ensures **institutional parity.**

6. **Better Enforcement Mechanism-** Constitutional backing can ensure **stronger monitoring** of gender-related laws (e.g., Domestic Violence Act, Sexual Harassment Act).

7. **Improved Coordination-** With constitutional status, NCW could act as a **nodal authority** linking state commissions and ministries on gender issues.

**Arguments Against**

1. Constitutionalization won’t automatically improve outcomes unless **implementation capacity and political will** increase. Eg- non-binding recommendations

2. **Risk of politicization and executive interference** in appointments like **NHRC**

3. **Existing Powers Underutilized-** limited **investigative capacity and enforcement follow-up.**

4. **Risk of Bureaucratization-** Constitutional status may make the body more formal.

5. **Institutional issues** like vacancies, limited funding and delays in appointments may persist.

**Way Forward**

6. Create an **Inter-Commission Coordination Council** chaired by NHRC to avoid overlaps.

7. Strengthen NCW’s **investigative and enforcement powers** within the existing legal framework.

8. Ensure **time-bound government response** to NCW recommendations.

9. Provide **financial and staffing autonomy** through statutory amendment.

10. Simplify public interface through a **single online grievance portal** linked to all commissions.

The **3R approach of Reform, Reorientation and Restructuring** can enhance functioning of NCW as an effective **Bulwark Of Democracy** in India.

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q62-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 63 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q63-civilsdaily**

**Whether the National Commission for Scheduled Castes (NCSC) can enforce the implementation of constitutional reservation for the Scheduled Castes in the religious**

**minority institutions? Examine. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **NCSC,** established under **Article 338,** is a **constitutional body** mandated to safeguard the interests and rights of Scheduled Castes (SCs).

**Constitutional and Legal Position on reservation for the SC in the religious minority institutions**

1. **Article 15(4) & 16(4)-** Empower the State to make **special provisions** for socially and educationally backward classes, including SCs.

2. **Article 30(1)-** Grants **minority communities** the right to establish and administer educational institutions of their choice.

3. **Article 338(5)-** Authorizes NCSC to **investigate, monitor, and report** on safeguards provided for SCs.

4. **Constitution (Scheduled Castes) Order, 1950-** Restricts SC status to **Hindus, Sikhs, and Buddhists,** excluding Muslims and Christians.

**Key Judicial Pronouncements**

1. **T.M.A. Pai Foundation v. State of Karnataka (2002)-** Minority institutions have the **right to autonomy in administration** under Article 30, though subject to reasonable regulation.

2. **P.A. Inamdar v. State of Maharashtra (2005)-** State cannot impose reservation policy on unaided minority institutions.

**Analysis**

1. The NCSC can **recommend or monitor** steps for reservation in institutions, but it **cannot enforce** such provisions in minority institutions, as-

  - **Article 30(1)** guarantees minorities autonomy in managing their educational institutions.

  - **Judicial precedents** protect minority institutions from mandatory reservation.

  - **NCSC’s powers are recommendatory,** not binding.

2. Furthermore, since **Scheduled Castes of minority religions (Muslims and Christians)** are **not constitutionally recognized as SCs,** NCSC **has no jurisdiction** over them.

3. The matter instead falls under the **National Commission for Minorities (NCM)** or policy domain of the **Parliament,** not NCSC’s enforcement powers.

Any change in this framework requires **constitutional amendment** and judicial validation to balance SC Rights with **Articles 29-30** protections.

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q63-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 64 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q64-civilsdaily**

**“The Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) has a very vital role to play.” Explain how this is reflected in the method and terms of his appointment as well as the range of powers**

**he can exercise. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**CAG** is established under **Article 148 of the Constitution** as the **supreme audit authority** of India. Ambedkar called it as **most important officer** under the Constitution of India

**Importance of the office of CAG**

**1. Guardian of Public Purse -** CAG ensures all government expenditure is sanctioned by law and used appropriately.

**2. Bulwark of Democracy along with ECI and SC**

**3. Promotes Accountability - Eg: 2G Spectrum scam (2010)** - CAG reported ₹1.76 lakh crore presumptive loss.

**4. Strengthens Parliamentary Oversight -** CAG reports form the basis for examination by **Public Accounts Committee (PAC)** and **COPU.**

**5. Ensures Financial Propriety -** Audits whether expenditure follows financial rules, principles, and economy norms.

**6. Auditor of Federal Finances -** Audits accounts of both Union and State Governments under Articles 149-151.

**7. Promotes Good Governance -** Performance audits drive improvements in scheme design, implementation, and outcomes.

**Reflection of importance in method and terms of appointment**

**1. Appointed by the President of India under Article 148(1)** by warrant under his hand and seal.

**2. Oath of Office** under **Third Schedule** to uphold the Constitution. The oath symbolises the solemn responsibility and independence of the office.

**3. Fixed Tenure -** CAG holds office for **6 years or until age 65,** whichever is earlier (Section 3, CAG Act 1971). Fixed tenure shields from executive pressure and ensures continuity.

**4. Removal Same as Supreme Court Judge-** This stringent removal procedure protects CAG from arbitrary action by executive.

**5. Salary Charged on Consolidated Fund,** not voted by Parliament (Article 148(3)).

**6. Service conditions cannot be varied** to CAG's disadvantage during tenure (Article 148(4)).

**7. No Eligibility for Further Government Office Reflection of importance in powers vested**

**1. Comprehensive audit authority** across federal levels. **-** CAG audits all expenditure from **Consolidated Fund of India, Consolidated Funds of States,** and UTs with legislatures.

**2. Audit of Contingency Fund and Public Account -** Ensures complete coverage of government finances.

**3. Audit of Public Sector Undertakings- Eg:** Audits LIC, ONGC, FCI, BHEL, Indian Railways under Companies Act provisions.

**4. Audit of Autonomous Bodies substantially financed by government** (more than ₹1 crore or 75% of revenue). **Eg:** ICAR, CSIR

**5. Performance Audit** evaluating economy, efficiency, effectiveness of schemes. **Eg:** Performance audits on MGNREGA, NHM, PMAY, Swachh Bharat Mission.

**6. CAG** Standardises government accounting practices by prescribing **form in which accounts of Union and States** shall be maintained (Article 150).

**7. Independent Investigation Powers -** CAG can **call for any document, information, books, accounts** from any government office without restriction.

**8. Discretionary Powers - Section 23, CAG (DPC) Act 1971** allows CAG to issue directions on accounting and audit principles.

| Challenges | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Discretionary nature of propriety audits | 1. Empower the CAG with ‘Comptroller<br>powers’ (on the lines of the UK model) |
| 2. CAG’s reports are recommendatory, not enforceable. | Mandatory action-taken reports to Parliament. |
| 3. Post-facto nature of audit, reduce preventive control. | 3. Enforce follow-up on CAG findings and<br>introduce periodic compliance audits |

“The CAG is the conscience-keeper of public finance, ensuring not only lawful but also wise spending.” - **2nd ARC**

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q64-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 65 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q65-civilsdaily**

**“The duty of the Comptroller and Auditor General is not merely to ensure the legality of expenditure but also its propriety.” Comment. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG),** established under **Article 148** of the Constitution, is the **guardian of the public purse.**

While legality focuses on whether expenditure is made according to law, **propriety** examines whether such expenditure is **justified, prudent, and in the public interest.**

**Ensuring Legality of Expenditure**

1. Ensures all expenditures are **authorized by Parliament or State Legislature** through Appropriation Acts.

2. Verifies that funds are drawn from the **Consolidated Fund** only under proper sanction.

3. Checks **compliance with financial rules, codes, and delegated authorities.**

4. Prevents **unauthorized re-appropriation or excess expenditure** beyond approved limits.

5. Eg- CAG’s audit of **defence expenditure.**

**Ensuring Propriety of Expenditure**

- Examines whether expenditure is **necessary, justifiable, and in the public interest** - **Ensures Responsible Use of Public Funds**

- **Prevents Misuse -** Evaluates **waste, extravagance, or favouritism** even if legally permissible.

- Strengthens **spirit of financial responsibility -** Questions whether spending ensures **value for money** and meets **ethical standards** of governance.

- Promotes a **culture of fiscal morality and prudence** in the use of public funds.

- **Strengthens Parliamentary Control and oversight** with insights on **ethical and prudent financial management Examples of Propriety Concerns Highlighted by CAG**

- **2G Spectrum Allocation (2010)**

- **Commonwealth Games (2010)**

- **Coal Block Allocations (2012)-** Identified **procedural irregularities** and favoritism

| Challenges | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Discretionary nature of propriety audits | 1. Empower the CAG with ‘Comptroller powers’<br>(on the lines of the UK model) |
| 2. CAG’s reports are recommendatory, not<br>enforceable. | Mandatory action-taken reports to Parliament. |
| 3. Post-facto nature of audit, reduce<br>preventive control. | 3. Enforce follow-up on CAG findings and<br>introduce periodic compliance audits |

“The CAG is the conscience-keeper of public finance, ensuring not only lawful but also wise spending.” - **2nd ARC**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q65-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 66 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q66-civilsdaily**

**How is the Finance Commission of India constituted? What do you know about the terms of reference of the recently constituted Finance Commission? Discuss. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **Finance Commission of India (FC),** established under **Article 280 of the Constitution,** is a **quasi-judicial body.** It plays a vital role in maintaining the **fiscal federal balance** envisioned by the Constitution.

**Constitution of the Finance Commission**

1. **Constitutional Provision-** As per **Article 280(1),** the **President of India** constitutes the Finance Commission every **five years** or earlier if necessary.

2. **Composition-**

  - Consists of a **Chairperson** and **four other members,** appointed by the President.

  - Their **qualifications and terms of service** are determined by the **Finance Commission (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act, 1951.**

3. **Tenure-** The Commission usually has a tenure of **five years,** extendable by Presidential order.

**Terms of Reference (ToR) of the Recently Constituted Finance Commission**

The **16th Finance Commission** was constituted in **December 2023,** chaired by **Dr. Arvind Panagariya.**

Its recommendations will apply for the **period 2026-2031.**

1. Division of Tax Proceeds

- Recommend the **distribution of net tax proceeds** between the **Union and the States** under **Chapter I, Part XII** of the Constitution.

- Determine the **allocation of shares among individual States** from the total divisible pool of central taxes.

2. Principles for Grants-in-Aid

- Lay down the **principles governing grants-in-aid** to States from the **Consolidated Fund of India.**

- Recommend the **amounts to be provided** to States as grants under **Article 275,** for purposes other than those specified in the provisos to clause (1).

3. Enhancing State Funds for Local Bodies

- Suggest measures to **increase the Consolidated Fund of States** to supplement resources of **Panchayats and Municipalities.**

- Base these recommendations on the **inputs of respective State Finance Commissions,** ensuring fiscal strengthening of local governance.

4. Evaluation of Disaster Management Financing

- Review existing **funding mechanisms for Disaster Management,** particularly those created under the **Disaster Management Act, 2005.**

- Recommend improvements in the structure, sharing pattern, and utilization of **National and State Disaster Response Funds.**

5. Any Other Matters Referred by the President

- Examine and recommend on additional fiscal issues to ensure “sound finance,” including fiscal consolidation, resource augmentation, and expenditure efficiency.**

**Challenges Ahead**

1. **Data Gaps and Quality Issues-** Relies on outdated and inconsistent official data, affecting fiscal assessments.

2. **Political Factors-** Faces pressure to balance conflicting interests of Centre, States, and political stakeholders.

3. **Overlap with GST Council-** GST decisions affect revenue flows and reduce FC’s fiscal autonomy.

4. **Limited Role in Local Governance-** Depends on weak or irregular State Finance Commissions for data and recommendations.

5. FCs have **no enforcement power,** as their recommendations are **advisory** in nature.

**Way Forward**

1. Ensuring **state representation** in decision making

2. **Permanence-** Make FC a permanent body for continuous review and coordination. (Rajamannar Committee)

3. **Addressing Emerging Issues and Challenges -** Factor in **digital transformation and green financing** for sustainable growth.

4. Build **data-driven analytical capacity** with reliable and updated fiscal databases.

The Finance Commission remains the **keystone of India’s fiscal federalism,** ensuring both **equity and efficiency** in resource distribution.

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q66-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 67 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q67-civilsdaily**

**“Recent amendments to the Right to information Act will have profound impact on the autonomy and independence of the Information Commission”. Discuss [Year: 2020] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Right to Information (RTI) Act, 2005** is a cornerstone of **transparent and accountable governance** in India.

The **RTI (Amendment) Act, 2019** introduced changes to the service conditions of the **CIC** and **ICs.**

**Fact File**

1. Over 2.5 crore (25 million) RTI applications have been filed since 2005 **Amendments to the RTI Act, 2019**

1. The **tenure** of the CIC and ICs (earlier fixed at 5 years) is now **to be determined by the Central Government.**

2. The **salaries, allowances, and other service conditions** of CICs and ICs are also **subject to executive notification,** replacing the earlier parity with Election Commissioners.

3. The **status and equivalence** of the CIC and ICs with constitutional authorities like the Election Commission have been removed.

**Impact on Autonomy and Independence**

1. **Executive Control Over Tenure,** undermines **security of office** and increasing **executive dependence.**

2. The original status equal to the **Election Commission** is withdrawn, reducing the Commission’s **symbolic and functional autonomy.**

3. **Threat to Federal Autonomy-** Centre’s control over **State Information Commissions’ service conditions.**

4. **Chilling Effect on Decision-Making-** Fear of punitive transfers or reduced tenure can deter **bold and impartial rulings** against powerful authorities.

5. Perceived loss of independence can **erode citizens’ confidence** in the Commission as a neutral watchdog.

6. Compromised autonomy weakens enforcement of the **right to information,** curbing transparency and accountability.

**Other issues (Satark Nagrik Sangathan Report)**

1. 7 out of 29 Information Commissions were **completely defunct** between **July 2023 and June 2024**

2. **In 2024,** 9 Commissions were functioning **without a Chief Information Commissioner**

3. **Rising Backlogs-** Over **4 lakh appeals and complaints** pending

4. **High Rejection Rate-** The **CIC** returned **42% of appeals/complaints** received

5. Since 2005, only **9% of all Information Commissioners** have been **women Way Forward**

**1. Establish a National Coordination Committee (NCC)** to monitor RTI implementation and ensure uniformity across states.

**2.** Fill **vacancies in Information Commissions** promptly to prevent delays and backlogs.

**3.** Engage **information management experts** for proper classification, cataloguing, and storage of records.

**4.** Introduce a **separate legal chapter** to protect **RTI applicants and activists** from harassment and retaliation.

Sunlight is the best disinfectant. Thus, **the autonomy of the Information Commissions must be safeguarded** to strengthen Right to Information under Article 19.

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q67-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 68 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q68-civilsdaily**

**Though the Human Rights Commissions have contributed immensely to the protection of human rights in India, yet they have failed to assert themselves against the mighty and**

**powerful. Analyzing their structural and practical limitations, suggest remedial measures. [Year: 2021] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

NHRC is a **statutory body,** established under the **Protection of Human Rights act 1993.** It is established in conformity with **Paris Principles 1991.**

**Role Played by Human Rights Commissions in India**

**1. Protection of Life and Liberty- Eg-** NHRC intervention in **Manipur fake encounter cases (2013)** led to a Supreme Court-monitored CBI probe.

2. **Ensuring Accountability of State Machinery- Eg-** NHRC recommended **Rs. 10 lakh compensation** to victims of the **Hashimpura massacre** (2015).

**3. Protection of Vulnerable Groups- Eg-** NHRC’s recommendations against **bonded labour** led to several state-level rehabilitation programmes.

**4. Monitoring Custodial and Institutional Conditions- Eg-** NHRC report on **Muzaffarpur shelter home (2018)** exposed severe human rights violations.

**5. Promoting Human Rights Awareness** through **training programmes, seminars, and media campaigns**

**6. Policy and Legal Reforms- Eg-** NHRC’s recommendations on **prevention of manual scavenging** and rehabilitation.

**7. Addressing Emerging Human Rights Issues - Eg-** NHRC issued advisories on **migrant workers’ rights during COVID-19 lockdown (2020).**

**8. Collaboration with International and National Agencies - Eg- India’s Universal Periodic Review (UPR)** reports before the **UNHRC.**

**Issues Associated with NHRC**

**In 2024,** the **GANHRI** deferred the **accreditation of the NHRC** for the second year in a row.

**1. Statutory Limitations on Jurisdiction -** NHRC **cannot take up cases older than one year** or matters **pending before a court (sub judice).**

2. Cases involving military personnel often **fall outside the NHRC’s purview**

**3.** The NHRC lacks the authority to punish violators, **cannot directly impose** penalties or award relief to victims.

**4. Observations by the Sub-Committee on Accreditation (SCA) of GANHRI**

  - **Lack of Diversity -** Only **95 out of 393 staff positions** are held by women, violating the **Paris Principles** requirement of pluralism and representativeness.

**b. Government-Dominated Selection Committee curbs opposition input** and reduces transparency in selection.

**c. Weak Investigation Mechanism -** NHRC **depends on police and government officials on deputation** for investigation.

**d. Limited Engagement with Civil Society**

**5. Additional Institutional Challenges**

  - **Vacancies and Delays** in appointments reduce capacity and response efficiency.

  - **Financial Dependence-** Budgetary allocations come through the **Ministry of Home Affairs,** affecting autonomy.

  - **Poor Implementation of Recommendations-** NHRC recommendations are **advisory,** often ignored or delayed by governments.

  - **Backlogs-** Growing pendency of complaints due to limited staff and rising caseloads.

**Way Forward**

1. **Strengthen Legal Powers-** Amend the **1993 Act** to make NHRC/SHRC recommendations **legally binding.**

2. **Independent Investigation Wing-** Establish a **separate cadre** of trained human rights investigators.

3. **Expand Jurisdiction-** Review restrictions on cases involving **armed forces** and paramilitary personnel.

4. **Financial Autonomy-** Provide **independent budgetary control,** like constitutional bodies (e.g., EC, CAG).

5. **Empower State Commissions-** Strengthen SHRCs through **funding and capacity-building support.**

6. **Enhance Awareness-** Conduct **public education campaigns** with NGOs and media support.

7. **Regular Review Mechanism-** Form a **National Human Rights Coordination Council** to monitor and evaluate performance.

The **3R approach of Reform, Reorientation and Restructuring** can enhance functioning of NHRC as an effective **Bulwark Of Democracy** in India.

[Question ID: 2021-gs2-q68-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 69 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q69-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the role of the National Commission for Backward Classes in the wake of its transformation from a statutory body to a constitutional body. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **102nd Constitution Amendment Act, 2018** inserted **Article 338B,** elevating the commission to par with the NCSC and NCST.

**Role of the National Commission for Backward Classes**

1. **Investigating Constitutional Safeguards** provided to backward classes under the Constitution.

2. **Exercising Civil Court Powers** to summon officials, demand public records, and examine witnesses during active inquiries.

3. **Inquiry into Rights Violations:** investigates specific citizen grievances regarding the outright denial or subversion of rights belonging to SEBCs.

4. **Advising on Socio-Economic Development:** Formulates policy guidelines and advises both Union and State governments on targeted welfare planning for backward communities.

5. **Evaluating Departmental Progress:** Reviews and audits the grassroots performance and execution speed of central and state-level affirmative action schemes.

6. Presents comprehensive structural **review reports to the President of India** regarding the operational health of welfare frameworks.

7. **The Union and State governments consult** the NCBC on all major policy matters affecting SEBCs.

8. **Enforcing Reservation Compliance:** Monitors and checks public sector recruitment pipelines to guarantee strict adherence to the mandated 27% OBC quotas.

9. Aids the institutional procedure of identifying and reviewing communities for the Central OBC list under **Article 342A.**

**Case Studies**

8. **Enforcing Creamy Layer Compliance in Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs)**

9. The commission played a central key role in implementation of the 27% OBC reservation within the All India Quota for undergraduate and postgraduate medical and dental courses (NEET).

**Major issues**

4. Recommendations are **not binding** on executive

5. **Overlapping jurisdiction** with other bodies like NHRX, NCW etc

6. Lack of administrative and financial **autonomy** Strengthening the NCBC is essential to achieve constitutional objective of **Social Justice**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q69-civilsdaily]

## Question 70 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q70-civilsdaily**

**Discuss India as a secular state and compare it with the secular principles of the US constitution. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Secularism** implies the **separation of religion from the State** and **equal treatment of all faiths.**

While the **Indian Constitution** follows the **Principled Distance Model,** the **US Constitution** establishes a **strict wall of separation** between religion and the State.

**India as a Secular State**

1. **Constitutional Basis:** Secularism is enshrined in the **Preamble** (added by the 42nd Amendment, 1976) and **Articles 25-28** ensuring **freedom of religion.**

2. **Objective:** To promote **religious harmony and inclusive coexistence** in a multi-faith society.

## **3. Core Features**

  - **Freedom of conscience and religion** (Art. 25).

  - **Right to manage religious affairs** (Art. 26).

  - **Freedom from religious instruction in State institutions** (Art. 28).

  - **State neutrality and equal respect** for all religions.

4. **Positive Secularism:** The State can **intervene in religion** to uphold **social reform and equality-e.g., Abolition of untouchability (Art. 17), Temple Entry Acts, S.R. Bommai (1994)** upheld secularism as a **basic feature.**

**Similarities with US secularism**

1. **Constitutional Guarantee: India - Articles 25-28, US - First Amendment (1791).**

2. **No State Religion:** Neither India nor the US recognizes any **official State religion.**

3. **Religious Freedom:** Citizens enjoy **freedom of conscience, belief, and worship** in both countries.

4. **Equality Before Law:** Both uphold **non-discrimination on religious grounds** and **equal protection under law** (India - Art. 14; US - 14th Amendment).

5. **Judicial Enforcement:** The **Supreme Courts** in both countries act as **guardians of secular principles** (S.R.

Bommai, Everson v. Board).

6. **Protection from Religious Domination:** Both prevent **majoritarian religious influence** over State policy.

7. **Democratic Pluralism:** Both promote **tolerance, coexistence, and religious pluralism** as part of democratic ethos.

**Differences**

| Aspect | India | United States |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nature of<br>Secularism | Positive secularism - equal respect for all<br>religions. | Negative secularism - strict<br>separation of Church and State. |
| State-Religion<br>Relationship | Principled distance; State can intervene<br>for reform (e.g., temple entry, abolition of<br>untouchability). | Complete separation; State<br>cannot interfere in religious<br>matters. |
| Constitutional<br>Basis | Preamble (42nd Amendment) and Arts.<br>25-28. | First Amendment to the US<br>Constitution. |
| State’s Role | Active and reformative - ensures equality<br>among religions. | Passive and neutral - avoids<br>religious involvement. |
| Scope of Freedom | Protects collective and individual religious<br>rights. | Focuses on individual religious<br>liberty. |
| Judicial Doctrine | S.R. Bommai (1994) - secularism is a basic<br>feature. | Everson v. Board (1947) - “wall<br>of separation” principle. |
| Objective | Social harmony and religious equality. | Individual freedom of<br>conscience. |
| Societal Context | Evolved in a multi-religious and plural<br>society. | Evolved in a predominantly<br>Christian but liberal society. |

Both India and the US uphold **freedom of religion,** but their approaches differ-India’s **secularism is inclusive and reformative,** suited to its **plural society,** while the **US model is exclusionary and separationist,** reflecting its **individualist liberal tradition.**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q70-civilsdaily]
