## Question 71 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q71-civilsdaily**

**Compare and contrast the President’s power to pardon in India and in the USA. Are there any limits to it in both countries? What are ‘preemptive pardons’? [Year: 2025] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The power to pardon is an essential **sovereign function,** allowing the Head of State to grant clemency and show mercy, in the interest of justice, humanity, or public welfare.

U.S. President **Joe Biden** issued an unconditional pardon to his son, who was convicted of federal tax violations and gun charges.

**Difference**

| Aspect | President of India | President of the United States |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Constitutional Basis | Article 72 of the Indian Constitution | Article II, Section 2 of the U.S.<br>Constitution |
| Scope of Power | Offences under Union law,<br>court-martial, and death sentences | Includes all federal offences, except<br>in impeachment |
| Death Penalty | The President can pardon death<br>sentences, including those by the<br>states. | The President can pardon only<br>federal death sentences and not for<br>state crimes. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Court-Martial | Explicitly includes power over<br>court-martial sentences | No express provision; mostly<br>excluded |
| Advisory Role | Exercises power on the advice of<br>the Council of Ministers (Maru Ram<br>Case) | Discretionary; acts independently |
| Judicial Interpretation | In Kehar Singh v. Union of India<br>(1989), the Supreme Court held that<br>the President’s power is broad but<br>not absolute; it can be judicially<br>reviewed if exercised arbitrarily. | In Ex Parte Garland (1866), the U.S.<br>Supreme Court confirmed the<br>absolute nature of the presidential<br>pardon, barring impeachment<br>cases. |

**However, there are a few similarities**

| Aspect | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Executive Nature | Both powers are part of the Executive functions under the<br>Constitution. |
| Objective | To correct judicial errors |
| Constitutional Provision | Both powers are explicitly provided in their respective Constitutions. |
| Types of Clemency | Both can grant pardon, reprieve, respite, remission, and commutation. |
| Death Sentence | Both can pardon death sentences under their jurisdiction. |

**Preemptive Pardons**

8. Pardons granted **before conviction or even before formal charges are filed,** protecting from future prosecution.

9. **Eg- President Gerald Ford’s 1974 pardon of Richard Nixon** in the Watergate scandal.

While the power to pardon is vital as a **safety valve of justice,** it must be exercised with **restraint, transparency, and caution.**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q71-civilsdaily]

## Question 72 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q72-civilsdaily**

**What can France learn from the Indian Constitution’s approach to secularism? [Year: 2019] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**Secularism** ensures the **neutrality of the State toward religion** while protecting **freedom of belief and**

**conscience.**

While France follows **Laïcité,** emphasizing **strict separation of religion and State,** India practices **positive secularism,** ensuring **equal respect for all religions** within a plural society.

**France’s Model of Secularism (Laïcité)**

- Based on **non-recognition and non-involvement** of the State in religious affairs.

- Guarantees **freedom of conscience** but **restricts public religious expression** (e.g., veil ban in schools, 2004).

- Aims to preserve **republican unity and neutrality** in the public sphere.

**India’s Model of Secularism**

9. Enshrined in the **Preamble** and **Articles 25-28** of the Constitution.

10. Guarantees **freedom of religion, equal treatment of all faiths,** and **State neutrality.**

11. Allows **State intervention for social reform** (e.g., abolition of untouchability, Sabarimala judgment).

12. Described as **positive secularism** - promoting harmony through engagement, not exclusion.

13. Upheld as a **basic feature** of the Constitution in S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994).

**Lessons France Can Learn from Indian Secularism**

- **Inclusive Neutrality:** The State can respect all religions equally rather than excluding them from the public sphere.

- **Reform-Oriented State Role:** The State can **intervene in religious practices** to uphold human rights and gender equality (e.g., abolition of untouchability, temple entry)

- **Multiculturalism -** respecting cultural and religious rights and customs. Promoting plurality instead of uniformity

- **Freedom of Religious Expression:** Allowing individuals to **express faith in public spaces** strengthens personal liberty (e.g., Sikh turban, Muslim veil).

- **Pluralism as Democratic Strength:** Religious diversity, when accommodated constitutionally, enhances **national unity and democratic legitimacy.**

- **Balancing Liberty with Equality:** Secularism should protect **individual conscience** while also advancing **social justice** and **non-discrimination.**

- **Dialogue over Restriction:** Promoting **inter-faith dialogue and accommodation** builds social cohesion better than prohibitive policies.

- **Protection of minority rights** through special provisions. Eg- Article 29,30.

Secularism need not mean **“absence of religion in public life,”** but rather **“equal respect and peaceful coexistence of all religions under a neutral State.”**

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q72-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 73 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q73-civilsdaily**

**Critically examine the procedures through which the Presidents of India and France are elected. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **President of India** serves as the **constitutional head of the State** and the **symbol of national unity** within a **parliamentary democracy.**

The **President of France,** under the **Fifth Republic (1958),** is both the **head of State** and a **key executive authority** in a **semi-presidential system,** sharing power with the Prime Minister.

Their **election procedures** reflect the differing nature of their political systems.

**Procedure for Election of the President of India**

1. **Constitutional Basis: Articles 54** of the Constitution.

## **2. Electoral College**

  - Elected members of **Parliament** (Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha).

  - Elected members of **State Legislative Assemblies** (including Delhi and Puducherry).

## **3. Voting System**

  - **Proportional representation by single transferable vote (STV)** and **secret ballot** (Article 55).

  - **Vote value** of MLAs and MPs ensures **federal balance.**

4. **Eligibility:** Indian citizen, **35 years or above,** qualified for Lok Sabha membership.

5. **Term: Five years,** eligible for re-election.

6. Significance

  - Indirect election is relevant as president is nominal head

  - Federal Balance maintained

  - Only elected members participate in elections **Procedure for Election of the President of France**

1. **Constitutional Basis: Article 6** of the **French Constitution (1958).**

2. **Mode: Direct universal suffrage** since **1962,** following de Gaulle’s reform.

3. **Voting System: Two-round majority system:** A candidate must secure **50%+ votes** in the first round; otherwise, a **runoff between top two** in the second round.

4. **Term: Five years** (reduced from seven in 2000).

5. **Eligibility:** French citizen, **18 years or above,** meeting civic and nomination requirements.

6. Significance

  - **Accountability** - Direct election is in line with executive powers of president

  - **President** enjoys absolute majority

  - **Stability** due to security of tenure **Criticism of Procedure for Election of the President of India**

1. **Indirect election** limits **popular legitimacy.**

2. **Value of votes** of state legislators differs as it is based on population

3. **Symbolic** authority-President’s office often reduced to formality under parliamentary control.

**Criticism of Procedure for Election of the President of France**

1. **Personality-based campaigns** overshadow policy debates.

2. **Cohabitation** risk-President and Prime Minister from different parties may create policy deadlock.

3. **Over-centralization of power** in the executive, especially under strong presidents.

The **Indian procedure** ensures **federal balance and political neutrality** through indirect election, while the **French model** ensures **democratic legitimacy** through direct popular choice.

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q73-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 74 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q74-civilsdaily**

**The judicial systems in India and UK seem to be converging as well as diverging in recent times. Highlight the key points of convergence and divergence between the two**

**nations in terms of their judicial practices. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

India and the United Kingdom share a **common-law heritage,** where judicial institutions evolved from British colonial foundations.

**Points of Convergence**

1. **Common Law Tradition:** Both follow the **adversarial system,** rely on **precedents,** and uphold **judicial reasoning** as binding (stare decisis).

2. **Judicial Independence:** Guaranteed through **security of tenure, financial autonomy,** and **appointment systems** (Art. 124-147; Constitutional Reform Act, 2005).

## **3. Human Rights and Constitutional Values**

  - India: Expansion of **Article 21** to include right to dignity, privacy (Puttaswamy, 2017).

  - UK: **Human Rights Act (1998)** incorporates **European Convention on Human Rights (ECHR)** into domestic law.

4. **Judicial Accountability and Transparency:** Open court proceedings, **reasoned judgments,** and live streaming of constitutional benches (India, 2023).

5. **Judicial Activism:** Both judiciaries increasingly intervene in governance and rights issues (Kesavananda Bharati, R (Miller) 2017 on Brexit).

6. **Technological Modernization:** Adoption of **e-courts, virtual hearings, and AI-assisted case management** in both systems.

**Points of Divergence**

1. **Constitutional Framework:** India has a **written Constitution** establishing **judicial supremacy;** UK follows **parliamentary sovereignty** and **unwritten conventions.**

2. **Judicial Review Power:** Indian courts can **strike down laws** violating the Constitution (Kesavananda Bharati); UK courts can only **issue declarations of incompatibility.**

3. **Hierarchy and Structure:** India has a **single integrated judiciary;** UK has **distinct legal systems** for England, Scotland, and Northern Ireland.

4. **Appointment Mechanism:** India uses the **Collegium system;** UK uses the **Judicial Appointments Commission** with statutory oversight.

5. **Public Interest Litigation (PIL):** India practices **broad locus standi** for social justice (S.P. Gupta); UK maintains **restrictive standing** for judicial review.

6. **Binding Nature of Precedent:** In India, **Supreme Court decisions bind all** (Art. 141); in UK, **Parliamentary sovereignty** can override judicial interpretation.

7. **Judicial Activism vs. Restraint:** Indian judiciary is **transformative and interventionist;** UK judiciary is **cautious and precedent-bound.**

8. **Basic Structure Doctrine:** Unique to India-courts can protect the **basic structure** of the Constitution; no such principle exists in the UK.

While **India’s judiciary functions as a constitutional guardian,** the **UK judiciary operates under parliamentary sovereignty,** reflecting two evolving but interconnected models of democratic justice.

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q74-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 75 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q75-civilsdaily**

**Compare and contrast the British and Indian approaches to Parliamentary sovereignty [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

.**

**10**

**Parliamentary sovereignty** is a fundamental principle of the **British constitutional system,** signifying that Parliament is the **supreme legal authority.** In India, **Parliament’s power is limited by constitutional supremacy and judicial review.**

**Similarities**

1. **Representative Authority:** In both countries, **Parliament represents the will of the people** and is the **central law-making body.**

2. **Supremacy in Legislative Function:** Both Parliaments have **broad legislative powers** to make, amend, or repeal laws.

3. **Parliamentary Privileges:** Members in both enjoy **freedom of speech and immunity** within legislative proceedings.

4. **Rule of Law Framework:** Both function under the **rule of law,** ensuring legislation aligns with legal principles.

5. **Bicameral Legislature:** Each has a **two-house system** (UK: Commons and Lords; India: Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha).

| Aspect | Britain | India |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nature of<br>Sovereignty | Absolute parliamentary sovereignty -<br>Parliament can make or unmake any law. | Limited parliamentary sovereignty<br>- constrained by the Constitution. |
| Source of<br>Authority | Parliament is supreme and sovereign. | Parliament is created and limited by<br>the Constitution. |
| Judicial Review | Not permitted - courts cannot invalidate<br>Acts of Parliament. | Permitted - courts can strike down<br>unconstitutional laws (Kesavananda<br>Bharati). |
| Written<br>Constitution | Unwritten and based on conventions. | Written with explicit separation of<br>powers. |
| Amendment<br>Power | Unlimited - no constitutional restriction. | Limited - subject to basic structure<br>doctrine. |
| Constraints on<br>Power | Only political constraints (public opinion,<br>elections). | Constitutional and judicial<br>constraints. |
| Role of<br>Judiciary | Subordinate to Parliament. | Co-equal guardian of the<br>Constitution. |

While both systems share a **parliamentary foundation and democratic ethos,** the **UK upholds parliamentary supremacy,** whereas **India enshrines constitutional supremacy**

**Governance**

**Important Aspects of Governance**

## Question 76 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q76-civilsdaily**

**Discuss the role of the Competition Commission of India in containing the abuse of dominant position by the Multi-National Corporations in India. Refer to the recent decisions [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

.**

**10**

The **Competition Commission of India (CCI),** established under the **Competition Act, 2002,** is the chief regulatory body to promote and sustain **fair competition** in markets.

**Role of CCI in Containing Abuse of Dominant Position by MNCs**

**1. Enforcement of Competition Act, 2002 0** It can initiate investigations suo motu or based on complaints from consumers or firms.

**2. Regulation of Mergers and Acquisitions** involving MNCs to ensure they do not lead to **market monopolisation** or restrict competition.

**3. Investigation and Monitoring of market practices.** It can ask the Director **General (DG)** for investigation into dominant firms. Eg- investigation against e-Commerce companies

4. **Asian Paints Case (2024):** CCI ordered an investigation into **exclusionary practices** that restricted competition in the decorative paints market.

**5. Imposition of Penalties** on firms found guilty of abusing dominance. Eg- **Google was** fined **₹1,337 crore** for abusing dominance in the **Android mobile ecosystem**

**6.** Conducts **awareness campaigns** to inform consumers about their rights **Challenges**

- **Global Nature of MNCs:** Difficult to regulate cross-border conduct and global digital platforms.

- **Proof Burden:** Difficult to establish anti-competitive “effects.”

- **Lengthy Litigation:** MNCs challenge orders in courts, delaying enforcement.

- **Jurisdictional Conflicts:** Overlaps with data and consumer protection authorities like TRAI **Way Forward**

- Build **economic and digital expertise** in CCI and DG offices.

- Enhance **international cooperation** with global antitrust regulators.

- Ensure **faster adjudication** and reduce judicial delays.

- Update the **Competition Act** to handle **AI and platform dominance.**

The CCI has emerged as a **key pillar of India’s economic governance,** aligning with the vision of **Viksit Bharat**

**2047.**

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q76-civilsdaily]

## Question 77 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q77-civilsdaily**

**The Citizen’s Charter is an ideal instrument of organisational transparency and accountability, but it has its own limitations. Identify the limitations and suggest measures**

**for greater effectiveness of the Citizen’s Charters. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **Citizen’s Charter,** introduced in India in **1997 (DARPG)** following the **UK model,** aims to make public services **transparent, accountable, and citizen-centric** by clearly defining service standards, timelines, and grievance redressal mechanisms.

## **Core Principles of the Citizen’s Charter**

1. **Quality**

2. **Choice**

3. **Standards**

4. **Value**

5. **Accountability**

6. **Transparency Role in Ensuring Transparency and Accountability**

1. **Defines Service Standards - Eg-** Passport Seva Kendra specifies delivery within **3 working days** for Tatkal applications.

2. **Enhances Administrative Transparency -** Makes **procedures, responsibilities, and timelines public,** reducing scope for arbitrariness and discretion.

3. **Promotes Accountability of Officials -** Identifies **responsible officers** for each service and grievance redressal, ensuring **answerability for delays or failures.**

4. **Empowers Citizens** to **demand better service delivery,** question inefficiencies, and seek **grievance redressal** through defined channels.

5. Builds **mutual expectations** between government and citizens, enhancing trust in public institutions.

6. Provides a **benchmark for assessing departmental efficiency** and monitoring service outcomes through periodic audits.

7. Promotes **feedback-based improvement** by institutionalizing **citizen input** in service reforms.

**Limitations of Citizen’s Charter**

1. Charters are **non-statutory** and lack **penal provisions** for non-compliance, reducing accountability.

2. **Top Down Approach** - Charters are **formulated by bureaucrats** without citizen consultation, making them **non-representative** and **unrealistic.**

3. **Low Public Awareness - Eg-** only **36% of users** knew of service standards **(NITI Aayog, 2023).**

4. **Weak Grievance Redressal -** Absence of **clear escalation mechanisms** leads to **ineffective resolution** and loss of citizen confidence.

5. **Bureaucratic Resistance -** Many departments treat the Charter as a **procedural formality** rather than a reform instrument.

6. **Poor Review and Monitoring -** Charters are **rarely updated** or evaluated; lack of **performance metrics** weakens impact.

7. **Fragmented Implementation -** No **uniform structure or standards** across ministries and states, causing inconsistency in service quality.

8. **Accessibility issues** due to absence of regional language versions **Measures for Greater Effectiveness**

1. Enact a **Citizen’s Charter Act** with **penalties for non-compliance,** similar to **Right to Public Services Acts** in MP and Bihar.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p84_img68.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

2. Involve **civil society, user groups, and local bodies** in designing realistic and demand-driven service standards.

3. **Integration with Grievance Systems -** Link Charters with **CPGRAMS** and **State Service Guarantee Acts** for **real-time grievance tracking.**

4. **Regular Review and Evaluation -** Institutionalize **annual audits** and **third-party evaluations** to assess compliance and update service commitments.

5. Conduct **public awareness campaigns** and train officials under the **Sevottam Framework** for **citizen-oriented delivery.**

6. **Technological Integration -** Promote **digital dashboards, online service tracking,** and **data-based performance monitoring.**

7. **Performance Incentives and Accountability -** Introduce **reward mechanisms** for compliant departments and **penalties** for persistent failures.

By aligning it with the **2nd ARC recommendations,** it can evolve from a symbolic commitment to a **practical framework for responsive, transparent, and citizen-centric governance.**

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q77-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 78 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q78-civilsdaily**

**The Citizens’ charter has been a landmark initiative in ensuring citizen-centric administration. But it is yet to reach its full potential. Identify the factors hindering the**

**realisation of its promise and suggest measures to overcome them. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

Originating in the **United Kingdom (1991),** India adopted Citizens' Charters in **1997** to ensure administrative accountability.

**Importance in Citizen-Centric Administration**

1. **Empowers Citizens:** Transforms passive recipients into active stakeholders. **Eg:** Rights-based service delivery.

2. **Enhances Accountability:** Holds specific officials responsible for service failures.

3. **Promotes Transparency:** Clearly lists available services and required documents.

4. **Standardizes Quality:** Establishes a uniform "benchmark" for public services. **Eg:** ISO certification.

5. **Grievance Redressal:** Provides a structured mechanism for reporting dissatisfaction.

6. **Fosters Trust:** Bridges the gap between the public and government through feedback loops.

7. **Time-Bound Delivery:** Commits administration to strict deadlines. **Eg:** Passport issuance.

8. **Value for Money:** Ensures taxpayers receive efficient services for their taxes.

**Factors Hindering Realisation of its Promise**

1. **Vague Standards:** Promises are often worded ambiguously rather than using quantifiable benchmarks. **Eg:**

"Prompt service."

2. **Lack of Legal Teeth:** Voluntary nature means no penalties exist for violating charter commitments. **Eg:**

Unenforceable timelines.

3. **Top-Down Approach:** Centralized drafting ignores local ground-level constraints and resource availability.

**Eg:** Uniform rural-urban charters.

4. **Resistance to Change:** Bureaucratic inertia prevents officials from adopting a service-oriented mindset. **Eg:**

Ignoring citizen feedback.

5. **Inadequate Infrastructure:** Offices often lack the funds or manpower to meet promised standards.

6. **Poor Publicity:** Awareness is often restricted to a small notice board inside the office.

7. **Static Nature:** Most documents do not adapt to technological changes or evolving needs. **Eg:** Outdated manual forms.

8. **Siloed Operations:** Focuses on individual departments rather than the citizen’s "end-to-end" journey.

9. **Language Barrier:** Charters are often not available in regional or vernacular languages.

10. **Lack of Audits:** Departments rarely publish independent evaluations of their adherence to standards. **Eg:**

Missing annual reports.

11. **Stagnant Updates:** A DARPG audit found several central charters haven't been updated in over **5 years.**

12. **Poor Grievance Disposal:** Satisfaction rates for grievance redressal in many states hover below **40%.**

**Successful Examples of Citizen Charters in India**

1. **Passport Seva Kendra:** clear timelines, digital tracking, and professional service environment

2. **Indian Railways:** compensation, cleanliness standards, and punctuality targets.

3. **Taxpayers’ Charter:** Defines rights like privacy and fair treatment.

4. **National Health Mission:** Charters in government hospitals outlining free medicines and diagnostic services for the poor.

**Measures to Overcome Challenges**

1. **Statutory Backing:** Legally mandate service delivery through "Right to Services" Acts. **Eg:** MP Lok Sewa Act.

2. **Adopt Sevottam:** Implement the 2nd ARC-recommended Sevottam model for quality management. **Eg:** IS 15700:2005 certification.

3. **Decentralized Drafting:** Allow local units to customize charters based on specific resources.

4. **Technological Integration -** Promote **digital dashboards, online service tracking,** and **data-based performance monitoring. Eg:** Sakala portal, Karnataka.

5. **Stakeholder Consultation:** Conduct "Jan Sunwais" (public hearings) to update charters based on feedback.

6. **Independent Monitoring:** Employ third-party NGOs to evaluate departmental performance annually. **Eg:**

Quality Council of India audits.

7. **Capacity Building:** Train frontline staff in technical aspects of service delivery through Mission Karmayogi.

8. **Awareness:** Use social media and local languages to educate citizens. **Eg:** "Jago Grahak Jago" style ads.

By aligning it with the **2nd ARC recommendations,** it can evolve from a symbolic commitment to a **practical framework for responsive, transparent, and citizen-centric governance.**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q78-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 79 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q79-civilsdaily**

**What are the aims and objects of recently passed and enforced, The Public**

**Examination (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024? Whether University/State Education**

**Board examinations, too, are covered under the Act ? [Year: 2024] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

.**

The **Public Examination (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024,** was enacted to curb widespread **malpractices, paper leaks, and organised cheating** in recruitment and entrance examinations conducted by central authorities.

**Fact File**

**1. India saw 65 exam paper leaks since 2019**

  - Major Paper Leaks

  - National Eligibility cum Entrance Test (NEET)-UG 2024

  - Indian Army’s Common Entrance Examination for recruiting soldiers in 2021,

  - Central Teachers’ Eligibility Test (CTET) 2023,

  - NEET-UG 2021

  - Joint Entrance Examination (JEE) Mains 2021.

**Aims and Objectives of the Act**

1. **Provide clear legal framework** to deal with issue of paper leaks

2. **Defining Unfair Practices -** Includes **leakage of question papers, tampering of answer sheets, manipulation of computer systems,** and **collusion with officials or service providers.**

3. **Empowering Examination Authorities -** Allows **surprise checks, blacklisting of service providers,** and **enhanced oversight** mechanisms.

4. Authorises the **Centre to refer cases** to **Central Investigation Agencies** for probing large-scale malpractices.

5. Enables setting up of **special courts** for **time-bound adjudication** of offences.

6. **Stringent Punishments -** Prescribes **3-5 years of imprisonment** and fines up to **₹10 lakh,** extendable for organised crimes, to **restore public confidence.**

7. **Addressing Organised Examination Crimes -** Provides for **property attachment** and prosecution of institutions involved in organised cheating networks.

8. **Deterring Unfair Means -** Prevents **cheating, impersonation, and paper leaks,** ensuring **transparency, fairness, and credibility** in examinations.

**Significance**

**1. Ensuring Integrity of Public Examinations**

**2. Deterring Organised Malpractices**

**3. Protecting Merit and Equal Opportunity**

**4. Enhancing Accountability of Examination Authorities**

**5. Promoting Transparency and Trust**

**6. Strengthening Governance and Fair Recruitment Coverage under the Act**

- The Act applies to **“Public Examinations”** conducted by authorities listed in its **Schedule** or **notified later by the Central Government.**

## **● The Schedule presently includes**

  - **Union Public Service Commission (UPSC)**

  - **Staff Selection Commission (SSC)**

  - **Railway Recruitment Boards (RRBs)**

  - **Institute of Banking Personnel Selection (IBPS)**

  - **National Testing Agency (NTA)** and other **Central Government recruitment bodies.**

- **University and State Education Board examinations** are **not automatically covered** unless **specifically notified** by the **Central Government** under Section 2 of the Act.

**Limitations of the Act**

1. **Limited Coverage - State Boards and Universities** are **not automatically covered.**

2. **No Dedicated Investigative Mechanism -** Investigation left to **DSP-level officers**

3. **Technology Gaps -** Insufficient use of **digital tracking, encryption, and data protection tools** to prevent leaks.

4. **Ambiguity in Defining “Unfair Means” -** The broad wording may lead to **subjective interpretation** and misuse.

5. Heavy reliance on **Central Government notification powers** may limit federal flexibility.

6. Focuses mainly on **punitive measures,** not on **systemic prevention and capacity-building.**

7. **Judicial Burden -** Creation of **special courts** without adequate infrastructure may lead to **delays in trial.**

8. **Training Deficit -** Lack of **awareness and training** among invigilators and exam staff undermines effective implementation.

The **Act** represents a **major reform** to uphold the **integrity, fairness, and accountability** of India’s examination system.

**Civil Services**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q79-civilsdaily]

## Question 80 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q80-civilsdaily**

**“Institutional quality is a crucial driver of economic performance”. In this context suggest reforms in the Civil Service for strengthening democracy. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**Civil services,** as the steel frame and core institution of governance, plays a decisive role in **policy formulation,**

**implementation, and Economic growth.**

**World Bank (2024)** notes that countries with **higher governance indicators** show **20-25% higher**

**per capita income growth.**

| Importance of Institutional Quality | Major Challenges |
| --- | --- |
| Predictable Governance | Policy Uncertainty. Eg- policy paralysis during UPS 2 |
| Efficient Resource Use | Corruption (Vohra Committee) |
| Policy Continuity | Political Interference |
| Transparency and Accountability | Secrecy and Opaqueness. Eg- Poor implementation of<br>social audits |
| Rule of Law and Contract<br>Enforcement | Judicial Delays. Eg- >5Cr pending cases |
| Ease of Doing Business | Regulatory Overlaps |
| Fiscal Discipline | Populist Spending. Eg- Revdi Culture |
| Attracting investment | Red Tapism. Eg- environmental clearances |

**Reforms in Civil Service to Strengthen Democracy**

**Political Reforms**

- **Political Neutrality:** Ensure **insulation from political interference** through Civil Services Boards as per **T.S.R. Subramanian Case (2013).**

- **Transparency in Appointments:** Adopt **merit-based and open selection** for key administrative posts.

- **Continuity in Tenure:** Fix **minimum tenure** for key field officers to ensure **policy stability and accountability.**

- **Decentralisation of Power:** Strengthen **PRIs and ULBs** by devolving **funds, functions, and functionaries,** promoting participatory democracy.

**Institutional Reforms**

- **Capacity Building:** Implement **Mission Karmayogi** for continuous learning, digital skills, and behavioural transformation.

- **Performance Management:** Introduce **outcome-based appraisals** and digital tools like **SPARROW.**

- **Code of Ethics:** Institutionalise a **Civil Services Code of Ethics** promoting integrity, impartiality, and empathy.

- **Ethical Auditing:** Conduct **periodic integrity audits** through initiatives like **Mission Satyanistha** (Indian Railways).

- **Reform Commission:** Establish a **Permanent Civil Service Reform Commission** for policy evaluation and innovation.

**Economic Reforms**

- **Efficiency and Cost Optimisation:** Encourage **outsourcing of non-core functions** for better utilisation of resources.

- **Technology Integration:** Use **AI, CPGRAMS, and e-Office** for efficient, transparent, and real-time service delivery.

**Social Reforms**

- **Citizen-Centric Governance:** Implement **Citizen’s Charters** and **Right to Service Acts** to ensure time-bound service delivery.

- **Public Participation:** Enhance **citizen engagement platforms** such as **Prashasan Gaon Ke Sang Abhiyan (Rajasthan).**

- **Value Orientation:** Reinforce **integrity, empathy, compassion, and public service motivation** among officers.

- **Inclusivity:** Promote **gender and social diversity** in recruitment and leadership positions.

A reformed service structure is essential for fulfilling the **2nd ARC’s vision of a proactive, accountable, and responsive administration.**

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q80-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 81 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q81-civilsdaily**

**The Doctrine of Democratic Governance makes it necessary that the public perception of the integrity and commitment of civil servants becomes absolutely positive.**

**Discuss. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Doctrine of Democratic Governance** implies that **governance derives its legitimacy from the will of the people.**

Under this doctrine, **civil servants act as trustees of public interest,** and hence, a **positive public perception** is vital for sustaining **democratic legitimacy.**

**Importance of Integrity and Commitment of Civil Servants**

1. Integrity ensures **credibility of government institutions** and strengthens **citizen confidence** in the administrative system. **Eg-** Swachh Bharat Mission, Beti Bachao Beti Padhao.

2. **Moral Legitimacy of Governance -** A bureaucracy perceived as **honest, impartial, and service-oriented** upholds the **ethical foundation of democracy.**

3. **Efficient Policy Implementation -** Committed civil servants execute programs **objectively and transparently,** reducing **corruption and leakages.** Eg- DBT

4. A professional and non-partisan service ensures **policy continuity** and **institutional resilience** across political transitions.

5. **Social Justice and Inclusivity -** Ethical and empathetic administration safeguards **equity, rights, and participation** of marginalized citizens.

6. **Economic and Institutional Performance -** Integrity-driven governance promotes **predictable policies,** fostering **investment and sustainable development.**

7. **Whole-of-Government Approach:** Encourages **coordination and accountability** across departments. **Eg-** PM gati Shakti Mission.

8. **Participative Democracy:** Strengthens **transparency and accountability** through **RTI, Citizen’s Charter, and Social Audits.**

**Challenges Affecting Public Perception**

1. **Corruption and favouritism.** (Vohra Committee)

2. **Politicisation and arbitrary transfers.**

3. **Lack of transparency** in decision-making processes.

4. **Weak grievance redressal.**

5. **Poor accountability frameworks.**

**Measures to Strengthen Integrity and Public Confidence**

1. Adopt a **Civil Services Code of Ethics** based on **integrity, impartiality, and objectivity** (as recommended by **2nd ARC).**

2. Expand **Mission Karmayogi** to include modules on **public service values, empathy, and accountability.**

3. Ensure **secure tenure** and protect officers from **political pressure** through **Civil Services Boards** (T.S.R.

Subramanian Case, 2013).

4. **Citizen-Centric Accountability -** legal Backing to **Citizen’s Charters,** and **social audits.**

5. **Technological measures - CPGRAMS** and **e-Office** to minimise discretion and enhance accountability An **ethical, efficient, and citizen-centric bureaucracy** upholds the **Doctrine of Democratic Governance,** ensuring that power is exercised with **integrity, fairness, and public trust.**

**E-Governance**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q81-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 82 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q82-civilsdaily**

**E-governance in not only about utilization of the power of new technology, but also much about critical importance of the ‘use value’ of information. Explain. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**The World Bank** defines e-governance as the use by government agencies of information technologies that can transform relations with citizens, businesses, and other arms of government.

**E-Governance as Utilization of the Power of New Technology**

1. **Digital Platforms** like **e-Office, UMANG, DigiLocker, CPGRAMS,** and **GeM.**

2. **Automation and Paperless Processes: Eg- e-Procurement, e-Courts.**

3. **Connectivity and Infrastructure Expansion: Eg- Digital India, BharatNet,** and **5G rollout.**

4. **Data Integration and Interoperability: Eg-** Unified databases such as **Aadhaar** and **SECC.**

5. **Smart Technologies and AI Use: Eg- IndiaAI Mission, Smart Cities.**

6. **Citizen-Government Interface: Eg- Mobile governance** and **MyGov platform.**

**Importance of the ‘Use Value’ of Information**

**Use Value** - The **relevance, accessibility, and usability** of information by citizens, administrators, and policymakers to make informed decisions and ensure accountability.

1. **Informed Decision-Making:** Data-driven policies depend on real-time, accurate information. Eg**Aadhaar-linked welfare, PM-KISAN, JAM Trinity**

2. Platforms like **RTI Portal, NJDG,** and **CPGRAMS** ensure **information availability,** empowering citizens to hold government accountable.

3. **Targeted Service Delivery:** Use of information helps identify beneficiaries and prevent inclusion exclusion errors. Eg- **SECC data.**

4. **Citizen Empowerment:** Information accessibility through **UMANG, DigiLocker,** and **MyGov** enables citizens to interact directly with the state.

5. **Evidence-Based Governance-** Dashboards like **PRAGATI** and **Aspirational District Programme** use information analytics for performance monitoring.

6. **Crisis Management and Responsiveness:** During **COVID-19,** platforms like **CoWIN** and **Aarogya Setu** turned data into real-time governance tools for vaccine delivery and tracking.

7. **Reduction in Asymmetry and Corruption:** Public disclosure reduces discretion and builds public trust. Eg**GeM portal, PFMS**

| Challenges | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| Digital Divide: Only 43% rural households have<br>internet access (NFHS-5, 2021). | Use and expand CSCs and PMGDISHA. |
| Data Quality & Standardization Issues: Eg-<br>Aadhaar-SECC mismatch | Data Integration. Eg- PM Gatishakti<br>platform |
| Privacy Concerns: CoWIN data leaks. | Enforce privacy-by-design under DPDP Act<br>2023. |
| Institutional Capacity Limits. | Capacity Building via Karmayogi iGot. |

**Technology enables e-governance; the use value of information sustains it.**

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q82-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 83 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2020-gs2-q83-civilsdaily**

**“The emergence of Fourth Industrial Revolution (Digital Revolution) has initiated e-Governance as an integral part of government”. Discuss. [Year: 2020] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **Fourth Industrial Revolution (4IR),** marked by **digital technologies, AI, IoT, blockchain, and big data,** is transforming governance, economy, and society. **Klaus Schwab (WEF),** “the Fourth Industrial Revolution is fundamentally changing how governments serve citizens.”

## **Core Pillars of Industry 4.0**

1. **Autonomous Robots**

2. **Augmented Reality**

3. **Cloud Computing**

4. **Internet of Things (IoT)**

5. **System Integration**

6. **Additive Manufacturing (3D Printing)**

7. **Cybersecurity**

8. **Simulation / Digital Twin**

9. **Extensive Data Analysis (Big Data & AI) Digital Revolution Enabling e-Governance**

1. **Data-Driven Governance:** Use of real-time data and analytics for decision-making. Eg- **PRAGATI** Portal.

2. **Efficient Service Delivery: Eg- UMANG, DigiLocker, e-SHRAM, PM-KISAN.**

3. **Administrative Efficiency** through automation and paperless systems. Eg- **e-Office, SPARROW, CPGRAMS.**

4. **Transparency & Accountability:** Eg- **RTI Portal, NJDG, GeM, Open Data Portal.**

5. **Financial Inclusion** through **UPI, DBT, JAM Trinity.** Eg- **491 million UPI users**

6. **Participatory governance** through **MyGov, CPGRAAMS.**

7. **Digital Connectivity** through **Digital India, BharatNet, PM-WANI, 5G rollout.**

8. **Cybersecurity:** Creation of secure cyberspace via **CERT-In** and **National Cyber Security Policy 2023.**

9. **E-Administration: e-Courts, GeM, DigiLocker** replacing manual, paper-based procedures.

10. **Digital Literacy: PMGDISHA** trained **6.3 crore citizens** across 2.5 lakh Gram Panchayats.

11. **AI in Governance: IndiaAI Mission (₹10,371 crore)** promoting AI-based decision-making.

| Challenges | Way Forward |
| --- | --- |
| Digital Divide: Only 43% rural households have<br>internet access (NFHS-5, 2021). | Use and expand CSCs and PMGDISHA. |
| Data Quality & Standardization Issues: Eg-<br>Aadhaar-SECC mismatch | Data Integration. Eg- PM Gatishakti<br>platform |
| Privacy Concerns: CoWIN data leaks. | Enforce privacy-by-design under DPDP Act<br>2023. |
| Institutional Capacity Limits. | Capacity Building via Karmayogi iGot. |
| Lack of Interoperability: Fragmented databases | “Whole-of-Government” model. |

Thus, the future of governance is **data-led, citizen-centric, and digitally empowered.**

[Question ID: 2020-gs2-q83-civilsdaily]

## Question 84 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q84-civilsdaily**

**e-governance is not just about the routine application of digital technology in the service delivery process. It is as much about multifarious interactions for ensuring**

**transparency and accountability. In this context evaluate the role of the ‘Interactive Service**

**Model’ of e-governance. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**The World Bank** defines e-governance as the use by government agencies of information technologies that can transform relations with citizens, businesses, and other arms of government.

**E-Governance - multifarious interactions for ensuring transparency and accountability.**

**Government to Citizen (G2C)**

1. **UMANG, DigiLocker** - Online service access.

2. **CPGRAMS** - Grievance redressal & feedback.

3. **MyGov, Jan Bhagidari** - Citizen participation.

4. **Aadhaar-DBT, PM-KISAN** - Direct benefit transfers.

**Government to Business (G2B)**

- **MCA21, GSTN, NSWS** - Digital compliance & licensing.

- **GeM Portal** - Transparent procurement.

- **Invest India, Ease of Doing Business Dashboard** - Single-window clearance.

**Government to Government (G2G)**

- **PRAGATI, e-Office** - Inter-departmental coordination.

- **PFMS** - Fund tracking & financial accountability.

**Government to Employee (G2E)**

- **SPARROW** - Online performance appraisal.

- **iGOT-Karmayogi** - Capacity building & training.

- **e-Office, HRMS** - Paperless workflow.

**Interactive service model**

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p92_img69.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**UN e-Government Survey (2022)** defines interactive e-services as “a two-way digital interface enabling citizens to engage, transact, and participate in governance.”

**Role of the ‘Interactive Service Model’ of e-Governance**

1. **Citizen-Centric Governance:** Shifts from departmental silos to **integrated, citizen-focused service delivery. Eg-** Sakala (Karnataka) - time-bound, transparent service guarantee.

2. **Bridging Government-Citizen Gap:** through **digital and doorstep access** to services. **Eg-** MPeSeva in Madhya Pradesh.

3. **Participative and Responsive Governance through two-way interaction** between citizens and government. **Eg-** MyGov portal.

4. **Trust-Based Model of Governance** through **transparency and proactive disclosure** of information. **Eg-** Jan Soochna Portal (Rajasthan) - real-time public data access.

5. **Transparency and Accountability in Service Delivery:** Enables **real-time grievance tracking** and **service feedback loops. Eg-** CPGRAMS, UMANG, GeM Portal.

6. Ensures **zero tolerance to corruption** through traceable digital transactions. Eg- DBT has saved **₹3.48 lakh crore**

7. **Empowered and Informed Citizens** to shape governance outcomes. **Eg-** Open Government Data Portal, NDAP.

8. **From Ad hoc to Whole-of-Government Approach:** Promotes integrated and **citizen-centric service delivery.**

**Challenges in Implementing the Interactive Service Model**

1. **Digital Divide:** Only **43% of rural households** have internet access (NFHS-5, 2021), limiting inclusivity.

2. **Digital Literacy:** Merely **10% of rural population** is digitally literate (NSSO data, 2022).

3. **Data Privacy and Security Risks: Eg-** CoWIN data leak.

4. **Institutional Resistance** to adopt **open data and feedback-based systems.**

5. Multiple portals without integration and **interoperability** hinder seamless user experience.

6. **Exclusion and inclusion errors** in digital systems reduce trust in e-services. Eg- authentication errors in **Aadhaar-linked DBT** or **ration delivery.**

7. **Weak Common Service Centre (CSC) Infrastructure: poor connectivity, limited equipment, and untrained staff**

8. **Lack of People-Centric Governance:** Most government websites are **only in English,** not in **vernacular languages,** excluding non-English users.

**Way Ahead**

1. **Strengthening Digital Infrastructure:** Extend **optical fibre connectivity** under **BharatNet** to all **Gram Panchayats.**

2. Promote **citizen digital engagement and training** through **PMGDISHA** and **Digital India** initiatives.

3. **Embedding Digital Literacy in Education:** Integrate **e-literacy programmes** like **e-Kidz** and **IT Clubs** in school curricula.

4. **Business Process Reengineering (2nd ARC):** Simplify procedures, ensure **platform integration,** and promote **seamless inter-departmental coordination.**

5. **Leverage 4th Industrial Revolution Technologies:** Use **AI, IoT, and blockchain** for predictive governance and smart delivery systems.

This can transform e-Governance into a **trust-based, citizen-driven, and participatory governance model.**

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q84-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 85 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q85-civilsdaily**

**Implementation of information and Communication [Year: 2019]**

### Answer

Technology (ICT) based Projects /**

**Programmes usually suffers in terms of certain vital factors. Identify these factors, and**

**suggest measures for their effective implementation.(10)**

ICT in governance refers to the strategic use of information technology to enhance administrative efficiency, transparency, and service accessibility.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p93_img70.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Fact File**

- **DBT** supports over **966 schemes.**

- The government saved more than **₹4.31 lakh crore** through the JAM trinity (2015-March 2024).

- Total broadband subscriptions in India crossed the **100 crore (1 billion)** milestone in late 2025.

- Over **52.5 crore PM Jan Dhan accounts** opened (deposits exceeding ₹2.1 lakh crore.) **Major ICT-Based Projects/Programmes in India**

1. **Digital India:** The umbrella programme for transforming India into a digitally empowered society.

2. **BharatNet:** connected over **2.15 lakh Gram Panchayats** with 42.36 lakh route km of optical fiber.

3. **e-NAM:** A pan-India electronic trading portal for agricultural commodities.

4. **UMANG App:** provide access to 1,500+ central and state government services.

5. **DigiLocker:** cloud-based platform for issuance and verification of digital documents/certificates.

6. **e-Courts:** Aimed at digitizing the Indian judiciary to reduce case pendency and improve access.

7. **U-WIN:** Digital platforms for tracking universal immunization.

8. **ONDC:** Open Network for Digital Commerce, democratizing e-commerce for MSME.

9. **Bhashini: AI-led language translation platform** to overcome linguistic barriers.

**Major Issues in ICT Projects**

1. **Digital Divide - Eg:** Rural internet penetration **59.06%** vs urban **131.45%** (TRAI Q1 2025).

2. **Cybersecurity Vulnerabilities- Eg: ₹22,845 crore** cybercrime losses in 2024 - 206% rise (I4C, MHA 2025).

3. **Data Privacy Concerns -** Weak enforcement of data protection exposes citizens to misuse risks.

4. **Poor Digital Literacy - Eg:** Only **38% women** have ever used internet (NFHS-5, MoHFW).

5. **Inadequate Infrastructure -** Internet connectivity, electricity, devices remain insufficient in remote regions.

6. **Capacity Constraints - lower level** government staff lack training in digital tools, data analytics, AI skills.

7. **Targeting and verification errors** exclude genuine beneficiaries from welfare schemes. **Eg:** PM-Kisan validation errors stranded **2.18 crore families** (Dvara Research).

8. **Departmental Silos -** Ministries operate in isolation; lack integrated digital data ecosystems for governance.

9. **Faulty validation processes. Eg:** Aadhaar-PDS biometric failures linked to Simdega starvation death, Jharkhand (NHRC).

10. **Linguistic Barriers -** English-dominant interfaces exclude regional language users from accessing services.

**Measures for Effective Implementation**

1. **Bridge Digital Divide -** Saturate rural connectivity through BharatNet expansion and affordable smartphones distribution.

2. **Strengthen Cybersecurity Framework -** Implement zero-trust architecture, ICT audits, dedicated CERT-In response teams.

3. **Strengthen DPDP Act 2023 -** Enforce verifiable consent, data minimisation, breach reporting through Data Protection Board.

4. **Mass Digital Literacy Drive -** Scale PMGDISHA and Mission Karmayogi to all citizens and government employees.

5. **Citizen Co-creation -** Engage citizens through MyGov, hackathons, and pilot testing before scaling projects.

6. **Inter-Ministerial Convergence -** Adopt PM Gati Shakti model integrating data across ministries on single GIS platform.

7. **Outcome-Based Monitoring -** Real-time dashboards, third-party audits, beneficiary feedback for course correction.

8. **Public-Private Partnerships -** Engage startups, fintechs, and academia for innovation and last-mile delivery.

9. **Adopt Global Best Practices -** Estonia's X-Road, Once-Only Principle for seamless inter-departmental data sharing.

For Viksit Bharat 2047, robust ICT governance demands inclusion, security, capacity, and citizen-centric design.

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q85-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 86 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q86-civilsdaily**

**Has digital illiteracy, particularly in rural areas, couple with lack of Information and**

**Communication Technology(ICT) accessibility hindered socio-economic development?**

**Examine with justification. [Year: 2021] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

As per **UNDP (2022),** Digital access is now a core dimension of human development. However, **digital illiteracy** and **poor ICT access** have created a **digital divide,** restricting equitable growth.

**Digital illiteracy and accessibility**

1. **Internet Access:** Only **43% of rural households** have internet access (NFHS-5, 2021).

2. **Digital Literacy:** Merely **10% of rural population** is digitally literate (NSSO data, 2022).

3. **Infrastructure Gaps:** More than **35 thousand Gram Panchayats** lack connectivity under **BharatNet.**

4. **Device Ownership:** Less than **15% of rural households** have computers or tablets (NSSO).

5. **Gender Divide:** Only **33% of rural women** have mobile internet access (GSMA Report, 2023).

6. **Exclusion and inclusion errors** in digital systems reduce trust in e-services. Eg- authentication errors in **Aadhaar-linked DBT** or **ration delivery.**

7. **Weak Common Service Centre (CSC) Infrastructure: poor connectivity, limited equipment, and untrained staff**

8. **Lack of People-Centric Governance:** Most government websites are **only in English,** not in **vernacular languages,** excluding non-English users.

**Impact of Digital Illiteracy and Poor ICT Access on**

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p95_img71.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Socio-Economic Development**

**1. Education and Skill Development**

- **Limited online learning:** Only 24% rural students could attend online classes during COVID-19 (ASER 2021).

- **Digital exclusion** restricts access to e-learning platforms like SWAYAM, PMGDISHA, and DIKSHA.

**2. Employment and Livelihoods**

- Rural youth miss digital job opportunities in gig economy and e-commerce.

- Farmers lack access to digital market tools like **e-NAM** or **Kisan Suvidha App.**

**3. Financial Inclusion**

- Inability to use **UPI, digital banking, and DBT systems** limits access to formal finance.

- Rural MSMEs struggle with e-payments and online compliance (GST, MCA21).

**4. Governance and Welfare Access - Eg-** exclusion from **Aadhaar-based DBT** due to authentication errors and poor connectivity.

**5. Health and Social Services -** Lack of ICT prevents use of **telemedicine platforms (eSanjeevani)** and **digital health records.**

**6. Gender and Social Inequality -** Women, SC/ST, and elderly are most excluded due to **low literacy and device ownership.**

**However, there are some Achievements**

1. Expanding Digital Infrastructure

- **BharatNet:** Over **2.14 lakh Gram Panchayats** connected with optical fibre; 97.6% villages have mobile coverage.

- **5G rollout (2022-25):** 4.7 lakh towers covering 99.6% districts.

2. Massive Digital Empowerment

- **PMGDISHA:** Trained over **6.3 crore citizens** in digital literacy.

- **Common Service Centres (5.3 lakh)** serve as ICT hubs in 2.5 lakh Gram Panchayats.

3. Financial and Payment Revolution

- **UPI:** Handles **85% of India’s digital payments,** processing **₹24 lakh crore/month** (June 2025).

Enabled **financial inclusion of 491 million individuals** and **65 million merchants.**

4. e-Governance and Inclusion

- **UMANG:** 2,300+ services in 23 languages; 8.7 crore users.

- **DigiLocker:** 56 crore users; promotes paperless governance.

- **Jan Soochna Portal (Rajasthan):** Promotes proactive transparency.

**Way Forward**

1. **Strengthen Digital Infrastructure:** Accelerate **BharatNet Phase-II** to connect all Gram Panchayats

2. **Enhance Digital Literacy:** Expand **PMGDISHA** and integrate digital literacy in **school curricula** (e-Kidz, IT clubs).

3. **Affordable Access:** Subsidize data costs and promote **public Wi-Fi hotspots (PM-WANI)** in rural regions.

4. **Promote Local Language Content:** Use platforms like **BHASHINI** for vernacular digital inclusion.

5. **Encourage PPP Models:** Collaborate with private sector for last-mile connectivity and training. EgCSC-SPV.

6. **Inclusive Design:** Ensure gender-sensitive and community-based ICT training modules.

Bridging this digital divide is essential to achieve **“Digital India for All”** and realize the vision of **inclusive growth under Viksit Bharat@2047.**

[Question ID: 2021-gs2-q86-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 87 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q87-civilsdaily**

**Reforming the government delivery system through the Direct Benefit Transfer Scheme is a progressive step, but it has its limitations too. Comment. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

As per **World Bank (2023),** India’s DBT architecture is the largest social protection systems globally, covering over

**900 million people.**

**Progressive Aspects of the DBT Scheme**

**1. Cost Savings -** DBT system helped India achieve **₹3.48 lakh crore in cumulative savings** by eliminating **ghost beneficiaries, duplication, and leakages. (BlueKraft Digital Foundation, 2025)**

2. **Better targeting** - Subsidy allocations reduced from **16% (pre-DBT)** to **9%** of total government expenditure.

**3. Transparency and Reduction in Leakages -** DBT has significantly reduced **ghost beneficiaries and duplication. Eg-In PAHAL (LPG subsidy),** ₹1.2 lakh crore saved (MoPNG, 2023).

**4. Financial Inclusion - PM Jan Dhan Yojana** enabled over **50 crore accounts,** empowering poor women and rural households to receive funds directly.

**5. Efficiency and Timeliness -** Eliminated intermediaries and delays. Eg-MNREGA, PM-KISAN, and PMUY payments.

**6. Strengthening Governance Accountability -** Real-time monitoring via **Public Financial Management System (PFMS)** ensures **audit trails** and transparency.

**7. Inclusive Welfare Delivery** and targeted support during crises. Eg-During COVID-19, over ₹68,000 crore transferred to **20 crore women Jan Dhan accounts.**

**8. Promotes Digital and Cashless Economy - Eg- UPI** handles **85% of India’s digital payments,** processing **₹24 lakh crore/month** (June 2025).

**Limitations of DBT Implementation**

1. **Exclusion Errors:** Aadhaar authentication failures lead to denial of benefits. **Eg-** Jharkhand PDS (2017) saw 10-15% exclusion (NITI Aayog).

2. **Digital Divide:** Only **43% rural households** have internet access (NFHS-5, 2021).

3. **Weak Banking Infrastructure:** Shortage of **bank branches and CSPs** in rural and hilly areas.

4. **Data Privacy and Security Risks:** Eg- **Aadhaar and CoWIN data leaks.**

5. **Technocratic Bias:** Focus on automation sidelines those lacking **digital literacy** or documentation

6. **Administrative Delays:** Verification and coordination issues cause **payment rejections or delays.** Egpayment delays in MGNREGA

7. **Limited Grievance Redressal:** Weak feedback mechanisms for correction of DBT errors.

**Way Forward**

1. **Improve Authentication:** Use **offline Aadhaar,** multi-factor verification, and local validation.

2. **Institutional Reforms:** Apply **Business Process Re-engineering (2nd ARC)** for simpler workflows.

3. **Social Audits and Human Interface:** Combine digital governance with **local institutions** for last-mile trust.

4. **Strengthen Digital Infrastructure:** Accelerate **BharatNet Phase-II** to connect all Gram Panchayats

5. **Enhance Digital Literacy:** Expand **PMGDISHA** and integrate digital literacy in **school curricula** (e-Kidz, IT clubs).

India must move toward **“Technology with Inclusion”** - ensuring **no beneficiary is left behind.**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q87-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 88 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q88-civilsdaily**

**e-governance, as a critical tool of governance, has ushered in effectiveness, transparency and accountability in governments. What inadequacies hamper the**

**enhancement of these features? 10**

The **2nd ARC's 11th Report [Year: 2023] [Marks: 2008]**

### Answer

envisioned **"SMART Governance",** Simple, Moral, Accountable, Responsive, and Transparent, through e-governance.

**E-governance ushering in effectiveness**

**1. Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT)-** saved more than **₹4.31 lakh crore**

**2. Unified Payments Interface (UPI) -** Revolutionised digital payments with real-time, low-cost, interoperable transactions. **Eg: 20+ billion transactions/month**

**3. Aadhaar-Based Authentication -** Universal digital identity enables paperless, presence-less service delivery at scale.

**4. DigiLocker -** Digital storage of documents enables instant verification across services.

**5. PM Gati Shakti - Inter-Ministerial Convergence E-governance ushering in transparency**

**1.** Open, competitive procurement on Government e-Marketplace **(GeM)** eliminates discretion and middlemen.

**2. CoWIN Platform -** Real-time vaccination tracking with public dashboards ensured transparent COVID response.

**3. Online RTI Portal**

**4. Open Government Data Portal -** data.gov.in publishes datasets across ministries for citizen scrutiny and research.

**5. e-Procurement and e-Tendering -** reduces corruption opportunities.

**E-governance ushering in accountability**

**1. CPGRAMS Grievance Redressal -** enables time-bound resolution and tracking by citizens.

**2. MyGov -** Direct policy participation through online consultations, polls, and feedback mechanisms.

**3. Social Audit via MGNREGA -** Online MIS publishes worksite details, wages, attendance

**4. Aspirational Districts Dashboard -** Real-time performance ranking of 112 districts on 49 indicators **Inadequacies hampering e-governance**

1. **Digital Divide - Eg:** Rural internet penetration **59.06%** vs urban **131.45%** (TRAI Q1 2025).

2. **Cybersecurity Vulnerabilities- Eg: ₹22,845 crore** cybercrime losses in 2024 - 206% rise (I4C, MHA 2025).

3. **Data Privacy Concerns -** Weak enforcement of data protection exposes citizens to misuse risks.

4. **Poor Digital Literacy - Eg:** Only **38% women** have ever used internet (NFHS-5, MoHFW).

5. **Inadequate Infrastructure -** Internet connectivity, electricity, devices remain insufficient in remote regions.

6. **Capacity Constraints - lower level** government staff lack training in digital tools, data analytics, AI skills.

7. **Targeting and verification errors** exclude genuine beneficiaries from welfare schemes. **Eg:** PM-Kisan validation errors stranded **2.18 crore families** (Dvara Research).

8. **Departmental Silos -** Ministries operate in isolation; lack integrated digital data ecosystems for governance.

9. **Faulty validation processes. Eg:** Aadhaar-PDS biometric failures linked to Simdega starvation death, Jharkhand (NHRC).

10. **Linguistic Barriers -** English-dominant interfaces exclude regional language users from accessing services.

**Measures for Effective Implementation**

1. **Bridge Digital Divide -** Saturate rural connectivity through BharatNet expansion and affordable smartphones distribution.

2. **Mass Digital Literacy Drive -** Scale PMGDISHA and Mission Karmayogi to all citizens and government employees.

3. **Citizen Co-creation -** Engage citizens through MyGov, hackathons, and pilot testing before scaling projects.

4. **Outcome-Based Monitoring -** Real-time dashboards, third-party audits, beneficiary feedback for course correction.

5. **Adopt Global Best Practices -** Estonia's X-Road, Once-Only Principle for seamless inter-departmental data sharing.

For Viksit Bharat 2047, robust ICT governance demands inclusion, security, capacity, and citizen-centric design.

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q88-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 89 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q89-civilsdaily**

**E-governance projects have a built-in bias towards technology and back-end integration than user-centric designs. Examine. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**The World Bank** defines e-governance as the use by government agencies of information technologies that can transform relations with citizens, businesses, and other arms of government.

**E-governance Projects**

1. **Government to Citizen (G2C) - UMANG, DigiLocker**

2. **Government to Business (G2B) - MCA21, GSTN, GeM Portal**

3. **Government to Government (G2G) - PRAGATI, e-Office**

4. **Government to Employee (G2E) - SPARROW, iGOT-Karmayogi Built-in Bias Towards Technology and Back-End Integration**

1. **Infrastructure-Centric Approach:** Focus on **servers, networks, and databases** rather than citizen interface.

2. **Neglect of ease of access** for end-users. Eg- complexity in GSTN and MCA 21 portals.

3. **Exclusion Errors:** Aadhaar authentication failures lead to denial of benefits. **Eg-** Jharkhand PDS (2017) saw 10-15% exclusion (NITI Aayog).

4. **Technology Outpacing Capacity:** Rapid adoption of AI, blockchain, and analytics without adequate **digital literacy or institutional capacity** at the local level.

**Lack of User-Centric Designs**

1. **Language and Accessibility Barriers:** Most portals available only in **English,** excluding non-English users.

2. **Complex Interfaces** and Poorly designed websites discourage participation. Eg- multiple logins and verification steps.

3. **Digital Divide and Exclusion:** Only **43% of rural households** have internet (NFHS-5, 2021).

4. Most systems lack built-in **feedback loops or grievance redressal.**

5. **Absence of Design Thinking:** Systems designed from administrative, not citizen, perspective

6. **One-Size-Fits-All Approach:** Uniform design ignores regional, gender, and social diversity, affecting inclusivity.

Tech-centric Design → Low Usability → Exclusion → Inefficiency ↓ User-centric Design → Accessibility → Participation → Accountability

**However, there are few Achievements**

1. **UMANG App:** Unified access to **2,300+ government services** in **23 languages**

2. **MyGov Platform:** Enables **citizen consultation and idea crowdsourcing.**

3. **Jan Soochna Portal (Rajasthan):** Promotes **proactive disclosure of welfare data.**

4. **eSanjeevani Telemedicine Platform:** Over **18 crore consultations,** with simple, accessible interface for rural users.

5. **BHASHINI:** Supports **35+ Indian languages,** breaking language barriers and enhancing inclusivity.

6. **PMGDISHA:** Over **6.3 crore citizens trained** in digital literacy.

**Way Forward**

1. **Adopt Design Thinking:** Involve citizens in service design and usability testing.

2. **Local Language Interfaces:** Scale BHASHINI integration across all digital services.

3. **Social Audits and Human Interface:** Combine digital governance with **local institutions** for last-mile trust.

4. **Strengthen Digital Infrastructure:** Accelerate **BharatNet Phase-II** to connect all Gram Panchayats India must move toward **“Technology with Inclusion”** - ensuring **no beneficiary is left behind.**

**Civil Society Organisations**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q89-civilsdaily]
