## Question 90 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q90-civilsdaily**

**What are the methods used by the Farmers organizations to influence the policy- makers in India and how effective are these methods? [Year: 2019] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**Farmers’ organisations** are collective associations that represent the interests of cultivators, agricultural laborers, and rural producers and seek to **influence agricultural policy** and **secure fair prices.** Eg- **Bharatiya Kisan Union**

**(BKU)**

**Methods Used by Farmers’ Organisations to Influence Policymakers**

**1. Protest and Agitation -** Organize **rallies, dharnas, sit-ins, and tractor marches** etc. **Eg-** 2020-21 **Farm Laws Protest.**

**2. Lobbying and Negotiation -** Engage directly with **ministries, parliamentary committees, and NITI Aayog** to submit **memoranda, draft proposals,** and **policy recommendations.**

**3. Electoral and Political Pressure**

- Mobilize **vote banks in rural constituencies** and influence party manifestos.

- Forming **political parties** to contest elections. **Eg- Shetkari Sanghatana** in Maharashtra

**4.** Use **mass media, social media, and civil society networks** to build public opinion. **Eg-**

**#NoFarmersNoFood** campaigns on X.

**5. Coalition and Network Building -** Create alliances with **trade unions, civil society groups, and opposition parties. Eg- All India Kisan Sangharsh Coordination Committee (AIKSCC)** unites over 200 farmer unions.

**6. Legal and Institutional Interventions - Eg-** Farmer bodies filed PILs against **land acquisition** and **farm laws.**

**Effectiveness of Methods Used by Farmers’ Organisations**

**Positive Outcomes**

1. **Policy Reversals** through sustained pressure. **Eg-** Repeal of the **Three Farm Laws (2021)** after year-long protests.

2. **Enhanced Political Awareness - Eg-** Farmers’ mobilisation in **western Uttar Pradesh and Punjab.**

3. **Short-Term Economic Gains -** Secured **loan waivers, input subsidies,** and **MSP hikes.**

4. Brought **rural issues to national discourse** via social media.

5. **Greater Institutional Inclusion - Eg-** Farmer representatives included in **Committee on MSP (2022)** for reform proposals.

6. **Strengthened Democratic Participation Limitations**

1. **Fragmentation - divisions based on region, caste, and crop pattern.** Eg- dominance of North Indian Farmers in Farm Laws protest

2. Many farmer groups are **linked with political parties,** reducing credibility as neutral stakeholders.

3. Role is majorly **protective, limiting** structural reforms. Eg- opposition to GM crops

4. **Weak Policy Research and Data-Based Advocacy**

5. **Use of violent means** - Eg- red fort incident during farm protests

6. **Marginalization of small and women farmers** in representation Farmer movements represent the democratic heartbeat of rural India. They must move from **protest-based mobilization** to **knowledge-based engagement** with the state.

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q90-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 91 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2021-gs2-q91-civilsdaily**

**“Pressure groups play a vital role in influencing public policy making in India.” Explain how the business associations contribute to public policies. [Year: 2021] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**Pressure groups** are organized associations that seek to influence government policies without contesting elections.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p100_img72.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Samuel H. Finer** calls them “Invisible empires”

**Vital Role of Pressure Groups**

1. **Agents of Political Modernization and Socialization**

2. **Interest Articulation - Eg- Farmer Unions**

3. **Facilitates Social Progress - Eg-** Eg- **NAZ Foundation’s fight against Section 377.**

4. **Enables Constructive Participation ('safety-valve'** outlet)

**5. Future political leadership - Eg-** Arvind Kejriwal (earlier part of IAC campaign) **Role of Business Associations in Public Policies**

**1. Policy Advocacy and Lobbying- Eg- ASSOCHAM** lobbied for **GST simplification** and **corporate tax reduction (2019).**

**2. Research Support -** Provide **expert input, data, and feedback.** Eg- **pre-budget consultations** with **CII** and **FICCI**

**3.** Act as **implementing partners** for national missions. **Eg- CII-NSDC collaboration** under **Skill India Mission.**

**4. International Economic Diplomacy -** Facilitate **trade forums, business summits, and global investor meets.** Eg- opposition to RCEP

**5. Legal and Regulatory Influence -** Push for **ease of compliance** and regulatory clarity. **Eg- ASSOCHAM** provided feedback for **Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020.**

**6. Promoting Responsible and Inclusive Growth** through **CSR and ESG norms.**

**7.** Provide a platform **for consultations** for key issues like **industrial revolution 4.0 etc.**

**Challenges**

**1. Elite Bias -** limited representation for **MSMEs, startups, and informal sector.**

**2. Fragmentation** due to multiple bodies **(CII, FICCI, ASSOCHAM, NASSCOM)** lead to **overlapping agendas** and **diluted influence.**

**3. Regulatory Capture -** Eg- Corporate-Politicians-Bureaucracy nexus **(Vohra committee)**

4. **Poor Regulation and Secrecy** lead to **corruption and crony capitalism.**

**Way Forward**

**1. “Lobbying Regulation Act”** similar to the USA

2. Financial Transparency in Functioning For **Viksit Bharat @2047** business associations must move from **protest-based mobilization** to **knowledge-based engagement** with the state.

[Question ID: 2021-gs2-q91-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 92 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q92-civilsdaily**

**Public charitable trusts have the potential to make India’s development more inclusive as they relate to certain vital public issues. Comment. [Year: 2024] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**Public charitable trusts (PCTs)** are **non-profit legal entities** and act as **social capital institutions** that complement the government in achieving **inclusive and sustainable development.**

**Role of Public Charitable Trusts in Promoting Inclusive Development**

1. **Expanding Access to Education - Eg-** Azim Premji Foundation Trust - runs 3500+ schools and Azim Premji University to democratize education.

**2. Strengthening Healthcare Access** in underserved areas. **Eg-Tata Trusts:** Developed Cancer Care Network - **17 cancer centres** in Tier-II cities.

**3. Promoting Rural Livelihoods and Skill Development -** Eg- **Aga Khan Rural Support Programme** benefited **1.5 million rural households** through watershed and women’s cooperatives in Gujarat and MP.

**4. Addressing Social Inequality and Marginalization** - **Eg-Smile Foundation** runs Mission Education and Swabhiman programs benefiting **750,000 underprivileged children**

**5. Environmental Sustainability -** Invest in **water conservation, renewable energy, and afforestation** projects. Eg- Go Green Initiative of Wadia trust

**6. Disaster Relief and Humanitarian Response -** Eg-Akshaya Patra Foundation delivered meals during COVID-19 lockdowns.

**7. Promoting Democratic Participation and Rights Awareness - Eg- Centre for Equity Studies** promotes policy reforms for migrant and urban poor welfare.

**8. Promoting Research and Policy Innovation - Eg- PRIDE India** documents and disseminates data on women’s representation in local governance.

**Challenges in the Functioning of Public Charitable Trusts**

1. **Regulatory Complexity:** Multiple laws (Income Tax, FCRA, Trusts Act) create compliance burden.

2. **Transparency Deficit:** Eg- CBI report: <10% NGOs file audited financial statements.

3. **Urban Bias** leaves rural regions underserved.

4. **Duplication:** Overlap among similar welfare programs reduces efficiency.

5. **Funding Constraints -** Heavy dependence on **foreign or donor funding.**

6. **Anti Developmental Role - Eg- IB Report -** NGO activism leading to **loss of 2% of GDP Way Forward**

1. **Vijay Kumar Committee Recommendations: 'Light regulation'** of NGO

**2. 2nd ARC:** enact a law to set up an independent **National Accreditation Council**

**3.** Create formal **government-NGO platforms (as in Kerala’s Kudumbashree).**

This can strengthen Public charitable trusts act as **agents of equity and inclusion,** aligning private philanthropy with public good.

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q92-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 93 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q93-civilsdaily**

**Civil Society Organizations are often perceived as being anti-State actors than non-State actors. Do you agree? Justify. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

**CSOs** are **non-state, non-profit entities** that represent citizens’ interests and act as intermediaries between the state and the people. However, their role oscillates between **cooperation** (as development partners) and **confrontation** (as watchdogs).

**Civil Society Organizations as Anti-State Actors - Arguments in Favour**

1. CSOs frequently **expose corruption, inefficiency, and rights violations** by state institutions. Governments perceive such actions as **hostile scrutiny.**

2. **Opposition to Government Policies and Projects** - Eg- Narmada Bachao Andolan (NBA) opposed Sardar Sarovar dam project

3. **National Security Concerns - Eg-** 2014 Intelligence Bureau Report claimed foreign-funded NGOs caused a “2-3% GDP slowdown” by delaying energy and mining projects.

4. **Mobilisation of Protests** - CSOs lead **agitations and mass movements** that directly confront state authority. Eg- **Anti-AFSPA protests in Manipur** led by Irom Sharmila.

5. **Foreign Funding Suspicions** - Eg- **FCRA action against Greenpeace India** for alleged anti-development activities.

6. **Use of PILs and Social Media Activism** is perceived as obstruction. **Eg-** Citizens for Justice and Peace (CJP) filed petitions challenging the CAA and NRC.

**Civil Society Organizations as Non-State but Not Anti-State Actors - Arguments Against**

1. **Complementing the State in Service Delivery -** CSOs **fill governance gaps** in education, health, and rural development. **Eg-** Azim Premji Foundation Trust runs 3500+ schools

2. **Policy Advocacy - Eg-** MKSS inspired the **Right to Information Act (2005)**

3. CSOs **empower citizens** by promoting political awareness and participatory governance. **Eg- ADR** enhances electoral transparency through candidate data publication.

4. **Humanitarian and Relief Support - Eg-** Akshaya Patra Foundation distributed 200 million meals during COVID-19; Goonj provided relief materials during Kerala floods.

5. **Promoting Inclusive Development -** CSOs advance the goals of **equity, gender justice, and social inclusion.** Eg- Sakhi Resource Centre (Kerala)

6. CSOs help India align with **SDGs** and **climate commitments,** enhancing India’s global standing. **Eg-** WWF-India and TERI partner with MoEFCC for biodiversity programs.

**Way Forward**

1. **Vijay Kumar Committee Recommendations: 'Light regulation'** of NGO

**2. 2nd ARC:** enact a law to set up an independent **National Accreditation Council**

**3.** Create formal **government-NGO platforms (as in Kerala’s Kudumbashree).**

4. **Encourage Social Accountability Tools** like Jan Sunwai, Community Scorecards, and Participatory Planning.

5. **Shift from Confrontation to Collaboration** - seeing CSOs as governance partners, not adversaries NGOs are **“integral cogs in the wheel of good governance”.** A balanced partnership between genuine NGOs and the government is crucial for India's progress.

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q93-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 94 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q94-civilsdaily**

**What are environmental pressure groups? Discuss their role in raising awareness, influencing policies and advocating for environmental protection in India. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

**Environmental Pressure Groups (EPGs)** are **organized civil society or citizens’ groups** that seek to influence public opinion, government policy, and corporate behavior on **ecological and sustainability issues.**

**Role of Environmental Pressure Groups in India**

**Raising Environmental Awareness**

1. **Public Mobilization** through **mass movements, awareness drives, and media campaigns.** Eg-

  - Chipko Movement (1973) - led by Sunderlal Bahuguna to stop deforestation in Uttarakhand.

  - Save Silent Valley Movement (Kerala, 1978) - against a hydroelectric project threatening tropical rainforest.

2. **Environmental Education - -** Centre for Environment Education (CEE) works with schools and universities on sustainability curricula.

3. **Media and Digital Advocacy -** Fridays for Future India leverages digital activism for climate awareness.

**Influencing Public Policy and Legislation**

1. **Policy Advocacy and Research Inputs -** Provide scientific evidence, policy recommendations, and stakeholder consultations to shape environmental laws.

2. **Judicial Activism and PILs - Eg-** MC Mehta v. Union of India cases (Ganga Pollution, Oleum Gas Leak) strengthened India’s environmental jurisprudence.

3. **Shaping Environmental Institutions -** Their advocacy contributed to the creation of **MoEFCC (1985), National Green Tribunal (2010),** and **Environment Impact Assessment (EIA)** framework.

**Advocacy for Environmental Protection and Sustainable Development**

1. **Grassroots Environmental Movements - Eg-** Appiko Movement (Karnataka) replicated Chipko in Western Ghats.

2. **Corporate Accountability -** Pressure corporations to adopt **clean technologies and CSR-based sustainability models.** Eg- anti-mining **movement** in the **Niyamgiri** mountains in Odisha

3. **Climate Change and Renewable Energy Advocacy -** TERI (The Energy and Resources Institute) conducts research on climate resilience and sustainable energy policy.

4. **Urban Environmentalism -** Citizen for Green Bengaluru and Clean Air Collective India promote sustainable urban planning.

**Challenges Faced by Environmental Pressure Groups**

1. **Perception of Obstructionism:** Often labelled as **anti-development** or “foreign influenced.”

2. **Funding and Regulatory Constraints:** Tightened **FCRA norms** and compliance burdens.

3. **Limited Representation:** Urban, elite-driven activism may overlook local realities.

4. **Fragmentation and Coordination Gaps:** Lack of unified national environmental network.

5. **Balancing Ecology and Economy:** Difficulty in reconciling conservation with livelihood interests.

**Way Forward**

1. **Institutionalized Government-CSO Dialogue:** Establish consultative platforms under **MoEFCC and NITI Aayog.**

2. **Data-Driven Advocacy:** Leverage **NDAP** and **IndiaAI Mission** for environmental data visualization.

3. **Grassroots Capacity Building:** Empower **Panchayats and tribal groups** for local conservation.

4. **Transparency and Collaboration:** Adopt transparent funding and promote state-civil society partnerships.

5. **Environmental Education:** Integrate sustainability into national curriculum via National Education Policy (NEP) 2020.

Environmental pressure groups act as **guardians of ecological justice** and **public conscience** in a rapidly developing economy.

**Development Processes**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q94-civilsdaily]

## Question 95 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2018-gs2-q95-civilsdaily**

**“Policy contradictions among various competing sectors and stakeholders have resulted in inadequate ‘protection and prevention of degradation to environment.” Comment**

**with relevant illustrations. [Year: 2018] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

While environmental protection is constitutionally mandated **(Article 48A, 51A(g)),** it is marked by **overlapping sectoral priorities** and **fragmented institutional mandates.**

**Policy Contradictions Among Various Competing Sectors**

**1. Energy vs. Environment -** Expansion of **coal-based thermal power** leads to **deforestation, displacement, and carbon emissions. Eg-** Coal block allocations in **Hasdeo Arand (Chhattisgarh)**

**2. Agriculture - MSP policy** promotes water-intensive crops like paddy and sugarcane, leading to **water scarcity, salinity, and soil degradation. Eg-** Over-extraction of groundwater in **Punjab and Haryana.**

**3. Industry vs. Environmental Regulation - Ease of Doing Business** reforms relax environmental clearances (EIA 2020 draft allows post-facto approvals).

**4. Infrastructure vs. Ecosystem Stability - Eg-** Projects like **Bharatmala, Char Dham Highway, and river-linking projects** lead to **habitat fragmentation and increased disaster vulnerability.**

**5. Tourism -** Unregulated **eco-tourism and pilgrimage infrastructure** stress fragile ecosystems. **Eg- Joshimath Crisis.**

**6. Urban Development - Unplanned urbanisation** encroaches upon natural drainage systems. **Eg- Chennai floods (2015)** due to wetland encroachment; Bengaluru lake pollution.

**Policy Contradictions Among Various Competing Stakeholders**

**1. Central vs. State Governments -** Conflict between **industrial promotion by states** and **environmental clearance norms** by Centre.

**2. Government vs. Local Communities - Top-down project approvals** often ignore **Gram Sabha consent** under **Forest Rights Act (2006).** Eg- Niyamgiri Case

**3. Corporate Interests vs. Civil Society -** Private sector prioritizes profit, while NGOs and activists demand sustainability. **Eg-** Industrial pollution in **Sterlite Copper Plant (Thoothukudi).**

**4. Judiciary vs. Executive - Eg-** NGT banned illegal constructions in **Aravalli Hills (Gurugram, 2018)** and **Delhi Ridge**

**5. Inter-Ministerial Contradictions - Eg- MoEFCC** seeks conservation, while the Ministry **of Coal or Power** pushes extraction projects.

**Way Forward**

1. **Integrated Policy -** Establish a **National Council for Sustainable Development (NCSD)** under **NITI Aayog.**

Model it on the **UK’s Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs.**

2. **Whole-of-Government Approach -** Institutionalize **inter-ministerial task forces** for projects with ecological sensitivity.

3. Implement committee recommendations like **Madhav gadgil Committee** on Western Ghats

4. **Green Budgeting -** Integrate **ecosystem valuation** and environmental costs into **budgets.**

5. Create **National Environmental Information Grid (NEIG)** for real-time monitoring of forests, emissions, and water resources.

6. **Community Participation -** Ensure **FRA and PESA provisions** are respected in all forest and mining projects through **joint monitoring committees** including tribal representatives.

These measures can help align the developmental process with **SDG-13 (Climate Action)** and India’s **Panchamrit targets.**

[Question ID: 2018-gs2-q95-civilsdaily]

## Question 96 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q96-civilsdaily**

**‘In the context of neo-liberal paradigm of development planning, multi-level planning is expected to make operations cost effective and remove many implementation blockages.’**

**Discuss. [Year: 2019] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **Indian Constitution envisions a Welfare State under the DPSP (Articles 36-51),** mandating the State to ensure **social, economic, and political justice** through equitable development. **Neo-liberal paradigm of development planning** has redefined how welfare objectives are pursued.

**Characteristics of the Neo-Liberal Paradigm of Development Planning**

1. **Market Orientation** - Eg- 1991 economic reforms.

2. **Decentralized Governance** - Eg- 73rd & 74th Amendments.

3. **Outcome-Based Planning**

4. **Public-Private Partnerships (PPP)** - Eg- Smart Cities Mission.

5. **Ease of Doing Business**

6. **Technology-Driven Governance** - Eg- DBT.

7. **Fiscal Prudence** - Eg- rationalisation of CSS **Multi-Level Planning for Cost-Effectiveness**

**1. Integrated Planning** removes duplication and overlapping **Eg- Aspirational Districts Programme** converges 15+ central schemes for health, education, and livelihoods.

**2. Bottom-Up Planning** ensures **context-specific solutions** reflecting local needs and geographies. EgVillage Development Plans by Gram Sabhas

**3. Participatory Decision-Making** empowers **Gram Sabhas, SHGs, and local NGOs,** leading to **community ownership and reduced wastage. Eg- Social Audits under MGNREGA.**

**4. Data-Driven planning** and resource allocation. Eg- PM Gati Shakti Mission

**5. Fiscal Accountability - Eg- 15th Finance Commission** introduced performance-linked local grants for service delivery.

**Multi-Level Planning for Removing Implementation Blockages**

**1. Inter-Governmental Coordination** among Centre, States, and Local Bodies. **Eg- PM Gati Shakti National Master Plan** links 16 ministries through a single GIS platform for synchronized infrastructure rollout.

**2. Streamlined Administrative Processes** reduces **bureaucratic red tape and delays.**

**3. Real-Time Monitoring** allows early identification of bottlenecks and mid-course correction. **Eg- PRAGATI platform** facilitates top-level review and resolution of project delays.

**4. Capacity Building -** Multi-tier structure enables **technical support and training** from higher levels to local planners. Eg- **karmayogi iGot Platform**

**5. Crisis Management - Decentralized governance** strengthens resilience during natural disasters or pandemics.

**Challenges in Multi-Level Planning**

1. **Institutional Fragmentation -** Overlapping jurisdictions and poor coordination between Centre, State, and local bodies. **Eg-** Delays in **PM Awas Yojana (Urban)**

2. **Capacity Deficit at Local Levels -** lack of 3F’s

3. **Fiscal Dependence** on state and central grants. **Eg-** Only **10% of ULBs** generate sufficient own-source revenue (NITI Aayog).

4. **Incomplete or outdated** local datasets hinder **data-driven decision-making.**

5. **Political Centralization - Eg- District Planning Committees (Article 243ZD)** remain underutilized in most states.

6. **Weak Accountability - Eg-** Inadequate **social audit mechanisms Way Forward for Strengthening Multi-Level Planning**

1. **Adopt Best Practices**

**a.** Kerala’s **People’s Plan Campaign**

  - Participatory Budgeting in **porto alegre brazil**

2. **Institutional Convergence and Coordination - Eg-** Expand **PM Gati Shakti** model to social sectors like health and education.

3. **Capacity Building through Digital Governance - Eg-** Kerala’s **Information Kerala Mission** digitized local governance workflows.

4. **Decentralized Governance** based on **principle of subsidiarity.**

5. **Data Integration** through **NDAP, GIS platforms, and PRAGATI dashboards** for evidence-based decisions.

A coordinated, transparent, and participatory planning ecosystem can truly make development inclusive, sustainable and rapid.

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q96-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 97 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2019-gs2-q97-civilsdaily**

**The need for cooperation among various service [Year: 2019]**

### Answer

sectors has been an inherent component of development discourse. Partnership bridges bring the gap among the sectors.**

**It also sets in motion a culture of ‘Collaboration’ and ‘team spirit’. In the light of statements**

**above examine India’s Development process. (15)**

The **Indian Constitution envisions a Welfare State under the DPSP (Articles 36-51),** mandating the State to ensure **social, economic, and political justice** through equitable development.

**Need for Cooperation Among Service Sectors**

1. **Complex Interdependence** - Development issues are **multi-dimensional.**

2. **Integrated Development Approach**

3. **Inter-sectoral Coordination**

4. **Efficiency and Resource Optimization**

5. **Innovation and Knowledge Sharing**

6. **Inclusive Governance** and development through participation

7. **Accountability and Transparency India’s Development Process through Collaborative Partnerships**

**1. Government-Private Sector Partnership (PPP) - Eg- Smart Cities Mission, Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission**

**2. Inter-Governmental Collaboration - Eg- NITI Aayog’s Governing Council, PM Gati Shakti Master Plan**

**3. Government-Civil Society Partnership - Eg- SEWA** and **PRADAN** partner with government programmes for women’s empowerment and livelihoods.

**4. Public-Private-Community Partnerships (PPCP) - Eg- Swachh Bharat Mission** combines government funding, corporate CSR, and community action.

**5. Technology and Data Collaborations - Eg- UPI** integrate government, fintech, and private service sectors.

**Achievements**

1. **Economic - Fastest growing Economy**

2. **Infrastructure Efficiency through PPPs**

  - PPP projects worth **₹11 lakh crore** under implementation (DEA, 2024).

  - **NH construction speed** increased from **12 km/day (2014)** to **37 km/day (2023).**

3. **Financial Inclusion via Digital Collaboration**

  - **UPI transactions** exceeded **₹24 lakh crore (June 2025);** serves **491 million individuals** and **65 million merchants.**

  - **JAM trinity** enabled **₹3.48 lakh crore savings** by eliminating subsidy leakages (BlueKraft, 2024).

4. **Water and Sanitation Progress through Multi-Stakeholder Models**

  - **Jal Jeevan Mission:** 15 crore households connected to tap water (2025).

  - **Swachh Bharat Mission:** Rural open defecation reduced from 55% (2014) to near zero (2020).

5. **Decentralized Development through Cooperative Federalism**

  - **15th Finance Commission** devolved **₹4.36 lakh crore** directly to PRIs and ULBs for local service delivery.

  - **Aspirational Districts Programme:** 95% districts show improvement in health & education indicators (NITI Aayog, 2023).

6. **Self-Help Group (SHG) movement:** 9.3 crore women linked to banks under **DAY-NRLM,** with credit linkage of **₹9.5 lakh crore** (2024).

**Limitations**

1. **Institutional Fragmentation -** Overlapping jurisdictions and poor coordination between Centre, State, and local bodies. **Eg-** Delays in **PM Awas Yojana (Urban)**

2. **Capacity Deficit at Local Levels -** lack of 3F’s

3. **Incomplete or outdated** local datasets hinder **data-driven decision-making.**

4. **Weak Accountability - Eg-** Inadequate **social audit mechanisms**

5. **Digital Divide - Eg- NFHS-5 (2021)** showed only **43% of rural households** have internet access, affecting e-governance uptake.

**6. Trust Deficit between Stakeholders - Eg- CSO** perceived as **anti-development** or foreign influenced.

7. **Funding and Regulatory Constraints:** Tightened **FCRA norms** and compliance burdens for NGOs.

**Way Forward for Collaborative Partnerships**

**1. Adopt Best Practices**

  - Kerala’s **People’s Plan Campaign**

  - Participatory Budgeting in **porto alegre brazil**

**2. Institutional Convergence and Coordination - Eg-** Expand **PM Gati Shakti** model to social sectors like health and education.

**3. Capacity Building through Digital Governance - Eg-** Kerala’s **Information Kerala Mission** digitized local governance workflows.

**4. Decentralized Governance** based on **principle of subsidiarity.**

**5. Data Integration** through **NDAP, GIS platforms, and PRAGATI dashboards** for evidence-based decisions.

A **coordinated, transparent, and participatory** relations between various service sectors can truly make development **inclusive, sustainable and rapid.**

[Question ID: 2019-gs2-q97-civilsdaily]

## Question 98 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q98-civilsdaily**

**The Gati-Shakti Yojana needs meticulous coordination between the government and the private sector to achieve the goal of connectivity. Discuss. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **PM Gati Shakti National Master Plan (2021)** aims to transform India’s infrastructure landscape through **integrated, multimodal connectivity** across roads, railways, ports, airports, and logistics.

**Six pillars of Gati Shakti - a transformative approach for economic growth:**

1. **Analytical:** GIS-based, helps identify gaps and assets.

2. **Dynamic:** Updated regularly.

3. **Prioritization:** Focused and need-based planning.

4. **Comprehensive:** Covers 16 ministries under an integrated approach.

5. **Synchronization:** Digital platform enabling coordination.

6. **Optimization:** Better resource and asset utilization.

**Need for Coordination between Government and Private Sector**

1. **Improved Quality and Efficiency -** Public sector ensures regulatory and policy stability, while the private sector ensures **better project management** and minimizes **cost and time overruns.**

2. **Exchange of Expertise and Competence -** Eg- PPPs in Sagarmala and Bharatmala projects.

3. **Augmenting Fiscal Capacity -** Private investment supplements limited public capital.

4. **Fostering Entrepreneurship and Innovation -** Encourages **startups in logistics** (e.g., **Rivigo, Delhivery)** leveraging digital platforms.

5. **Synergies with National Monetisation Pipeline (NMP) -** Monetised assets create fiscal space for new projects under **Gati Shakti.**

6. **Efficient Dispute Resolution -** Joint mechanisms improve **grievance handling and PPP trust.**

**Challenges in Coordination**

1. **Institutional Overlaps** among ministries.

2. **Regulatory Uncertainty** deters long-term private investments.

3. **Land Acquisition and Environmental Clearances** remain bottlenecks.

4. **Data-Sharing Gaps and silo mentality Way Forward**

1. **Single-Window Digital Interface**

2. Standardize risk-sharing frameworks under PPP.

3. Empower the **Network Planning Group (NPG)** for inter-ministerial coordination.

“Connectivity is the new currency of competitiveness.” - NITI Aayog. **Gati Shakti Yojana** will help propel the **Indian economy to a $5 trillion level and beyond in “Amrit Kaal.”**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q98-civilsdaily]

## Question 99 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q99-civilsdaily**

**Do you agree with the view that increasing dependence on donor agencies for development reduces the importance of community participation in the development**

**process ? Justify your answer. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **Indian Constitution envisions a Welfare State under the DPSP.** Donor agencies play a vital role in financing and technical support for these welfare measures.

**Increasing Dependence Reduces Community Participation**

**1. Top-Down Project Design,** with **limited grassroots consultation.**

**2. Erosion of Local Ownership -** Beneficiary communities become **recipients, not stakeholders**

**3. Conditionalities** restrict **local policy space.** Eg- **IMF’s 1991 Structural Adjustment Programme** reduced social sector expenditure

**4. Dependency Syndrome -** Over-reliance on external funding discourages **domestic resource mobilization** and self-reliance. (A.G.Frank - “development of underdevelopment.”)

**5. Marginalization of Traditional Knowledge -** Donor-driven modern approaches often ignore **indigenous practices and local innovation.**

6. **Transparency and Accountability Gaps -** lack of clear monitoring frameworks or open reporting mechanisms limits public scrutiny and impact evaluation.

7. **Foreign Influence -** Eg-Concerns were raised about **World Vision India** allegedly promoting religious conversion using foreign funds.

**Positive Role of Donor Agencies in Development**

**1. Resource Mobilization -** World Bank funding for the **National Rural Health Mission (NRHM)**

**2. Capacity Building - -** DFID’s Bihar Rural Livelihood Project (JEEViKA) strengthened the capacity of SHGs and Panchayats.

**3.** Donor agencies often introduce **bottom-up approaches** and emphasize **stakeholder consultation.**

**4. Catalyzing Policy Reforms - Eg-** IMF’s fiscal frameworks encouraged better macroeconomic management post-1991 reforms.

**5. Promotion of Human Development - -** UNICEF and UNFPA have supported India’s **Reproductive and Child Health Programme (RCH-II)** and **Poshan Abhiyaan.**

**6. Strengthening Civil Society - -** UNDP’s Disha Project (with IKEA Foundation) enhanced employability of **1 million rural women** across 10 states.

Donor agencies are **“integral cogs in the wheel of good governance”.** A balanced partnership with government is crucial make development **inclusive, sustainable and rapid..**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q99-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 100 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q100-civilsdaily**

**“In contemporary development models, decision-making and problem-solving responsibilities are not located close to the source of information and execution, defeating**

**the objectives of development.” Critically evaluate. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

Development is most effective when **decisions are taken close to where problems occur (Principle of Subsidiarity).** However, **centralized and technocratic development models** separate **decision-making** from **execution,** leading to inefficiencies, alienation, and weak accountability.

<p align="center"><img src="images/civilsdaily_p111_img74.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

**Contemporary development models**

**1. Neoliberal -** privatization, and deregulation

**2. Technocratic Model - Data driven governance**

**3. Donor-Driven Model**

**4. PPP Model**

**5. Participatory Development Model Decision-Making and Problem-Solving Not Close to Ground**

**1. Centralized Policy Design,** with limited input from grassroots institutions. **Eg-** Uniform guidelines under **PMAY-Gramin**

**2. Bureaucratic Hierarchies** lead to delayed responses. Eg- delays in fund release under MGNREGA due to multi-tier approvals.

**3. Technocratic and Data-Centric Planning** leads to exclusion of indigenous knowledge.

**4. Weak Decentralization - PRIs** and **ULBs** lack fiscal and functional autonomy. Only **40% of states** have functional District Planning Committees (NITI Aayog, 2022).

**5. Lack of Coordination -** ministries and departments work in silos. Eg- **Smart Cities Mission:** 60% projects delayed due to poor coordination.

**6. Lack of Participatory democracy -** Communities are treated as **beneficiaries** rather than **partners** in development.

**7. Political and Fiscal Centralization** through centrally sponsored schemes

  - Regulatory Capture - Eg- Nexus of Bureaucracy-Politicians-Corporate. **(Vohra Committee) Counter-Argument**

  - **73rd & 74th Constitutional Amendments** empowered **PRIs and ULBs** for **local governance and participatory planning.**

  - **Gram Panchayat Development Plans (GPDPs)** institutionalized **bottom-up planning** in over **2.6 lakh Panchayats.**

  - **Aspirational Districts Programme (ADP) -** Promotes **data-driven local governance** with **district-level flexibility** and real-time monitoring.

  - **PM Gati Shakti National Master Plan -** Integrates **16 ministries** on a **GIS-based platform** to align planning and execution at ground level.

  - **Mission Karmayogi -** Builds **capacity of field-level officials** for local problem-solving and citizen-centric service delivery.

  - **Cooperative Federalism via NITI Aayog - Governing Council** enable **state-specific and local feedback-based planning.**

  - **Community-Led Models - Swachh Bharat Mission** and **Jal Jeevan Mission** emphasize **village-level implementation and monitoring.**

  - State Level Measures - Kerala’s **People’s Plan Campaign Way Forward**

  - **Capability Approach -** increase spending on Health (2.5%of GDP) and Education (6% of GDP)

  - **Adopt Best Practices -** Participatory Budgeting in **porto alegre brazil**

  - **Decentralized Governance** based on **principle of subsidiarity.**

“Growth becomes meaningful only when it expands human freedom and capability.” - **Amartya Sen** For this, decision-making authority and problem-solving responsibilities should be at the grassroots level.

**Social Justice**

**Government Policies and Interventions for Welfare Schemes for**

**Vulnerable Sections**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q100-civilsdaily]

## Question 101 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2022-gs2-q101-civilsdaily**

**The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 remains only a legal document without intense sensitisation of government functionaries and citizens regarding disability.**

**Comment. [Year: 2022] [Marks: 10]**

### Answer

The **RPwD Act, 2016** was enacted to align India’s legal framework with the **UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD)** to **promote equality, ensure dignity, and protect rights** of PwDs.

**Fact File**

**1. 2.21% of the total population are persons with disabilities (PwDs).**

**2. 21% of the total disabled are elderly (60+ years).**

**Key Features of the RPwD Act, 2016**

1. **Expanded definition:** Disability categories increased from **7 to 21,** including **autism, thalassemia, acid attack survivors,** etc.

2. **Reservation: 4% in government jobs** and **5% in higher education** for PwDs.

3. **Accessibility:** Mandates barrier-free public infrastructure, ICT accessibility, and universal design.

4. **Institutional Framework:** Establishment of **Central and State Advisory Boards, Chief Commissioner** and **State Commissioners** for PwDs.

5. **Legal Protection:** Punishment for discrimination, and provision of special courts to handle disability-related matters.

**Major Issues Hindering Effective Implementation**

**Bureaucratic Issues**

1. **Poor Institutional Implementation-** As per Department of Empowerment of PwDs, only **23 of 35 States/UTs** had constituted State Advisory Boards.

2. **Inaccessible Infrastructure-** Under **Accessible India Campaign (Sugamya Bharat),** only **3% of government buildings** were made fully accessible by 2024.

3. **Tokenistic Compliance-** Disability cells in ministries lack funds and trained staff.

4. Delay in appointing state commissioners and lack of special courts restrict legal recourse for PwDs.

5. **Fragmented Coordination-** Overlap between ministries (Social Justice, Urban Affairs, HRD) causes slow execution of inclusive programs.

**Citizen Awareness and Social Sensitisation Issues**

1. **Social Stigma and Prejudice-** PwDs continue to face exclusion, pity narratives, and stereotypes in media and public life.

2. **Lack of Awareness Among Citizens and Local Institutions-** Rural households and PRIs remain unaware of provisions such as disability certificates or reservation rights.

3. **Educational Exclusion-** Despite RTE inclusion, schools lack special educators and assistive devices; enrolment gaps persist.

4. **Digital Divide and Communication Barriers-** Most government websites and platforms fail web accessibility compliance standards (WCAG).

5. **Media Misrepresentation-** Stereotyping of PwDs continues despite **Supreme Court directives (2024)** against derogatory portrayals in films and media.

**Way Forward**

1. **Sensitisation -** Conduct **mandatory disability awareness training** for civil servants, teachers, and health workers.

2. **Grassroots Awareness Campaigns:** Use **community radio, local NGOs, and ASHA/Aanganwadi networks.**

3. **Institutional Strengthening:** Fully operationalize **State Advisory Boards,** ensure adequate funding and monitoring.

4. **Accessibility Revolution:** Enforce **Sugamya Bharat milestones** with real-time audits.

True inclusion demands a **“whole-of-society” approach-one** that blends policy, participation, and perception change to realise the vision of **“Sabka Saath, Sabka Vikas, Sabka Vishwas.”**

[Question ID: 2022-gs2-q101-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 102 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q102-civilsdaily**

**The crucial aspect of development process has been the inadequate attention paid to Human Resource Development in India. Suggest measures that can address [Year: 2023]**

### Answer

this adequacy.**

**10**

Human Resource Development refers to the strategic investment in education, health, and skills to empower individuals, foster productivity, and ensure sustainable national growth.

**Inadequate Attention to HRD in India**

1. **Stagnant Public Spending:** Education spending is ~2.9% of GDP (2024-25), far below the **6% target** set by NEP 2020. (Economic Survey 2025-26)

2. **Learning Poverty Paradox:** ASER 2024 reports that only **~43%** of Class V students can read a Class II-level text.

3. **Acute Skill Mismatch:** Only **56.35%** of Indian graduates were found employable by industry standards in 2026. (India Skills Report 2026)

4. **Chronic Nutrition Crisis: 35.5%** of children under five are stunted, affecting long-term human capital.

(NFHS-5)

5. **Gender Participation Gap:** Female Labour Force Participation Rate (FLFPR) stands at **32.7%** compared to over 75% for males. (PLFS 2024-25)

6. **The Persistence of Digital Divide:** Unequal access to tech-enabled learning alienates rural and economically vulnerable students.

7. **Vocational Stigma:** Less than **5%** of the workforce has formal vocational training. (NSDC 2024)

8. **Healthcare Infrastructure Gaps:** Public health spending remains around **2.1%** of GDP. (NHP recommended 2.5%)

9. **Mental Health Neglect:** Rising student anxiety and workplace burnout are modern HRD barriers.

10. **Brain Drain:** Failure to provide high-end research infrastructure leads to the flight of top-tier talent.

**Measures to Address HRD Inadequacy**

**1. Capability Approach -** increase spending on Health (2.5%of GDP) and Education (6% of GDP)

2. **Foundational Literacy Focus:** Prioritize the **NIPUN Bharat Mission** to ensure every child achieves grade-level competency

3. **Vocational-Academic Integration:** Mandate vocational training in secondary schools to bridge the gap between schooling and work. **(NEP, 2020)**

4. **Strengthening Primary Health:** Expand the **Ayushman Bharat Health and Wellness Centres** to ensure preventative care is a "right."

5. **Bridging the Digital Gap:** Use **BharatNet Phase-III** to provide high-speed fiber connectivity to every rural school.

6. **Empowering Women Workers:** Provide safe transport, childcare, and flexible work to boost female participation. **Eg:** Karnataka’s **Shakti Yojan** and free higher education for girls.

7. **Institutionalizing Mental Wellness:** Make Socio-Emotional Learning (SEL) a core part of the school and office curriculum.

8. **R&D and Innovation Hubs:** Establish the **National Research Foundation (NRF)** to fund deep-tech innovation and retain high-end talent.

By integrating health, education, and skills into a holistic HRD framework, India can achieve inclusive growth and the vision of a developed nation by 2047.

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q102-civilsdaily]

---

## Question 103 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS II)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q103-civilsdaily**

**The National Commission for Protection of Child Rights has to address the challenges faced by children in the digital era. Examine the existing policies and suggest measures the**

**Commission can initiate to tackle the issue. [Year: 2025] [Marks: 15]**

### Answer

The **NCPCR,** established under the **Commission for Protection of Child Rights Act, 2005,** is the apex body for safeguarding child rights under **Articles 14, 15(3), 21, and 39(e)-(f)** of the Constitution.

**Challenges Faced by Children in the Digital Era**

1. **Online Sexual Exploitation:** Increased exposure to **pornography, grooming, and trafficking.**

2. **Cyberbullying and Harassment:** UNICEF (2019) - 1 in 3 Indian children faced online bullying.

3. **Privacy and Data Protection Issues**

4. **Mental Health: Addiction, Self harm, Anxiety etc.**

5. **Digital Divide:** Unequal access to internet-based education deepens **learning inequality**

6. **Exposure to Harmful Content - Eg-** Blue Whale Challenge.

7. **Deepfakes & AI Manipulation - Eg-** Interpol flagged AI-generated child pornography.

**Functions and Powers of NCPCR**

1. **Examine and Review Safeguards**

2. **Inquire into Violations**

3. **Advise on Policy and Legislation**

4. **Monitor Implementation** of **child-related laws,** such as the **Juvenile Justice Act, POCSO Act,** and **RTE Act.**

5. **Research and Awareness**

6. Inspect **juvenile homes, observation homes, and child care institutions.**

7. **Quasi-Judicial Powers:** powers of a **civil court Existing Policies and Initiatives**

1. **IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021** - Mandate removal of child sexual abuse content and ensure age-based content filtering.

2. **POCSO Act, 2012 (Amended 2019)** - Covers online sexual abuse and child pornography.

3. **National Cybercrime Reporting Portal** - Allows reporting of child cyber exploitation.

4. **Digital India & PM eVIDYA** - Promote safe and inclusive digital learning.

## **5. NCPCR Initiatives**

  - **POCSO e-Box** - Online reporting of sexual abuse.

  - **Sammaan Portal** - Tracks child abuse cases online.

  - Guidelines on Online Child Safety (2020) during COVID.

  - **Guidelines for Ed-Tech Platforms** - Protect children’s digital privacy and safety.

  - **POCSO e-Courts Project (2018) - to fast-track POCSO trials using digital case management systems Issues in the Existing Policies and Legal Framework**

1. **Fragmented Mechanism:** Overlap among NCPCR, IT Ministry, and police leads to poor coordination.

2. **Outdated Laws:** IT Act 2000 and POCSO Act 2012 don’t address AI, dark web, or social media threats.

3. **Weak Child Data Protection:** Data Protection Act 2023 lacks strong safeguards for children’s data and consent.

4. **Poor Enforcement of IT Rules:** Platforms often ignore self-regulatory duties to remove harmful content.

5. **Limited Cyber Policing:** Lack of trained cyber experts and forensic tools in law enforcement.

6. **Low Awareness:** Parents, teachers, and officials unaware of digital risks and reporting mechanisms.

7. **Slow Justice Delivery:** POCSO trials and cybercrime cases face long delays and low conviction rates.

8. **Unregulated Ed-Tech and Gaming:** No clear norms for content, privacy, or addictive design in children’s apps.

**Measures NCPCR Can Initiate**

1. **Strengthen Legal and Regulatory Framework:** Recommend amendments to ensure **strict age verification, child data protection,** and content moderation.

2. **Establish Digital Child Protection Cell** to track online abuse and emerging threats.

3. **Cyber Safety Education** in schools and communities.

4. Conduct **periodic studies** on children’s digital behaviour and risks for evidence-based policymaking.

5. **Build Capacity of Law Enforcement** on **cyber forensics and child protection laws.**

6. Advocate **screen-time guidelines, parental supervision tools,** and awareness on balanced digital habits.

**“The child is both a promise and a responsibility.”** Thus, the **3R approach of Reform, Reorientation and**

**Restructuring** can enhance functioning of NCPCR as an effective **Guardian of Child Rights.**

**Comparison of Constitutions**

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q103-civilsdaily]
